# CM-EIP Extensive Question Bank

This question bank follows the sequence and style of the review questions in Appendix A of the IBF CM-EIP study guide, but expands them substantially. It is organised by chapter and section, covers all twelve chapters and all four appendices, and adds calculations, suitability decisions and scenario questions. Answers and rationales are hidden inside expandable sections; click **Show answer** after attempting each question.

For multiple-response questions, select every correct option. Unless stated otherwise, use a 360-day convention for all products other than Singapore Government Securities, which use a 365-day convention, and the formulae in Appendix B of the study guide. These are original revision questions, not a reproduction of the source's review-question set.

The bank is generated from `scripts/lib/questions-cmeip.mjs` by `scripts/append-questions.mjs`. Edit the source module, not this file: everything below the marker is rewritten on each run.

<!-- EXTENSION QUESTIONS BELOW — generated by scripts/append-questions.mjs -->

## Chapter 1 — Introduction to Investing, Investments and Financial Markets

### 1. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.1.1] Under section 4A of the Securities and Futures Act 2001, which investor category is defined by occupation rather than by wealth?

- a. Expert investor
- b. Institutional investor
- c. Retail investor
- d. Accredited investor

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Expert investor

**Rationale:** An expert investor is a person whose business involves acquiring, disposing of or holding capital markets products. The classification rests on occupational understanding of product risk, not on wealth or income.

</details>

### 2. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §1.1.1] An individual has net personal assets of S$2.4 million, including a primary residence worth S$1.8 million with an outstanding mortgage of S$600,000. Does the individual meet the net personal assets test for accredited investor status?

- a. Yes, because net personal assets exceed S$2 million
- b. No, because the residence is capped at S$1 million
- c. Yes, because the residence alone exceeds S$1 million
- d. No, because financial assets are not stated

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. No, because the residence is capped at S$1 million

**Rationale:** The residence counts net of the secured loan and is capped at S$1 million: S$1.8m − S$0.6m = S$1.2m, capped at S$1.0m. Other net personal assets are S$2.4m − S$1.8m = S$0.6m. Total counted = S$1.0m + S$0.6m = S$1.6m, below the S$2 million threshold.

</details>

### 3. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.1.1] What is the effect of the accredited investor opt-in regime introduced by MAS?

- a. An accredited investor must re-qualify every quarter
- b. A capital markets intermediary may treat any qualifying investor as an accredited investor automatically
- c. A capital markets intermediary may not treat a qualifying investor as an accredited investor unless the investor opts in
- d. An accredited investor is barred from retail safeguards

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. A capital markets intermediary may not treat a qualifying investor as an accredited investor unless the investor opts in

**Rationale:** Under the opt-in regime a CMI can no longer automatically treat anyone who qualifies as an accredited investor; the investor must opt to be treated as one, and may instead elect the full range of retail safeguards.

</details>

### 4. **[Multiple response]** [CM-EIP §1.1.1] Which of the following are typical return objectives quoted for client profiling? (Select all that apply)

- a. Preserve wealth and purchasing power at 2–4%
- b. Income with some growth at 4–6%
- c. Steady balanced growth at 6–8%
- d. Guaranteed capital growth above 15%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A, B, C

**Rationale:** The guide quotes four bands: 2–4% for preserving wealth and purchasing power, 4–6% for income with some growth, 6–8% for steady balanced growth and 8–12% for aggressive growth. No guaranteed return above 15% is offered.

</details>

### 5. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.1.2] Which product characteristic is associated with convertible bonds but not with plain bonds in the product-benefit matrix?

- a. The ability to hedge an inverse payout
- b. A defined interest yield
- c. A regular payout stream
- d. The ability to amplify gains and losses through leverage

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. The ability to amplify gains and losses through leverage

**Rationale:** Convertible bonds are marked as amplifying gains and losses through leverage, because the embedded equity option gives participation in the share price. Plain bonds are marked for defined yield, capital gains, regular payout and inverse-floaters as a hedge.

</details>

### 6. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.1.3] Why must a representative be alert to a client's business assets when advising on financial assets?

- a. Business financing needs and decisions about physical assets can materially affect the client's financial assets
- b. Business assets are exempt from disclosure requirements
- c. Business assets are treated as financial assets under the SFA
- d. Business assets are always larger than financial assets

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Business financing needs and decisions about physical assets can materially affect the client's financial assets

**Rationale:** For individuals the flow of funds between business, physical and financial assets is closely related. Business financing needs, and decisions about business and physical assets, can have a major impact on financial assets.

</details>

### 7. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.2.1] What is the principal opportunity cost of holding investible funds in cash over a long period?

- a. Loss of deposit insurance cover
- b. Loss of purchasing power as inflation erodes real value
- c. Exposure to currency risk
- d. Ineligibility for the CPF Investment Scheme

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Loss of purchasing power as inflation erodes real value

**Rationale:** Cash pays the lowest return. In a low interest rate environment, holding investible funds in cash carries a high opportunity cost because inflation erodes purchasing power.

</details>

### 8. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.2.2] Which money market instrument is quoted on an interest-bearing basis rather than a discount basis?

- a. Commercial paper
- b. Banker's acceptance
- c. Certificate of deposit
- d. Treasury bill

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Certificate of deposit

**Rationale:** Certificates of deposit carry a maturity date and a specific interest rate, generally on a compounding basis, with interest payable at maturity. Treasury bills, commercial paper and banker's acceptances are all issued at a discount to face value.

</details>

### 9. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §1.2.2] A treasury bill with a face value of S$100,000 is bought for S$98,500 and held to maturity in 90 days. What is the simple discount, expressed as a percentage of face value?

- a. 1.52%
- b. 1.48%
- c. 6.00%
- d. 1.50%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. 1.50%

**Rationale:** The discount is S$100,000 − S$98,500 = S$1,500, which is 1.50% of the S$100,000 face value. The annualised bank discount yield would be 1.50% × 360/90 = 6.00%, but the question asks for the discount as a percentage of face value.

</details>

### 10. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.2.2] A bond denominated in US dollars, sold in the United States, and issued by a Japanese corporation is a:

- a. Yankee bond
- b. Domestic bond
- c. Euroyen bond
- d. Eurodollar bond

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Yankee bond

**Rationale:** A Yankee bond is sold in the United States, denominated in US dollars, but issued by a foreign corporation or government. A Eurodollar bond is denominated in USD and sold outside the United States.

</details>

### 11. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.2.2] A Japanese company issues yen-denominated bonds for sale to investors in London. These are:

- a. Domestic bonds
- b. Euroyen bonds
- c. Samurai bonds
- d. Yankee bonds

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Euroyen bonds

**Rationale:** A Euroyen bond is denominated in yen and sold outside Japan. The guide gives exactly this example: a Japanese company issuing Euroyen bonds for sale in London.

</details>

### 12. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.2.3] In the order of priority of claim on a company's assets, which ranking is correct?

- a. Ordinary shareholders, preference shareholders, creditors
- b. Creditors, ordinary shareholders, preference shareholders
- c. Creditors, preference shareholders, ordinary shareholders
- d. Preference shareholders, creditors, ordinary shareholders

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Creditors, preference shareholders, ordinary shareholders

**Rationale:** Creditors have the highest priority, followed by preference shareholders, then ordinary shareholders, who hold the residual claim.

</details>

### 13. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.2.4] Which derivative is described as exchange-traded with standardised terms and may be cash settled or physically settled?

- a. Convertible security
- b. Company-issued warrant
- c. Structured warrant
- d. Futures contract

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Futures contract

**Rationale:** A futures contract is an agreement for future delivery of an asset at a predetermined price, traded on exchanges with standardised terms, and may be cash settled or physically settled.

</details>

### 14. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.2.4] Why does a convertible security usually pay a lower yield than the same issuer's plain corporate bond?

- a. Because the yield difference is the value of the embedded share option
- b. Because it has a shorter maturity
- c. Because it is unsecured
- d. Because it ranks below the plain bond in liquidation

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because the yield difference is the value of the embedded share option

**Rationale:** The convertible gives the holder the right to convert into ordinary shares. The price difference between the convertible's lower yield and the plain bond's yield is the value of the embedded share option.

</details>

### 15. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.2.5] Which client is professional portfolio management most clearly suited to?

- a. A client with strong views who trades daily
- b. A client who lacks the time, information, expertise, discipline or emotional temperament to invest actively
- c. A client seeking guaranteed returns
- d. A client who wishes to avoid all fees

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. A client who lacks the time, information, expertise, discipline or emotional temperament to invest actively

**Rationale:** Professional portfolio management benefits clients who do not have the time, appropriate information, expertise, discipline or emotional temperament to invest actively.

</details>

### 16. **[Multiple response]** [CM-EIP §1.3] Which of the following are participants in financial markets? (Select all that apply)

- a. Issuers
- b. Intermediaries such as broker-dealers and market makers
- c. Infrastructure providers such as clearing houses and central depositories
- d. Regulators

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A, B, C, D

**Rationale:** The guide lists investors, issuers, intermediaries, regulators, electronic exchanges, infrastructure providers and financial institutions as participants.

</details>

### 17. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.3] What distinguishes high frequency trading from algorithmic trading more generally?

- a. High frequency trading is a form of fundamental analysis
- b. High frequency trading does not use computer programs
- c. High frequency trading relies on computer speed to analyse large amounts of market information and self-execute trades in fractions of a second
- d. High frequency trading is only used in the bond market

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. High frequency trading relies on computer speed to analyse large amounts of market information and self-execute trades in fractions of a second

**Rationale:** Algorithmic trading uses a computer program following defined instructions to execute a trade and can slice large trades to minimise market impact. High frequency trading is a form of algorithmic trading that relies on computer speed and power to analyse significant amounts of market information and self-execute trades in fractions of a second.

</details>

### 18. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.3.1] In a primary market transaction, who receives the proceeds?

- a. The exchange
- b. The clearing house
- c. The selling investor
- d. The issuer

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. The issuer

**Rationale:** In the primary market the issuer receives the cash from subscribers, who receive financial claims that previously did not exist.

</details>

### 19. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.3.2] What is the essential economic function of the secondary market?

- a. It provides liquidity, which is essential to the proper functioning of capital markets
- b. It sets the coupon rate on new bonds
- c. It guarantees investors against loss
- d. It raises new capital for issuers

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. It provides liquidity, which is essential to the proper functioning of capital markets

**Rationale:** Proceeds from a secondary market sale go to the selling investor, not the issuer. The secondary market provides liquidity, which is essential to the proper functioning of capital markets.

</details>

### 20. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.3.3] Which characteristic of a good financial market is described as internal efficiency?

- a. Availability of information
- b. Low transaction costs across reaching the market, brokerage and transfer
- c. Narrow bid-offer spreads
- d. Price discovery

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Low transaction costs across reaching the market, brokerage and transfer

**Rationale:** An efficient market is characterised by low transaction costs applying to all aspects of the transaction, including the cost of reaching the market, the actual brokerage cost and the cost of transferring the security. This attribute is referred to as internal efficiency.

</details>

### 21. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.3.3] A security can be sold quickly but its price jumps substantially between transactions. Which characteristic of a good market is missing?

- a. Availability of information
- b. Marketability
- c. Price continuity
- d. Information efficiency

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Price continuity

**Rationale:** Marketability is the likelihood of a quick sale and is necessary but not sufficient for liquidity. Price continuity means prices do not change much from one transaction to the next unless new information arrives, and it is the missing component here.

</details>

### 22. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.3.4] Which body's rules govern the dealing and settlement procedures for Eurobonds?

- a. The Securities and Exchange Commission
- b. The Monetary Authority of Singapore
- c. The Options Clearing Corporation
- d. The Association of International Bond Dealers

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. The Association of International Bond Dealers

**Rationale:** Because participation in the Eurobond market is worldwide, dealing and settlement procedures are governed by the rules of the Association of International Bond Dealers (AIBD).

</details>

### 23. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.3.5] Which market settles on a T+0 basis?

- a. China
- b. United States
- c. Singapore
- d. Hong Kong

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. China

**Rationale:** In China the settlement period is T+0, on the philosophy that an investor must have the funds on hand to make the purchase. The US, Singapore and Hong Kong settle at T+2.

</details>

### 24. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.3.5] How does a dealer differ from a broker in the OTC market?

- a. A dealer acts as agent for a commission; a broker has a vested interest in the transaction
- b. A dealer has a vested interest in transactions and earns the spread between bid and offer; a broker acts as agent
- c. A dealer only trades listed securities
- d. A dealer cannot hold inventory

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. A dealer has a vested interest in transactions and earns the spread between bid and offer; a broker acts as agent

**Rationale:** Unlike brokers, dealers have a vested interest in transactions and earn the spread between the bid and offer prices.

</details>

### 25. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.3.6] What role does the Options Clearing Corporation perform in the US options market?

- a. It regulates the exchanges
- b. It sets the strike prices of all listed options
- c. It becomes the buyer for every seller and the seller for every buyer, guaranteeing contract obligations
- d. It acts as the market maker on the exchange floor

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. It becomes the buyer for every seller and the seller for every buyer, guaranteeing contract obligations

**Rationale:** The OCC acts as intermediary between the brokers representing buyers and sellers, becoming the buyer for every seller and the seller for every buyer, guaranteeing that all contract obligations will be met.

</details>

### 26. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.4] Which digital development is described as reducing the daily float of cash in the banking system?

- a. Non-bank digital wallets
- b. Digital currencies
- c. Internet banking
- d. Internet business transactions clearing directly

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Internet business transactions clearing directly

**Rationale:** Cash management is a traditional area of banking where transactions cleared through the banking system may take a few days, providing a daily float. That float has been reduced because of the greater efficiency of direct internet transactions.

</details>

### 27. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.4] Which statement about cryptocurrencies is correct according to the guide?

- a. They are not recognised as legal tender and make up about 1% of currency transactions
- b. They cannot be traded through futures contracts
- c. They are recognised as legal tender in Singapore
- d. They are backed by central bank reserves

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. They are not recognised as legal tender and make up about 1% of currency transactions

**Rationale:** Cryptocurrencies are not backed by reserves or central banks, are not recognised as legal tender, and make up only about 1% of currency transactions. Bitcoin futures trade on the CME and CBOE.

</details>

### 28. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §1.5] A 90-day commercial paper is best classified as which type of instrument?

- a. A capital market instrument
- b. A money market instrument
- c. A derivative
- d. A real asset

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. A money market instrument

**Rationale:** Money market instruments are highly liquid, short-term debt instruments with maturities of less than one year. Commercial paper maturing in 90 days is a money market instrument.

</details>

## Chapter 2 — Risk, Return and Time Value Calculations

### 29. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.1] Which statement best expresses the risk-return principle?

- a. Risk and return are unrelated in efficient markets
- b. Lower risk always produces higher realised returns
- c. Rational investors demand higher potential returns when they take on more risk
- d. Investors always prefer the highest available return regardless of risk

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Rational investors demand higher potential returns when they take on more risk

**Rationale:** The risk-return (or risk-reward) principle tells us that rational investors demand higher potential returns when they take on more risk, and do not demand as high a rate of return when risk is lower.

</details>

### 30. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.1] A CCC-rated bond and the stock of a AAA-rated blue-chip company with a conservative business and financial profile are compared. Which is generally considered safer?

- a. They are equally risky
- b. It cannot be determined from the information given
- c. The CCC-rated bond, because bonds rank above equities
- d. The blue-chip stock, because the CCC bond carries much higher credit risk

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. The blue-chip stock, because the CCC bond carries much higher credit risk

**Rationale:** The risk of a CCC-rated (junk or speculative grade) bond is much higher than that of a blue-chip corporate debt rated AAA. The stock of the blue-chip company will probably be considered safer than the CCC-rated bond if it has a conservative business and financial profile, even though equities are generally the riskier asset class.

</details>

### 31. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.1] What does the VIX measure?

- a. The expected 30-day volatility of the S&P 500
- b. The realised volatility of the STI over the past year
- c. The average credit spread on US corporate bonds
- d. The yield gap between equities and bonds

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. The expected 30-day volatility of the S&P 500

**Rationale:** The VIX shows the market's expectation of 30-day S&P 500 volatility and is referred to as the investor fear gauge. A higher VIX means more risk and volatility are expected.

</details>

### 32. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.1] At what level did the VIX peak during the Global Financial Crisis in October 2008?

- a. 30
- b. 89.5
- c. 20
- d. 48

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 89.5

**Rationale:** At the height of the Global Financial Crisis in October 2008 the VIX shot up to a high of 89.5. During the European Debt Crisis it reached 48.

</details>

### 33. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.2.1] An investor buys 1,000 shares at $5.00 each, receives a dividend of $0.20 per share, and sells one year later at $6.00. What is the total return?

- a. 20.0%
- b. 24.0%
- c. 26.0%
- d. 30.0%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 24.0%

**Rationale:** Total return = [CF + (PE − PB)] / PB = [0.20 + (6.00 − 5.00)] / 5.00 = 1.20 / 5.00 = 0.24 = 24%.

</details>

### 34. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.2.2] An investment returns 25% with probability 0.20, −30% with probability 0.20 and 15% with probability 0.60. What is the expected rate of return?

- a. 3.33%
- b. 8.00%
- c. 10.00%
- d. 15.00%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 8.00%

**Rationale:** E(R) = 0.20(0.25) + 0.20(−0.30) + 0.60(0.15) = 0.05 − 0.06 + 0.09 = 0.08 = 8%.

</details>

### 35. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.2.3] A product is expected to generate a maximum return of 2.5% over three months. What is the annualised rate of return?

- a. 10.00%
- b. 10.38%
- c. 10.40%
- d. 12.50%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 10.38%

**Rationale:** Annualised return = (1.025)^(12/3) − 1 = (1.025)^4 − 1 = 1.10381 − 1 = 0.1038 = 10.38%.

</details>

### 36. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.2.3] A product quotes an annualised target return of 12%. What return is achievable over four months?

- a. 3.00%
- b. 4.12%
- c. 3.85%
- d. 4.00%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 3.85%

**Rationale:** (1 + r4)^(12/4) − 1 = 0.12, so (1 + r4) = (1.12)^(4/12) and r4 = (1.12)^(1/3) − 1 = 0.0385 = 3.85%.

</details>

### 37. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.2.4] An investment returns 10.4% in a year when inflation is 2%. What is the real rate of return?

- a. 8.40%
- b. 8.00%
- c. 12.61%
- d. 8.24%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. 8.24%

**Rationale:** Real return = [(1 + 0.104) / (1 + 0.02)] − 1 = 1.104 / 1.02 − 1 = 0.08235 = 8.24%.

</details>

### 38. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.2.4] An investment returns 10.4% in a year when there is deflation of 1%. What is the real rate of return?

- a. 11.52%
- b. 11.40%
- c. 10.40%
- d. 9.31%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. 11.52%

**Rationale:** With deflation the denominator subtracts the rate: [(1 + 0.104) / (1 − 0.01)] − 1 = 1.104 / 0.99 − 1 = 0.1152 = 11.52%.

</details>

### 39. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.2.5] A JPY portfolio returns 121.88% while SGD/JPY rises 18.89%. What is the currency-adjusted return in SGD?

- a. 140.77%
- b. 86.62%
- c. 102.99%
- d. 121.88%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 86.62%

**Rationale:** The reference currency (SGD) is the first listed currency of SGD/JPY, so divide: (1 + 1.2188) / (1 + 0.1889) − 1 = 2.2188 / 1.1889 − 1 = 0.8662 = 86.62%.

</details>

### 40. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.2.5] A USD portfolio returns 87.06% while USD/SGD rises 8.12%. What is the currency-adjusted return in SGD?

- a. 78.94%
- b. 95.18%
- c. 102.24%
- d. 87.06%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 102.24%

**Rationale:** The reference currency (SGD) is the second listed currency of USD/SGD, so multiply: (1 + 0.8706) × (1 + 0.0812) − 1 = 1.8706 × 1.0812 − 1 = 1.0224 = 102.24%.

</details>

### 41. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.2.5] When restating a foreign portfolio return into a reference currency, what is the rule of thumb?

- a. Multiply when the reference currency is the first listed currency of the pair, divide when it is the second
- b. Always divide by (1 + currency return)
- c. Always multiply by (1 + currency return)
- d. Divide when the reference currency is the first listed currency of the pair, multiply when it is the second

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Divide when the reference currency is the first listed currency of the pair, multiply when it is the second

**Rationale:** Divide (1 + portfolio return) by (1 + currency return) when the reference currency is the first listed currency of the currency pair, and multiply when the reference currency is the second listed currency.

</details>

### 42. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.2.6] Which return measure is the money-weighted return in a portfolio management context?

- a. The internal rate of return
- b. The real rate of return
- c. The currency-adjusted return
- d. The compounded annualised rate of return

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. The internal rate of return

**Rationale:** The IRR is the money-weighted return: it is the actual return generated for the client given the movement of their cash flows, and it is sensitive to the size and timing of those flows. The compounded annualised rate is the time-weighted return.

</details>

### 43. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.2.6] A fund returns 2% in year 1, 10% in year 2 and −4% in year 3. What is the annualised compounded (time-weighted) return?

- a. 2.57%
- b. 2.51%
- c. 2.67%
- d. 7.71%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 2.51%

**Rationale:** Cumulative return = (1.02 × 1.10 × 0.96) − 1 = 1.07712 − 1 = 7.712%. Annualised = (1.07712)^(1/3) − 1 = 0.0251 = 2.51%.

</details>

### 44. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.3.1] Using returns of 25% (p = 0.20), −30% (p = 0.20) and 15% (p = 0.60), with an expected return of 8%, what is the standard deviation?

- a. 37.60%
- b. 3.76%
- c. 19.39%
- d. 13.30%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 19.39%

**Rationale:** Variance = 0.20(0.25 − 0.08)² + 0.20(−0.30 − 0.08)² + 0.60(0.15 − 0.08)² = 0.00578 + 0.02888 + 0.00294 = 0.03760. Standard deviation = √0.03760 = 0.1939 = 19.39%.

</details>

### 45. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.3.1] What is the difference between historical volatility and implied volatility?

- a. Historical volatility is forward-looking; implied volatility is backward-looking
- b. They are the same measure
- c. Implied volatility is calculated from bond prices
- d. Historical volatility is calculated from past prices; implied volatility is derived from the price of a share option

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Historical volatility is calculated from past prices; implied volatility is derived from the price of a share option

**Rationale:** Volatility is the standard deviation of the security. Historical volatility is calculated from past price changes; implied volatility is the volatility implied by the price of a share option.

</details>

### 46. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.3.2] Investment A has an expected return of 5% and a standard deviation of 8%. Investment B has an expected return of 15% and a standard deviation of 20%. Which is more attractive on a risk-per-unit-of-return basis?

- a. Investment B, with a coefficient of variation of 1.33
- b. They are equally attractive
- c. Investment A, because its standard deviation is lower
- d. Investment A, with a coefficient of variation of 1.60

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Investment B, with a coefficient of variation of 1.33

**Rationale:** CV_A = 0.08 / 0.05 = 1.60; CV_B = 0.20 / 0.15 = 1.33. On a risk per unit of return basis, Investment B is more attractive because it has the lower coefficient of variation.

</details>

### 47. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.3.3] A portfolio has an expected return of 6% and a standard deviation of 12%. What is the Z-score for a return of 0%?

- a. −2.0
- b. −0.5
- c. +0.5
- d. −1.0

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. −0.5

**Rationale:** Z = (X − E(r)) / σ = (0% − 6%) / 12% = −0.5. From the standard normal table this implies a 31% chance of a negative return.

</details>

### 48. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.3.3] For a normal distribution, approximately what proportion of outcomes falls within plus or minus two standard deviations of the mean?

- a. 68%
- b. 90%
- c. 95.4%
- d. 99.7%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 95.4%

**Rationale:** A Z-score of 2.00 corresponds to 0.9772, or about 97.7% on one side and 2.3% on the other, so about 95.4% of returns fall within plus or minus two standard deviations. Plus or minus one standard deviation covers 68%.

</details>

### 49. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.3.4] A balanced portfolio has an expected return of 6% and a standard deviation of 12%. Using a Z-score of −1.96, what is the value at risk at the 2.5% tail?

- a. −12%
- b. −6%
- c. −18%
- d. −25%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. −18%

**Rationale:** X = Zσ + E(r) = −1.96(0.12) + 0.06 = −0.2352 + 0.06 = −0.1752, approximately −18%. There is a 2.5% chance of the portfolio losing at least 18% in a given year.

</details>

### 50. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.3.4] A defensive portfolio has an expected return of 4% and a standard deviation of 4%. Using a Z-score of −2.58, what is the value at risk at the 0.5% tail?

- a. −10%
- b. −25%
- c. −4%
- d. −7%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. −7%

**Rationale:** X = Zσ + E(r) = −2.58(0.04) + 0.04 = −0.1032 + 0.04 = −0.0632, approximately −7%. There is a 0.5% chance of losing at least 7% in a given year.

</details>

### 51. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.3.4] What is the principal limitation of using historical data to estimate the probability of future losses?

- a. Factors and conditions influencing returns over the next few years could be quite different from those influencing past returns
- b. Historical data cannot be used with the normal distribution
- c. Value at risk cannot be computed from historical data
- d. Historical data is always unavailable

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Factors and conditions influencing returns over the next few years could be quite different from those influencing past returns

**Rationale:** The main problem with using historical data as the basis for predicting the probability of returns is that factors and conditions influencing returns for the next few years could be quite different from those influencing past returns. The financial community therefore also uses Monte Carlo simulation.

</details>

### 52. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.4.1] Which category of risk includes business risk, credit risk and fraud risk?

- a. Product-specific risk
- b. Issuer and counterparty risk
- c. Market risk
- d. Management risk

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Issuer and counterparty risk

**Rationale:** Issuer and counterparty risks relate mostly to business risk and include business risk, credit risk and fraud risk.

</details>

### 53. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.4.1] In the Lehman Brothers case, what was the approximate ratio of assets to the firm's own capital?

- a. USD680 million of assets supported by USD22.5 million of capital
- b. USD68 billion of assets supported by USD2.25 billion of capital
- c. USD680 billion of assets supported by USD22.5 billion of capital
- d. USD22.5 billion of assets supported by USD680 billion of capital

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. USD680 billion of assets supported by USD22.5 billion of capital

**Rationale:** At one point Lehman had close to USD680 billion of assets supported only by USD22.5 billion of the firm's capital.

</details>

### 54. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.4.1] Which risk cannot be fully diversified away?

- a. Concentration risk
- b. Business risk
- c. Fraud risk
- d. Market risk

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Market risk

**Rationale:** Market risk refers to risk that affects most assets in the market. Hence such risk cannot be fully diversified. All asset classes are affected by market risks in one way or another.

</details>

### 55. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.4.1] During the Global Financial Crisis of 2008–2009, which asset classes did not fall in tandem with the rest?

- a. Cash and government bonds
- b. Real estate and commodities
- c. High-yield bonds and hedge funds
- d. Equities and corporate bonds

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Cash and government bonds

**Rationale:** During the Global Financial Crisis almost all classes of financial assets except cash and government bonds fell in tandem. The correlation of asset classes tends toward 1 during periods of crisis.

</details>

### 56. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.4.1] On SGX, at what price movement is a circuit breaker triggered?

- a. A move of more than 5% from the reference price
- b. A move of more than 10% from the traded price 5 minutes prior
- c. A move of more than 20% from the previous close
- d. A move of more than 3% from the opening price

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. A move of more than 10% from the traded price 5 minutes prior

**Rationale:** On SGX, circuit breakers are triggered if the price for a stock would move by more than 10% of the traded price 5 minutes prior (the reference price). Once triggered, a 5-minute cooling-off period follows during which trading can only take place plus or minus 10% from the reference price.

</details>

### 57. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.4.1] In the 2015 Mizuho Securities incident, what was the cause of the loss of at least 27 billion yen?

- a. A fraudulent trade
- b. A system failure
- c. A typing error in the trade size and price
- d. A flash crash

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. A typing error in the trade size and price

**Rationale:** A typing error caused Mizuho Securities to lose at least 27 billion yen (about $225 million) when a trader tried to sell 610,000 shares at 1 yen each, having intended to sell 1 share at 610,000 yen.

</details>

### 58. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.4.1] Which of the following is a management risk rather than a market risk?

- a. A rise in factor costs
- b. A natural disaster
- c. A sudden market-wide shock
- d. A risk profile mismatch between the client and the recommended product

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. A risk profile mismatch between the client and the recommended product

**Rationale:** Management risk relates to how investments are potentially mismanaged. Risk profile mismatch and concentration risk are the two forms. Market shocks, factor costs and natural disasters are market risks.

</details>

### 59. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.4.1] What is a short squeeze?

- a. A situation where an absence of sellers forces short positions to cover at substantially higher prices
- b. A regulatory restriction on short selling
- c. A narrowing of the bid-offer spread
- d. A sudden fall in price forcing long positions to liquidate

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. A situation where an absence of sellers forces short positions to cover at substantially higher prices

**Rationale:** Those with a short position on a derivative instrument may find the price of the underlying asset suddenly rising on good news. If there is an absence of sellers, in this short squeeze situation, traders may be forced to cover their short positions at substantially higher prices.

</details>

### 60. **[Multiple response]** [CM-EIP §2.4.2] Which of the following are elements of risk management described in the guide? (Select all that apply)

- a. Due diligence checks on fund managers
- b. Timely research and risk management processes
- c. Client profile and product suitability assessment
- d. Process and system risk management

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A, B, C, D

**Rationale:** Risk management involves due diligence, timely research and risk management processes, assessing client profile and product suitability, risk diversification, and process and system risk management.

</details>

### 61. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.5.1] A SGD 1 million deposit earns 8% simple interest for 91 days. What is the interest earned?

- a. SGD 40,288.23
- b. SGD 19,945.21
- c. SGD 20,000.00
- d. SGD 20,343.02

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. SGD 19,945.21

**Rationale:** Simple interest = (1,000,000 × 8 × 91) / 36,500 = SGD 19,945.21. The denominator is 36,500 because Singapore dollar interest uses a 365-day basis.

</details>

### 62. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.5.1] Why does Singapore dollar interest use a denominator of 36,500 while US dollar interest uses 36,000?

- a. Because US deposits are always shorter term
- b. Because Singapore has higher interest rates
- c. Because the day basis differs: 365 days for Singapore dollars and 360 days for US dollars
- d. Because Singapore uses simple rather than compound interest

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Because the day basis differs: 365 days for Singapore dollars and 360 days for US dollars

**Rationale:** The denominator depends on the currency's day basis. Singapore dollar interest is based on a 365-day basis; US dollar interest is based on a 360-day basis.

</details>

### 63. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.5.2] What is the future value of $1,000 invested for 10 years at 5% compounded annually?

- a. $1,500.00
- b. $1,628.89
- c. $1,643.62
- d. $1,647.01

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. $1,628.89

**Rationale:** FV = PV(1 + k)^n = 1,000(1.05)^10 = 1,000(1.62889) = $1,628.89.

</details>

### 64. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.5.4] What is the future value of $1,000 invested for 10 years at 5% compounded quarterly?

- a. $1,647.01
- b. $1,500.00
- c. $1,628.89
- d. $1,643.62

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. $1,643.62

**Rationale:** FV = PV(1 + k/m)^(mn) = 1,000(1 + 0.05/4)^(4×10) = 1,000(1.0125)^40 = 1,000(1.64362) = $1,643.62.

</details>

### 65. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.5.4] An investment grows from $1,000 to $1,628.89 over 10 years. What is the compounded annual growth rate?

- a. 5.00%
- b. 6.29%
- c. 4.88%
- d. 62.89%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. 5.00%

**Rationale:** CAGR = (FV/PV)^(1/n) − 1 = (1,628.89/1,000)^(1/10) − 1 = 1.62889^0.1 − 1 = 0.05 = 5%.

</details>

### 66. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §2.5.5] At a 6% discount rate, what is the present value of $1,000 receivable in five years?

- a. $792.09
- b. $747.27
- c. $747.26
- d. $1,338.23

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. $747.27

**Rationale:** PV = FV / (1 + k)^n = 1,000 / (1.06)^5 = 1,000 / 1.33823 = $747.27.

</details>

### 67. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.5.5] What is the minimum appropriate discount rate for a present value calculation?

- a. The average market return
- b. The issuer's coupon rate
- c. The risk-free government bond yield of the same maturity as the intended holding period
- d. The current inflation rate

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The risk-free government bond yield of the same maturity as the intended holding period

**Rationale:** The actual discount rate is at least the risk-free government bond yield that has the same maturity as the intended holding period of the investment.

</details>

### 68. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.5.3] What is the effect of negative interest rates on the present value of short-end fixed income investments?

- a. It lowers the present value
- b. It has no effect
- c. It makes the present value indeterminate
- d. It raises the present value

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. It raises the present value

**Rationale:** Negative central bank rates drive short-term rates down, and investors initially tried to lock in yields at the short end, driving short-term fixed income values up. In time value terms, negative interest rates raise the present value of fixed income investments at the short end of the yield curve.

</details>

### 69. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §2.6] Which pair of risks make opposite predictions about the impact of a rise in interest rates on a bond investor's return?

- a. Interest rate risk and reinvestment risk
- b. Inflation risk and political risk
- c. Event risk and sector risk
- d. Credit risk and liquidity risk

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Interest rate risk and reinvestment risk

**Rationale:** A rise in interest rates pushes the bond price down (interest rate risk) while raising the rate at which coupons can be reinvested (reinvestment risk). These are not fully offsetting: generally the reinvestment effect only slightly mitigates the interest rate effect.

</details>

## Chapter 3 — Key Drivers of Market Movements and Asset Values

### 70. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.1] Which three primary factors influence asset values and market movement?

- a. Earnings, dividends and growth
- b. Fundamental factors, market-driven factors and investment behaviour
- c. Interest rates, inflation and exchange rates
- d. Supply, demand and regulation

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Fundamental factors, market-driven factors and investment behaviour

**Rationale:** There are three primary factors: fundamental factors, market-driven factors and investment behaviour. Fundamental and market-driven factors provide the rational explanation; investor behaviour reflects how emotion and sentiment affect price.

</details>

### 71. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.1] What does the Efficient Market Hypothesis contend?

- a. Prices never reflect available information
- b. Markets are always irrational
- c. Asset prices adjust rapidly to reflect new publicly available information, so price is fair value and no individual can consistently outperform
- d. Fundamental analysis always beats technical analysis

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Asset prices adjust rapidly to reflect new publicly available information, so price is fair value and no individual can consistently outperform

**Rationale:** The EMH postulated by Eugene Fama contends that markets are efficient and asset prices adjust rapidly to reflect any new publicly available information. Therefore an asset's price is its fair value and no individual may consistently outperform the market.

</details>

### 72. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.1.1] Which factor group is described as reflecting how emotional and market sentiment affects price movement?

- a. Regulatory factors
- b. Fundamental factors
- c. Market-driven factors
- d. Investment behaviour

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Investment behaviour

**Rationale:** Fundamental and market-driven factors provide the rational and logical explanation for how an asset's worth is reflected in its price, while investor behaviour reflects how emotional and market sentiment affects the price movement of the asset.

</details>

### 73. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.1] What is top-down asset allocation?

- a. Allocating a larger proportion of funds to developed economies, growing sectors and leading corporate performers
- b. Allocating equally across all asset classes
- c. Allocating only to index funds
- d. Selecting individual stocks first, then sectors

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Allocating a larger proportion of funds to developed economies, growing sectors and leading corporate performers

**Rationale:** In top-down asset allocation, a larger proportion of funds is allocated to the developed economies, growing sectors and industries, and the leading corporate performers within these industries.

</details>

### 74. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.2] What is the multiplier effect of fiscal policy?

- a. The tendency of tax cuts to increase the budget deficit
- b. The process by which an initial round of government spending ripples through industries, raising incomes and further consumption
- c. The multiplication of money supply through bank lending
- d. The compounding of investment returns over time

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. The process by which an initial round of government spending ripples through industries, raising incomes and further consumption

**Rationale:** Money from fiscal expenditure passes through the economy and eventually flows to consumers, producing a multiplier effect. The initial round of spending ripples through different industries, raising wages and profits, creating higher disposable incomes and triggering further consumption.

</details>

### 75. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §3.2.3] Using GDP = C + I + G + NE, which component is directly raised by a government programme of infrastructure development?

- a. C
- b. I
- c. G
- d. NE

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. G

**Rationale:** Government spending includes both investment and consumption, covering education, defence, capital investment, infrastructure development and health services. Infrastructure projects therefore raise G.

</details>

### 76. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.3] Which fiscal measure is intended to improve net exports?

- a. Increasing infrastructure spending
- b. Offering tax incentives for companies to relocate factories home
- c. Lowering the personal income tax rate
- d. Imposing tariffs on selected foreign goods

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Imposing tariffs on selected foreign goods

**Rationale:** Imposing tariffs on selected foreign goods makes the prices of some foreign goods less attractive relative to domestically made goods, which is intended to improve net exports, traditionally negative.

</details>

### 77. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.4] Which of the Fed's three tools has it relied on most heavily as its key method for influencing market liquidity?

- a. Open market operations
- b. Reserve requirement changes
- c. The discount window
- d. The Fed Funds Rate

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Open market operations

**Rationale:** Of the three, the Fed has relied heavily on open market operations as its key method for influencing market liquidity. It was used extensively to pump liquidity into the market in 2008.

</details>

### 78. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.4] In what order does a change in liquidity from open market operations affect the economy?

- a. The aggregate economy, then common stocks, then bond prices
- b. Bond prices, then common stocks, then the aggregate economy
- c. Common stocks, then bond prices, then the aggregate economy
- d. All three simultaneously

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Bond prices, then common stocks, then the aggregate economy

**Rationale:** This type of change in liquidity first affects bond prices, then common stocks and, finally, the aggregate economy.

</details>

### 79. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.4] What is the primary intermediate target of Singapore's monetary policy?

- a. The money supply
- b. Domestic interest rates
- c. The trade-weighted exchange rate
- d. The inflation rate directly

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The trade-weighted exchange rate

**Rationale:** Because of its open economy, Singapore's monetary regime centres around the management of its exchange rate. MAS conducts policy by managing a trade-weighted exchange rate index. The choice of the exchange rate as intermediate target implies MAS gives up control over domestic interest rates and money supply.

</details>

### 80. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.4] How often is Singapore's exchange rate policy band reviewed, and what is released after each review?

- a. Annually, with an Annual Report
- b. Monthly, with a press release
- c. Quarterly, with a Budget Statement
- d. About every six months, with a Monetary Policy Statement

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. About every six months, with a Monetary Policy Statement

**Rationale:** The length of the policy review cycle is typically six months. A Monetary Policy Statement is released after each review, accompanied by a Macroeconomic Review.

</details>

### 81. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §3.2.4] With a 10% reserve requirement, a new deposit of 10,000 passes through the banking system. What is the total money supply created after all rounds?

- a. 10,000
- b. 90,000
- c. 100,000
- d. 1,000,000

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 100,000

**Rationale:** A bank takes in a new deposit and lends out 90%, keeping 10% as statutory reserves. The process repeats through other banks, leading to a 10-fold increase in money supply: 10,000 becomes 100,000.

</details>

### 82. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.4] According to Fisher's equation of exchange, what happens when the central bank increases the money supply and the equation is fully operative?

- a. The price level rises, and velocity determines output
- b. Velocity falls proportionally
- c. Nothing changes
- d. Output rises proportionally

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. The price level rises, and velocity determines output

**Rationale:** When the equation is fully operative, the central bank cannot increase output by increasing money supply because as money M increases, the price level P increases, and the velocity of money V, which is socially determined, then determines output Q.

</details>

### 83. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.4] In the positive scenario for stock prices when interest rates rise, why may stock prices remain fairly stable?

- a. Because earnings fall as fast as the required return rises
- b. Because the increase in the required rate of return is offset by the increase in earnings growth
- c. Because dividends are cut
- d. Because the market ignores interest rates

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Because the increase in the required rate of return is offset by the increase in earnings growth

**Rationale:** In the positive scenario, rates rise with inflation and strong corporate earnings, and firms pass cost increases on to consumers. Stock prices may remain fairly stable because the increase in required rate of return k is offset by the increase in earnings growth g.

</details>

### 84. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.4] Why do banks typically benefit from a gradual increase in interest rates during the initial expansion?

- a. Because loan defaults fall to zero
- b. Because their bond portfolios appreciate
- c. Because they can widen the net interest spread by raising lending rates faster than deposit rates while still enjoying loan growth
- d. Because deposit rates rise faster than lending rates

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Because they can widen the net interest spread by raising lending rates faster than deposit rates while still enjoying loan growth

**Rationale:** During the initial expansion, the banking sector benefits from its ability to widen the net interest spread, raising lending cost faster than deposit rates, while still enjoying the demand in loan growth, creating net interest income expansion.

</details>

### 85. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.5] What is a technical recession?

- a. A rise in unemployment above 5%
- b. A decline in industrial production for one month
- c. A fall in the stock market of more than 20%
- d. Two quarters of contraction in GDP growth

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Two quarters of contraction in GDP growth

**Rationale:** Before GDP growth numbers reflecting two quarters of contraction, that is a technical recession, are announced, the equity markets would have typically corrected ahead of the announcements.

</details>

### 86. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.5] Which investment strategy is appropriate in the late bear to early bull phase of the business cycle?

- a. Invest in cyclical and high-beta equities and reduce cash holdings
- b. Buy growth stocks and high-yield bonds
- c. Hold defensive high-dividend stocks and investment grade bonds
- d. Keep cash, investment grade bonds and precious metals

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Invest in cyclical and high-beta equities and reduce cash holdings

**Rationale:** In the late bear to early bull phase, markets perceive the worst is over and asset values begin to rebound sharply. The appropriate strategy is to invest in cyclical and high-beta equities and reduce cash holdings.

</details>

### 87. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.5] What does a flattening yield curve suggest?

- a. An imminent economic boom
- b. One of the early predictors of an upcoming recession
- c. Rising inflation expectations
- d. A widening credit spread

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. One of the early predictors of an upcoming recession

**Rationale:** When longer-term credit demand appears weak, long-maturity bond rates may not rise as much even with Fed fund rate hikes. This results in a flattening of the yield curve, which is considered one of the early predictors of an upcoming recession.

</details>

### 88. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.6] Why do investors monitor economic calendars closely?

- a. Because calendars predict the exact market direction
- b. To avoid trading altogether
- c. Because price volatility of affected financial assets tends to increase immediately before and after an information release
- d. Because releases are always accurate

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Because price volatility of affected financial assets tends to increase immediately before and after an information release

**Rationale:** Knowing the dates and times of the releases of key information is useful because the price volatility of affected financial assets tends to increase during the period immediately before and after the information release.

</details>

### 89. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.7] How many components make up the US Composite Leading Economic Index published by The Conference Board?

- a. 4
- b. 7
- c. 10
- d. 12

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 10

**Rationale:** The CLEI comprises 10 indicators used to predict the direction of the economy. The Coincident Economic Indicators comprise 4 series and the lagging index comprises 7 components.

</details>

### 90. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.7] What does a Purchasing Managers' Index reading of 50 signify?

- a. Severe contraction
- b. No data available
- c. Strong expansion
- d. The dividing line between expansion and contraction

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. The dividing line between expansion and contraction

**Rationale:** In general, a reading of 50 or higher suggests expansion in the manufacturing sector whilst a reading below 50 indicates a contraction.

</details>

### 91. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.7] Which of the following is a coincident indicator?

- a. Nonfarm payroll employment
- b. Average prime rate charged by banks
- c. Housing starts and building permits
- d. Average weekly hours in manufacturing

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Nonfarm payroll employment

**Rationale:** Nonfarm payroll employment is one of the four Coincident Economic Indicators. Average weekly hours and housing starts are leading indicators; the average prime rate is a lagging indicator.

</details>

### 92. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.7] What is the principal limitation of composite leading indicators?

- a. They are published too infrequently to be useful
- b. They signal turning points but say nothing about the magnitude of the shifts
- c. They only apply to developing economies
- d. They are always wrong

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. They signal turning points but say nothing about the magnitude of the shifts

**Rationale:** While such composite indicators are useful as short-term indicators of turning points in the economy, they do not tell investors anything about the magnitude of these shifts.

</details>

### 93. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.8] Why can a country with a widening trade deficit become a target of speculative attack?

- a. Because its inflation rate is too low
- b. Because it has too much foreign currency reserves
- c. Because the deficit makes the country a debtor nation
- d. Because its interest rates are too high

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Because the deficit makes the country a debtor nation

**Rationale:** A country with a widening trade deficit can become the target of speculative attacks because this deficit makes the country a debtor nation. Such weaknesses are generally believed to have led to the Asian Currency Crisis in 1997.

</details>

### 94. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.8] Which inflation measure does the US Fed use as its primary gauge?

- a. The producer price index
- b. Average hourly earnings
- c. The headline Consumer Price Index
- d. Core Personal Consumption Expenditure

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Core Personal Consumption Expenditure

**Rationale:** The US Fed uses Core Personal Consumption Expenditure data, or core inflation, as its primary measure and has traditionally set 2% as the long-run inflation rate.

</details>

### 95. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.9] What happened to the pound sterling after the Brexit referendum in June 2016?

- a. It fell 10.5% in four days and was down 18% by October
- b. It was unchanged
- c. It fell 51.9% immediately
- d. It rose 10.5% in four days

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. It fell 10.5% in four days and was down 18% by October

**Rationale:** The surprise 51.9% affirmation vote for Britain to leave the European Union resulted in panic selling of the GBP. It fell 10.5% in 4 days and continued to drift downwards to a low of GBP/USD 1.21714, down 18%, by October.

</details>

### 96. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.10] In which stage of the industry life cycle are profit margins very high and profits able to more than double from a low earnings base?

- a. Early development
- b. Rapid growth
- c. Mature growth
- d. Decline

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Rapid growth

**Rationale:** In rapid growth, rising sales growth coupled with greater efficiency results in accelerating profit growth. During this phase profits can more than double from the low earnings base, and profit margins are also very high.

</details>

### 97. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.10] Which stage of the industry life cycle is described as probably the longest phase?

- a. Early development
- b. Rapid growth
- c. Stabilisation
- d. Decline

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Stabilisation

**Rationale:** Stabilisation is probably the longest phase of the life cycle. The industry growth rate tends to be in line with the growth rate of the overall economy.

</details>

### 98. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.10] Which of Porter's five forces is illustrated by the bankruptcy of Kodak following the emergence of digital photography?

- a. Bargaining power of suppliers
- b. Rivalry among existing competitors
- c. Threat of new entrants
- d. Threat of substitute products

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Threat of substitute products

**Rationale:** Substitute products limit the profit potential of an industry because they limit the prices that firms can charge. The emergence of digital photography and imaging resulted in the demise of camera film, bankrupting film-producing giant Kodak.

</details>

### 99. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.10] Which industry type is least affected by recession and economic adversity?

- a. Defensive
- b. Growth
- c. Interest-sensitive
- d. Cyclical

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Defensive

**Rationale:** Least affected by recession and economic adversity are the defensive industries. The food and public utilities industries are considered to be defensive.

</details>

### 100. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.2.11] Which approach to stock-picking focuses on firms with specific competitive advantages that distinguish them from others in the same industry?

- a. Top-down
- b. Bottom-up
- c. Sector rotation
- d. Market timing

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Bottom-up

**Rationale:** By applying a bottom-up approach to stock-picking, firms with specific competitive advantages distinguish themselves from others.

</details>

### 101. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.3.1] What typically happens when the bulk of available funds is almost fully invested in the market?

- a. New IPOs are cancelled
- b. The market accelerates upward
- c. The market plateaus and funds rotate to lagging sectors, with parts of the market becoming overvalued
- d. Interest rates fall sharply

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The market plateaus and funds rotate to lagging sectors, with parts of the market becoming overvalued

**Rationale:** When there are insufficient funds to sustain the upward drive, the market will start to plateau, with funds rotating to industry sectors and assets that have been lagging in relative performance. Typically this is when parts of the market become overvalued.

</details>

### 102. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.3.2] Why can a cluster of large IPOs depress the prices of similar stocks already trading?

- a. Because IPOs reduce market capitalisation
- b. Because IPOs raise interest rates
- c. Because IPOs increase the money supply
- d. Because funds are absorbed by the IPOs, and some investors take profit on existing stocks to participate

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Because funds are absorbed by the IPOs, and some investors take profit on existing stocks to participate

**Rationale:** Funds will be absorbed by IPOs, in some cases deliberately priced at a discount to ensure demand. This flow of monies to the IPOs can come at the expense of similar stocks already trading, and some investors will take profit on existing stocks to participate.

</details>

### 103. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.3.3] Why can futures and options amplify a market decline?

- a. Because leverage magnifies losses, forcing investors to reduce holdings to meet margin requirements and driving the market down further
- b. Because they are only traded by retail investors
- c. Because they settle in cash
- d. Because they are unleveraged

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because leverage magnifies losses, forcing investors to reduce holdings to meet margin requirements and driving the market down further

**Rationale:** Because of the leverage, returns to a derivative investor are magnified. As underlying assets fall, they cause further reduction to futures and options prices with magnified losses, and derivative investors may be forced to reduce holdings to meet margin requirements, further driving down the market.

</details>

### 104. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.3.3] When does the triple witching hour occur in the US market?

- a. The first Friday of each quarter, at the open
- b. The third Friday of March, June, September and December, in the last trading hour
- c. The last trading day of each month, at midday
- d. Every Friday at the close

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. The third Friday of March, June, September and December, in the last trading hour

**Rationale:** On the third Friday of every March, June, September and December, the last hour of the stock market trading session, 3.00 to 4.00pm New York local time, is called the triple witching hour. Index futures, index options and stock options all mature that day.

</details>

### 105. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.3.4] What is hot money?

- a. Money raised through an IPO
- b. Money held in central bank reserves
- c. Global money from hedge and other large funds seeking temporary inefficiencies and arbitrage opportunities
- d. Money deposited in savings accounts

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Global money from hedge and other large funds seeking temporary inefficiencies and arbitrage opportunities

**Rationale:** Hot money is global money from hedge and other large funds that seek to exploit temporary inefficiencies and arbitrage opportunities. It may flow into a market, sector or set of stocks and temporarily push up volume, interest and prices.

</details>

### 106. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.3.5] In a call market such as some French bourses, what is prioritised?

- a. Both price and timing equally
- b. Neither price nor timing
- c. Timing, but not price
- d. Price, but not timing

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Price, but not timing

**Rationale:** In a call market all the bids are submitted and batched for simultaneous execution once or twice a day when the market is called. Price, but not timing, prioritisation is practised.

</details>

### 107. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.3.5] What is the principal concern about dark pools for less sophisticated participants?

- a. Internally agreed trade prices, together with the size and lack of transparency of participant identities, put them at a disadvantage
- b. They only trade government bonds
- c. They guarantee better prices
- d. They are illegal in all jurisdictions

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Internally agreed trade prices, together with the size and lack of transparency of participant identities, put them at a disadvantage

**Rationale:** In the case of dark pools, internally agreed trade prices, along with the size and lack of transparency of identities of dark pool participants, put less sophisticated market participants at a disadvantage.

</details>

### 108. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.4.1] When does the herd instinct kick in most strongly?

- a. When information is abundant and markets are calm
- b. When information is scarce and threats seem imminent
- c. When interest rates are falling
- d. When volatility is low

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. When information is scarce and threats seem imminent

**Rationale:** Behavioural studies show that the herd instinct kicks in especially when information is scarce, and threats seem imminent.

</details>

### 109. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.4.1] What was the market price-earnings ratio at the peak of the US Internet Dot.com Bubble?

- a. 20x
- b. 50x
- c. 200x
- d. 500x

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 200x

**Rationale:** NASDAQ rose sharply from 1,000 to 5,000, with a market price-earnings ratio of 200x. Alan Greenspan coined the term irrational exuberance in December 1996, but the bubble continued until 2001.

</details>

### 110. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.4.2] How does loss aversion affect portfolio behaviour?

- a. Investors buy more of their losers immediately
- b. Investors sell losing positions first to realise losses
- c. Investors sell profitable holdings to lock in gains while holding on to book losses in the hope of a rebound
- d. Investors hold all positions equally

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Investors sell profitable holdings to lock in gains while holding on to book losses in the hope of a rebound

**Rationale:** Investors and investment managers may be unconsciously influenced by loss aversion to sell off their profitable holdings to lock in positive returns while still holding on to book losses on poorer performing securities in the hope that the securities rebound. This potentially has a double negative impact.

</details>

### 111. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.4.3] An investor who has committed funds to an investment only reads research supporting that investment. Which bias is this?

- a. Anchoring
- b. Herd instinct
- c. Loss aversion
- d. Confirmation bias

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Confirmation bias

**Rationale:** Confirmation bias refers to how we, as investors, tend to look for views and recommendations that are aligned with our own. We then reinforce the view by committing even more funds to the same investment.

</details>

### 112. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §3.5] Which of the following is a market-driven factor rather than a fundamental factor?

- a. Liquidity and funds flow
- b. Firm-specific competitive advantage
- c. Monetary policy
- d. Industry life cycle

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Liquidity and funds flow

**Rationale:** Market-driven factors refer to physical market conditions that affect short-term demand and supply. Major market-driven factors include liquidity flows, new issues of securities, trading influences from derivatives, hot-money flows and computer-driven momentum trading.

</details>

## Chapter 4 — Foreign Exchange

### 113. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.1.1] In the quotation USD/SGD 1.2520, which currency is the base currency and what does the rate mean?

- a. SGD is the base; SGD 1 = USD 1.2520
- b. USD is the base; USD 1 = SGD 1.2520
- c. USD is the counter currency; SGD 1 = USD 1.2520
- d. Both are base currencies

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. USD is the base; USD 1 = SGD 1.2520

**Rationale:** The first listed currency is the base currency and the second is the counter or term currency. The quotation refers to the number of units of counter currency that equals one unit of base currency, so USD 1 = SGD 1.2520.

</details>

### 114. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.1.1] An investor borrowed USD to fund a SGD investment. How is the resulting foreign exchange position described?

- a. Short both currencies
- b. Long USD and short SGD
- c. Short USD and long SGD
- d. Long both currencies

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Short USD and long SGD

**Rationale:** The investor sold USD spot for SGD, so the position is short USD and long SGD. To close it, the investor enters the opposite transaction, selling spot SGD and buying USD, and is said to have squared off the position.

</details>

### 115. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.1.1] What is the standard settlement period for a spot foreign exchange transaction?

- a. Five working days
- b. Same day
- c. Next working day
- d. Two working days

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Two working days

**Rationale:** Usually foreign exchange transactions are spot deals, made for delivery in two working days (value spot). There is also a market for same day delivery (value today) and next day delivery (value tomorrow).

</details>

### 116. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.2.1] What is the effect of higher interest rates on a currency, all else equal?

- a. They attract foreign capital inflow, causing the exchange rate to rise
- b. They cause capital outflow, weakening the currency
- c. They have no effect on the exchange rate
- d. They always cause immediate depreciation

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. They attract foreign capital inflow, causing the exchange rate to rise

**Rationale:** Higher interest rates offer lenders in an economy higher returns relative to other countries. Consequently, higher interest rates attract inflow of foreign capital, which causes the exchange rate to rise. The impact may be moderated by higher inflation or other factors.

</details>

### 117. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.2.2] What happened to the New Venezuelan Bolivar on the day of its launch in August 2018?

- a. It appreciated 95%
- b. It fell 95% in value
- c. It was unchanged
- d. It was pegged to the US dollar

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. It fell 95% in value

**Rationale:** Despite launching the New Venezuelan Bolivar in late August 2018, the new currency fell by 95% in value the very day of the launch. The IMF indicated Venezuela's inflation would have hit 1,000,000% in 2018.

</details>

### 118. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.2.4] Which countries successfully defended their currencies during the Asian Currency Crisis despite speculative attack?

- a. India, Philippines and Vietnam
- b. Indonesia, Thailand and Malaysia
- c. Hong Kong, Singapore and Taiwan
- d. Japan, Korea and China

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Hong Kong, Singapore and Taiwan

**Rationale:** Hedge funds and other speculators were able to exploit the vulnerability of some Asian currencies that had weaker foreign currency reserves, but countries with strong currency reserves such as Hong Kong, Singapore and Taiwan successfully defended their currencies.

</details>

### 119. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.2.5] What does a trade deficit imply for demand for the domestic currency, all else equal?

- a. No change in demand
- b. Immediate appreciation
- c. Higher demand for the domestic currency
- d. Weaker demand for the local currency

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Weaker demand for the local currency

**Rationale:** When a country has a trade deficit it needs to acquire more foreign currency to pay for imports, resulting in higher demand for foreign currency. All other things held constant, a trade deficit should lead to weaker demand for local currency.

</details>

### 120. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.2.5] What does an improvement in a country's terms of trade imply?

- a. Export prices rise by a greater rate than import prices, implying higher demand for the currency and appreciation
- b. Import prices rise faster than export prices
- c. The trade balance is unchanged
- d. The currency must depreciate

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Export prices rise by a greater rate than import prices, implying higher demand for the currency and appreciation

**Rationale:** The terms of trade improve when a country's export prices rise by a greater rate than its import prices. This in turn implies higher demand for the country's currency and accompanying currency appreciation.

</details>

### 121. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.2.6] Why could Greece not inflate its way out of its public debt crisis?

- a. Because its debt was denominated in gold
- b. Because as a Eurozone member it had no monetary policy independence
- c. Because it had no central bank
- d. Because its debt was all short-term

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Because as a Eurozone member it had no monetary policy independence

**Rationale:** As Greece did not have monetary policy independence because it is a member of the Eurozone, it could not simply inflate its way out of debt. Greece has needed three bail-out programmes totalling 300 billion euros.

</details>

### 122. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.3] In what order do foreign exchange markets open through the trading day?

- a. New York, London, Tokyo, Sydney
- b. London, New York, Tokyo, Sydney
- c. Sydney, Tokyo, Singapore/Hong Kong, London, New York, San Francisco
- d. Tokyo, London, Sydney, New York

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Sydney, Tokyo, Singapore/Hong Kong, London, New York, San Francisco

**Rationale:** The foreign exchange markets work round the clock, starting the day in Sydney, then Tokyo, Singapore/Hong Kong, London, New York and San Francisco.

</details>

### 123. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.3] According to the BIS Triennial Survey, what was average daily foreign exchange turnover in April 2019?

- a. USD2.0 trillion
- b. USD3.2 trillion
- c. USD5.1 trillion
- d. USD6.6 trillion

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. USD6.6 trillion

**Rationale:** Trading in FX markets averaged USD6.6 trillion per day in April 2019, up from USD5.1 trillion in April 2016. FX swaps rose to USD3.2 trillion per day, almost half of global FX trading.

</details>

### 124. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.3] Which currency was on one side of 88% of all foreign exchange trades in April 2019?

- a. The US dollar
- b. The Japanese yen
- c. The Chinese renminbi
- d. The euro

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. The US dollar

**Rationale:** The US dollar remained the world's dominant vehicle currency, on one side of 88% of all trades in April 2019. The euro remained second at 32%.

</details>

### 125. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.3] Which currencies are described as commodity currencies?

- a. CHF and JPY
- b. AUD and ZAR
- c. SGD and HKD
- d. EUR and GBP

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. AUD and ZAR

**Rationale:** The Australian dollar and South African rand are considered commodity currencies and tend to be particularly strong during periods of higher global economic growth or fiscal stimulus, when demand for metals and raw materials drives demand for both.

</details>

### 126. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.4] What is a pip in foreign exchange?

- a. 1% of the exchange rate
- b. 0.01, the second decimal place
- c. 0.0001, the fourth decimal place
- d. 0.001, the third decimal place

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 0.0001, the fourth decimal place

**Rationale:** FX rates are usually quoted to the fourth decimal place. The last digit of the quote is called a pip, equal to 0.0001, the smallest unit of price movement or quotation for foreign exchange.

</details>

### 127. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §4.4.1] A dealer quotes USD/SGD 1.2520-25. What is the spread in basis points?

- a. 0.5 basis points
- b. 50 basis points
- c. 25 basis points
- d. 5 basis points

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. 5 basis points

**Rationale:** The difference between 1.2525 and 1.2520 is 0.0005, also known as 5 basis points, which is the spread or profit earned by the bank.

</details>
