### 128. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.4.1] When a client asks a bank for a quote, at which prices does the client transact?

- a. Buys at the offer and sells at the bid
- b. Buys and sells at the mid rate
- c. Buys and sells at the bid
- d. Buys at the bid and sells at the offer

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Buys at the offer and sells at the bid

**Rationale:** The party asking for a quote will always buy at the offer rate and sell at the bid rate. In market parlance, the price is always against the price taker.

</details>

### 129. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.4.2] Which of the following is a direct quote in Singapore?

- a. GBP/USD 1.6816
- b. USD/SGD 1.2520
- c. AUD/USD 0.9235
- d. EUR/USD 1.3622

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. USD/SGD 1.2520

**Rationale:** A direct quote expresses the price of one unit of foreign currency in units of domestic currency, that is foreign/domestic. In Singapore, USD/SGD is a direct quote. GBP/USD, AUD/USD and EUR/USD are indirect quotes in Singapore.

</details>

### 130. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §4.4.3] In Singapore the direct quote is USD/SGD 1.2520. What is the corresponding indirect quote SGD/USD?

- a. 1.0000
- b. 0.8000
- c. 0.7987
- d. 1.2520

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 0.7987

**Rationale:** The indirect quote is the reciprocal of the direct quote: 1 / 1.2520 = 0.7987.

</details>

### 131. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.4.3] Which currencies are quoted as the base currency against the US dollar, contrary to the usual convention?

- a. CHF, CAD and SEK
- b. INR, IDR and THB
- c. JPY, CNY and SGD
- d. GBP, AUD, NZD and EUR

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. GBP, AUD, NZD and EUR

**Rationale:** Currencies in the colonial or old sterling bloc, comprising the UK pound sterling, the Australian dollar and the New Zealand dollar, are the base currency when quoted against the USD. The EUR is also quoted in this manner.

</details>

### 132. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §4.4.4] USD/SGD is 1.3362/1.3382 and USD/JPY is 112.6530/112.7330. What is the SGD/JPY bid rate?

- a. 84.1825
- b. 84.3684
- c. 84.2755
- d. 150.6100

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. 84.1825

**Rationale:** To get the SGD/JPY bid, divide the USD/JPY bid by the USD/SGD ask: 112.6530 / 1.3382 = 84.1825. To get the ask, divide the USD/JPY ask by the USD/SGD bid: 112.7330 / 1.3362 = 84.3684.

</details>

### 133. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.5] What does an investor forfeit by covering a foreign currency exposure with a forward contract?

- a. The right to receive dividends
- b. Potential gains from favourable movements in market rates
- c. The ability to sell the underlying security
- d. The interest earned on the foreign currency

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Potential gains from favourable movements in market rates

**Rationale:** By covering the exposure through locking in a known forward FX rate, the investor also forfeits potential gains from favourable movements in market rates.

</details>

### 134. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.5.1] Which currency generally trades at a forward discount?

- a. Both currencies equally
- b. Neither, since forward rates equal spot rates
- c. The currency of the country with the higher interest rate
- d. The currency of the country with the lower interest rate

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The currency of the country with the higher interest rate

**Rationale:** Generally, the currency of the country with the higher interest rate will trade at a forward discount, meaning the forward rate is cheaper than the spot rate. The currency with the lower interest rate trades at a forward premium.

</details>

### 135. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §4.5.2] Spot USD/SGD is 1.2520, the USD rate is 1.75% and the SGD rate is 0.75%, for 30 days. What are the forward points?

- a. +10 points
- b. −100 points
- c. +100 points
- d. −10 points

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. −10 points

**Rationale:** Forward points = S(Rc − Rb)(n/360) / [1 + Rb(n/360)] = 1.2520(0.0075 − 0.0175)(30/360) / [1 + 0.0175(30/360)] = −0.0010 / 1.0015 = −SGD 0.0010, or −10 points. The forward rate is 1.2510.

</details>

### 136. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.5.2] What do forward points reflect?

- a. The relative interest rates of the two currencies only
- b. The credit risk of the counterparties
- c. The expected inflation differential
- d. A forecast of the future spot rate

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. The relative interest rates of the two currencies only

**Rationale:** Forward points are only a reflection of the relative interest rates of the currencies. They are not an indication of what the future spot rate of the currencies will actually be.

</details>

### 137. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §4.5.3] The USD interest rate is 6%, the GBP rate is 5%, and GBP spot is USD 1.50. What is the one-year forward rate?

- a. USD 1.5000
- b. USD 1.5143
- c. USD 1.4857
- d. USD 1.5100

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. USD 1.5143

**Rationale:** By interest rate parity, F = S[(1 + Rc n/360)/(1 + Rb n/360)] = 1.50(1.06/1.05) = USD 1.5143 per GBP.

</details>

### 138. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §4.5.3] With the parity forward at USD 1.5143 but the market quoting USD 1.5100, an arbitrageur borrows GBP 1,000,000 at 5%, sells spot at 1.50, invests USD at 6% and buys GBP forward at 1.5100. What is the profit?

- a. USD 1,500
- b. USD 4,050
- c. USD 4,500
- d. USD 14,300

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. USD 4,500

**Rationale:** Repayment due is GBP 1,050,000. USD received = 1,000,000 × 1.50 = 1,500,000, growing at 6% to 1,590,000. Forward purchase of GBP 1,050,000 at 1.5100 costs 1,585,500. Profit = 1,590,000 − 1,585,500 = USD 4,500.

</details>

### 139. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §4.5.4] USD/JPY spot is 119.22/25 and one-month forward points are 52/51. What is the outright one-month rate?

- a. 119.2252/119.2551
- b. 119.2720/119.2760
- c. 119.1680/119.1980
- d. 119.2148/119.2449

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. 119.2148/119.2449

**Rationale:** Descending points (52/51) are negative, so subtract: 119.22 − 0.52 = 119.2148 and 119.25 − 0.51 = 119.2449. The bid is higher than the offer in point terms because the currency is at a forward discount.

</details>

### 140. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §4.5.4] AUD/USD spot is 0.6475/80 and one-month forward points are 17/21. What is the outright one-month rate?

- a. 0.6492/0.6501
- b. 0.6458/0.6459
- c. 0.6475/0.6480
- d. 0.6492/0.6480

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. 0.6492/0.6501

**Rationale:** Ascending points (17/21) are positive, so add: 0.6475 + 0.0017 = 0.6492 and 0.6480 + 0.0021 = 0.6501.

</details>

### 141. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.5.5] What is the key difference between a non-deliverable forward and a regular FX forward?

- a. An NDF has a longer tenor
- b. There is no physical delivery or exchange of the domestic currency in the offshore market
- c. An NDF is exchange-traded
- d. An NDF requires delivery of the notional amount

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. There is no physical delivery or exchange of the domestic currency in the offshore market

**Rationale:** The concept of an NDF is relatively similar to that of a regular FX forward. The key difference is that there is no physical delivery or exchange of the domestic currency in the offshore market. An NDF is a cash-settled, short-term forward contract.

</details>

### 142. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.5.5] What is the normal tenor of a non-deliverable forward contract?

- a. Five years or more
- b. One week to one month
- c. One month to one year
- d. One year to five years

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. One month to one year

**Rationale:** The normal tenor of the NDF contract ranges from 1 month to a year.

</details>

### 143. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §4.5.5] A client must pay CNY 10 million in three months. USD/CNY spot is 6.5845. If USD/CNY falls 5% to 6.2553, what is the additional USD cost compared with the current rate?

- a. USD 72,314
- b. USD 1,598,644
- c. USD 1,518,718
- d. USD 79,926

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. USD 79,926

**Rationale:** At 6.5845 the cost is 10,000,000 / 6.5845 = USD 1,518,718. At 6.2553 it is 10,000,000 / 6.2553 = USD 1,598,644. The difference is USD 79,926, which is the loss if the client does nothing.

</details>

### 144. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.6] Why is credit risk in a foreign exchange transaction only a fraction of the total transaction amount?

- a. Because the transaction is an exchange of buy and sell, not an outright extension of credit
- b. Because FX is exchange-traded
- c. Because settlement is immediate
- d. Because FX transactions are collateralised

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because the transaction is an exchange of buy and sell, not an outright extension of credit

**Rationale:** In forex transactions, credit risk is but a fraction of the total transaction amount because the transaction is an exchange, buy and sell, and not an outright extension of credit.

</details>

### 145. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.6] When does settlement or delivery risk arise in a foreign exchange transaction?

- a. At the moment the trade is agreed
- b. In the period just before settlement, which in FX is two days before payment
- c. Only if the counterparty becomes insolvent
- d. After settlement is complete

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. In the period just before settlement, which in FX is two days before payment

**Rationale:** Settlement risk is a specific counterparty credit risk which takes place in the period just before settlement. In FX, this is 2 days before payment. Continuous Linked Settlement has reduced delivery risk significantly.

</details>

### 146. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.7.1] Which exchange rate regime involves a country adopting another nation's currency as legal tender?

- a. Managed soft peg
- b. Currency board
- c. Currency substitution or dollarisation
- d. Monetary union

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Currency substitution or dollarisation

**Rationale:** A country may choose not to manage its own currency but to simply adopt another nation's currency as legal tender. This exchange rate regime is known as currency substitution or dollarization. The adopting country inherits the credibility of that currency, but not that other country's creditworthiness.

</details>

### 147. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.7.1] Under the Currency Interchangeability Agreement of 1967, how is the Brunei dollar related to the Singapore dollar?

- a. It is convertible only for trade payments
- b. It is not related
- c. It is pegged at a 5% discount
- d. It is interchangeable at par, and each is legal tender in the other country

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. It is interchangeable at par, and each is legal tender in the other country

**Rationale:** Under a Currency Interchangeability Agreement in 1967, the Brunei Dollar is interchangeable with the Singapore Dollar at par. The Brunei Dollar is legal tender in Singapore and vice versa.

</details>

### 148. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.7.1] What did China's defence of the CNY between July 2014 and December 2016 cost in foreign reserves?

- a. Almost USD 1 trillion, about 25%
- b. Almost USD 100 billion, about 5%
- c. Almost USD 500 billion, about 15%
- d. Nothing

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Almost USD 1 trillion, about 25%

**Rationale:** From July 2014 to December 2016, USD/CNY rose only about 11% from CNY6.20 to CNY6.90, but China ran down its foreign currency reserves by almost USD 1 trillion, or 25%.

</details>

### 149. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §4.7.2] A Singapore firm buys a GBP 1 million UK property, borrowing 80% of the value. GBP/SGD is 1.8060 and the GBP depreciates 5% to 1.7157 in a year. If the firm borrows in SGD, what is the loss?

- a. −SGD 1,444,800
- b. −SGD 90,300
- c. −SGD 18,060
- d. −SGD 361,200

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. −SGD 90,300

**Rationale:** Down payment = 0.2 × 1,000,000 × 1.8060 = SGD 361,200. SGD loan = 0.8 × 1,000,000 × 1.8060 = SGD 1,444,800. Sale proceeds = 1,000,000 × 1.7157 = SGD 1,715,700. Loss = 1,715,700 − 361,200 − 1,444,800 = −SGD 90,300.

</details>

### 150. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §4.7.2] Using the same property example, if the firm borrows in GBP instead, what is the loss?

- a. −SGD 343,140
- b. −SGD 361,200
- c. −SGD 18,060
- d. −SGD 90,300

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. −SGD 18,060

**Rationale:** Down payment = SGD 361,200. Net proceeds after repaying the GBP 800,000 loan = (1,000,000 − 800,000) × 1.7157 = SGD 343,140. Loss = 343,140 − 361,200 = −SGD 18,060. Borrowing in the investment currency is a natural hedge.

</details>

### 151. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.7.3] What does a currency carry trade rely on to be profitable?

- a. Falling interest rates in the invested currency
- b. A strengthening borrowing currency
- c. Rising volatility in the invested currency
- d. Relative stability in currency prices, so the interest differential is not wiped out by an adverse FX move

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Relative stability in currency prices, so the interest differential is not wiped out by an adverse FX move

**Rationale:** This strategy relies on relative stability in currency prices as an adverse exchange rate movement can wipe out the returns from the underlying interest rate differential.

</details>

### 152. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.7.3] In the JPY/AUD carry trade from 2008 to 2016, in which years did the strategy fail?

- a. 2008, 2011 and 2015
- b. 2010, 2013 and 2016
- c. Every year
- d. 2009, 2012 and 2014

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. 2008, 2011 and 2015

**Rationale:** For most years the strategy worked, but for 2008, 2011 and 2015 the strategy failed because the AUD, the invested currency, fell sharply against the borrowed currency, the JPY.

</details>

### 153. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §4.7.3] In 2008 the AUD fell 34.2% against the JPY and 19% against the SGD. What was the net loss on a JPY 100 million borrowing invested in AUD at 7%?

- a. −SGD 6,240
- b. −SGD 503,946
- c. +SGD 526,960
- d. −SGD 80,223

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. −SGD 503,946

**Rationale:** JPY 100 million was borrowed at 2.25%, converted into AUD 1.020 million (SGD 1.286 million) and invested at 7%. Total Australian proceeds converted back to SGD were SGD 1.111 million, lower than the SGD 1.615 million equivalent of the JPY principal and interest, producing a loss of SGD 503,946.

</details>

### 154. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §4.8] Which of the following is NOT one of the leading factors explaining exchange rate fluctuations?

- a. Inflation rates
- b. Political stability
- c. The level of the stock market index
- d. Interest rates

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The level of the stock market index

**Rationale:** The leading factors are interest rates, inflation rates, political stability, foreign currency reserves, trade account balances and terms of trade, and government or public debt. The stock market index level is not among them.

</details>

## Chapter 5 — Company Analysis and Understanding Financial Statements

### 155. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.1] Why is company analysis relevant beyond equity analysis?

- a. Because bonds are legally equities
- b. Because regulators require it
- c. Because equities are the only asset class
- d. Because it is also relevant for credit analysis of bond investments and other securities whose payments depend on the issuer's viability

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Because it is also relevant for credit analysis of bond investments and other securities whose payments depend on the issuer's viability

**Rationale:** Company analysis does not only relate to equity analysis, but is also relevant for credit analysis of bond investments and other securities whose cashflow payments are dependent on the ongoing viability of the issuing company.

</details>

### 156. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.1] Why does the guide argue that qualitative factors matter more than ratios?

- a. Because qualitative factors drive and precede the quantitative outcomes
- b. Because ratios cannot be calculated for most companies
- c. Because regulators forbid ratio analysis
- d. Because ratios are always wrong

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because qualitative factors drive and precede the quantitative outcomes

**Rationale:** It is more important to focus on the qualitative factors and conditions that allow for the company to be successful than to simply rely on ratios and numbers, as these qualitative factors drive and precede the quantitative outcomes.

</details>

### 157. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.2] Which competitive advantage is illustrated by De Beers, which still controls about 30–35% of world diamond supply?

- a. Superior brand differentiation
- b. Control over key resource supply
- c. Cost efficiency
- d. Control of distribution channels

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Control over key resource supply

**Rationale:** Companies that have substantial control over the raw materials and resources required for production have an advantage. Despite giving up its almost monopolistic control of diamond supply decades ago, De Beers continues to control about 30–35% of the world's diamond supply.

</details>

### 158. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.2] How many core businesses do diversified companies with shared costs typically hold?

- a. More than 10
- b. 1–2
- c. 3–4
- d. 5–6

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 3–4

**Rationale:** Such companies will typically have 3–4 core businesses where they have a competitive advantage and lead in market share. Resources are mainly focused on their core businesses, with a smaller portion allocated to developing new businesses.

</details>

### 159. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.3.2] Which accounting standard applies to listed companies in Singapore?

- a. Neither
- b. Both, at the company's choice
- c. US GAAP
- d. IFRS

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. IFRS

**Rationale:** Listed US companies follow US GAAP. All listed European companies follow IFRS, as does Singapore. As there are variations between US GAAP and IFRS, adjustments and restatements must be made to compare companies across markets.

</details>

### 160. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.3.2] What is a qualified audit report?

- a. A report issued when the auditors are not completely satisfied with how the financial statements are prepared
- b. A report issued only for banks
- c. A report confirming the accounts are perfect
- d. A report required only for IPOs

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. A report issued when the auditors are not completely satisfied with how the financial statements are prepared

**Rationale:** The investment community expects auditors to make the necessary qualifying comments if financial statements are not well-prepared. Qualified reports are provided when the auditors are not completely satisfied with how the company's financial statements are prepared.

</details>

### 161. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.4] Which balance sheet equation is used in British practice?

- a. Assets = Liabilities + Shareholders' Equity
- b. Assets − Liabilities = Shareholders' Equity
- c. Assets + Liabilities = Shareholders' Equity
- d. Assets = Shareholders' Equity − Liabilities

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Assets − Liabilities = Shareholders' Equity

**Rationale:** For British companies, the practice is to present assets net of liabilities, that is net assets on one side, which then equates to shareholders' equity on the other side. US companies present assets on one side with liabilities and shareholders' equity summing to the same total.

</details>

### 162. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.4] What does a common size balance sheet express each item as a percentage of?

- a. Total liabilities
- b. Sales revenue
- c. Total assets
- d. Shareholders' equity

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Total assets

**Rationale:** The items on the common size statement are reflected as a percentage of total assets, which is 100%. The common size statement allows for an easier comparison of balance sheet items across different companies.

</details>

### 163. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.5.1] What is the principal disadvantage of holding a perpetually large cash balance?

- a. It reduces the current ratio
- b. It increases leverage
- c. It increases the tax liability
- d. It represents underutilised assets and sacrifices potential returns

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. It represents underutilised assets and sacrifices potential returns

**Rationale:** When not invested in the business, cash balances represent underutilized assets. Keeping a perpetually large cash balance would also mean sacrificing potential returns that could have been generated from investing in other financial assets.

</details>

### 164. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.5.2] Which of the following is a warning signal for a retailer?

- a. A sudden build-up in inventories amid falling sales
- b. A steady inventory level with rising sales
- c. A fall in inventory with rising sales
- d. An increase in finished goods ahead of expected demand

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. A sudden build-up in inventories amid falling sales

**Rationale:** When carrying out quarterly reviews of inventory balances for retailers, one warning signal to watch for is the sudden build-up in inventories amidst falling sales numbers. An increase in finished goods in anticipation of greater demand is a positive reason.

</details>

### 165. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.5.3] Approximately what proportion of an airline's assets are accounts receivables, and why?

- a. About 50%, because airlines sell on credit
- b. Less than 5%, because airline ticket sales are largely cash-based
- c. About 25%, because of travel agent credit
- d. About 75%, because of corporate accounts

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Less than 5%, because airline ticket sales are largely cash-based

**Rationale:** Airline passenger ticket sales are largely a cash-based business, usually 95% or more, hence accounts receivables usually make up less than 5% of an airline's assets.

</details>

### 166. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.5.5] Why was Apple's plant, property and equipment only 7–8% of overall assets in its 2015/16 accounts?

- a. Because Apple leases all its equipment
- b. Because Apple sold its factories
- c. Because Apple outsourced manufacturing to subcontractors while retaining software, intellectual property and research
- d. Because Apple is a service company

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Because Apple outsourced manufacturing to subcontractors while retaining software, intellectual property and research

**Rationale:** Some companies have chosen to pass on some of the obsolescence risk to subcontractors. In Apple's 2015/16 accounts, PPE accounts for only 7–8% of overall assets.

</details>

### 167. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.5.6] What proportion of total assets do property holdings typically represent for Asian property companies, including holdings through associates and joint ventures?

- a. 95–100%
- b. 20–30%
- c. 40–50%
- d. 70–85%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. 70–85%

**Rationale:** Property holdings form the bulk of the value of these companies, typically 55–70%, and once holdings through associates and joint ventures are factored in, they account for 70–85% of overall asset value.

</details>

### 168. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.5.7] Why does a goodwill write-down improve reported ROE in subsequent years?

- a. Because it reduces equity, the denominator, without any operational change
- b. Because it increases total assets
- c. Because it reduces the tax rate
- d. Because it increases net income

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because it reduces equity, the denominator, without any operational change

**Rationale:** A goodwill write-down is typically accompanied by a direct charge against equity reserves. As unproductive asset value is removed from total assets but nothing changes operationally, ROA and ROE in subsequent years are higher as a result of the lower denominators.

</details>

### 169. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.6.1] What happened to Southeast Asian companies that financed operations with USD-denominated Eurobonds during the 1997 crisis?

- a. Their debt fell in local currency terms
- b. Their USD borrowings quadrupled in local currency terms when the Rupiah and Baht fell more than 75%
- c. They were unaffected
- d. They refinanced at lower rates

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Their USD borrowings quadrupled in local currency terms when the Rupiah and Baht fell more than 75%

**Rationale:** When the Rupiah and the Baht fell by more than 75% during the Asian Currency Crisis, the USD Eurobond borrowings suddenly quadrupled in value in local currency terms, and several companies had to declare bankruptcy.

</details>

### 170. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.6.2] What does a build-up of accounts payables each quarter accompanied by falling sales suggest?

- a. An increase in credit sales
- b. Strong bargaining power with suppliers
- c. The company may be facing difficulty in making payments
- d. Improved operating cash flow

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The company may be facing difficulty in making payments

**Rationale:** If there are signs in the quarterly statements that a company is stretching payables as indicated by a build-up of payables, it could suggest that the company is trying to increase liquidity. A negative scenario could be that the company is facing difficulty in making payments, signalled by a build-up of payables each quarter accompanied by falling sales.

</details>

### 171. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.7.3] How are hidden reserves created in a bank?

- a. By issuing new shares
- b. By paying high dividends
- c. By overstating loan losses
- d. By making large provisions in a downturn and being slow to reverse them when customers repay

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. By making large provisions in a downturn and being slow to reverse them when customers repay

**Rationale:** Banks make huge provisions for loan losses during economic downturns, but the actual losses may not materialize. When economies recover and customers start repaying, some banks may be slow in reversing their provisioning. This creates hidden reserves.

</details>

### 172. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §5.8] A company's revenue falls 76% while operating expenses fall only 60%. What does this illustrate?

- a. That costs are more inelastic than revenue, so a revenue fall produces an operating loss
- b. That the company is overstaffed
- c. That margins improve in a downturn
- d. That costs are more elastic than revenue

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. That costs are more inelastic than revenue, so a revenue fall produces an operating loss

**Rationale:** Given the more inelastic nature of costs, the cuts to operating expenses were not enough to offset the loss in revenue. This is exactly the SIA case, where a S$9.5 billion cost cut did not offset a S$12 billion revenue loss, producing an operating loss of about S$2.5 billion.

</details>

### 173. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.8.1] Into which two components should sales revenue growth be broken down?

- a. Domestic and international sales
- b. Unit sales growth and pricing changes
- c. Recurring and non-recurring revenue
- d. Cash and credit sales

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Unit sales growth and pricing changes

**Rationale:** When analysing the key drivers of sales growth, we should break the sales revenue component into two parts: unit sales growth and pricing changes. Whenever a company raises either of the two, sales revenue will increase.

</details>

### 174. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.8.3] How can depreciation expense be used to cosmetically improve reported profit?

- a. By increasing the asset base
- b. By shortening the depreciation period
- c. By stretching depreciation over more years, which lowers the annual charge without changing cash flow
- d. By capitalising all costs

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. By stretching depreciation over more years, which lowers the annual charge without changing cash flow

**Rationale:** When a company is not doing well, one cosmetic approach is to change its accounting practice by stretching depreciation expense over more years. A taxi fleet operator depreciating taxis over 10 years instead of 5 dramatically lowers depreciation expense and improves reported net profit, while nothing changes in cash flow.

</details>

### 175. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.9] Why is the cash flow statement particularly important for capital-intensive industries?

- a. Because it replaces the income statement
- b. Because it is audited
- c. Because it shows dividends paid
- d. Because net earnings may not accurately show the ability to support liabilities, and the statement adds back depreciation and other non-cash items

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Because net earnings may not accurately show the ability to support liabilities, and the statement adds back depreciation and other non-cash items

**Rationale:** For capital-intensive industries, a company's net earnings may not provide an accurate picture about its ability to support short-term liabilities and borrowings. The cash flow statement adds back depreciation and other non-cash items to assess the ability of the company to support financing and investing activities.

</details>

### 176. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.9] What is the bondholder's central concern in the cash flow statement?

- a. Consistently negative cash flow from operations
- b. High capital expenditure
- c. Large share buybacks
- d. Rising dividends

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Consistently negative cash flow from operations

**Rationale:** For most investors, especially bond holders, a major concern is when the cashflows from operations are consistently negative. A poor overall net cashflow position can be temporarily masked by the selling of assets and businesses.

</details>

### 177. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.10] Which share count is used to calculate book value per share?

- a. The weighted average number of shares
- b. The total number of shares outstanding at the financial year-end
- c. The fully diluted number of shares
- d. The shares outstanding at the ex-dividend date

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. The total number of shares outstanding at the financial year-end

**Rationale:** For per-share computations using numbers on the balance sheet, we use the number of outstanding shares at the end of the financial year, because the balance sheet reflects a snapshot of the financial position.

</details>

### 178. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.10] Which share count is used to calculate dividend per share?

- a. The fully diluted number of shares
- b. The weighted average number of shares
- c. The total number of shares outstanding as at the ex-dividend date
- d. The year-end number of shares

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The total number of shares outstanding as at the ex-dividend date

**Rationale:** When we consider dividends paid to shareholders, we use the total number of outstanding shares as at the ex-dividend date in order to calculate DPS.

</details>

### 179. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §5.11.1] A company has current assets of $600,000 including inventory of $250,000, and current liabilities of $300,000. What is the quick ratio?

- a. 0.83
- b. 1.50
- c. 2.00
- d. 1.17

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. 1.17

**Rationale:** The quick ratio excludes inventory: (600,000 − 250,000) / 300,000 = 350,000 / 300,000 = 1.17. The current ratio would be 2.00.

</details>

### 180. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.11.1] Why might a supermarket have a quick ratio as low as 0.2 without a liquidity problem?

- a. Because its current assets are fast-moving consumer goods financed by short-term supplier credit
- b. Because it holds no inventory
- c. Because it is loss-making
- d. Because it has no current liabilities

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because its current assets are fast-moving consumer goods financed by short-term supplier credit

**Rationale:** The quick ratio of a supermarket may be as low as 0.2 because it has substantial inventory, but it may not be a liquidity concern because a supermarket's current assets are fast-moving consumer goods that are financed by short-term supplier credit.

</details>

### 181. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.11.1] What is window dressing in the context of liquidity ratios?

- a. Reporting results more frequently
- b. Collecting receivables early, reducing inventory below normal and delaying purchases near year-end to flatter the ratios
- c. Increasing the dividend
- d. Issuing new shares

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Collecting receivables early, reducing inventory below normal and delaying purchases near year-end to flatter the ratios

**Rationale:** Current and quick ratios at financial reporting dates are susceptible to manipulation, otherwise known as window dressing. Toward the financial year-end, companies can collect receivables earlier, reduce inventory below normal levels and delay purchases.

</details>

### 182. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.11.1] A company has a current ratio of 1.5. It then increases both current assets and current liabilities by an equal dollar amount. What happens to the current ratio?

- a. It becomes negative
- b. It rises
- c. It falls
- d. It is unchanged

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. It falls

**Rationale:** An equal dollar increase in both current assets and current liabilities does not leave the current ratio unchanged. It leads to a lower ratio if it was previously more than 1, and to a higher ratio if it was previously less than 1.

</details>

### 183. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.11.2] Which ratio is of primary interest to bondholders?

- a. Inventory turnover
- b. Dividend yield
- c. Gross profit margin
- d. Leverage ratios, because their main risk is default on principal and interest

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Leverage ratios, because their main risk is default on principal and interest

**Rationale:** Leverage ratios help determine a firm's amount of financial risk and measure its capacity to meet principal and interest repayments. These ratios are of primary interest for bondholders, whose main risk is default on principal and interest payments.

</details>

### 184. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.11.2] How are debts carried on the balance sheet for the purpose of the debt-to-total assets ratio?

- a. At face value, not revalued
- b. At the present value of future cash flows
- c. At the original issue discount
- d. At market value

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. At face value, not revalued

**Rationale:** Note that debts are carried at their face values on the balance sheet and are not revalued. This is why foreign currency-denominated debt must be checked separately, because appreciation of that currency makes the market value of the debt much larger.

</details>

### 185. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.11.3] A firm has an interest coverage ratio of −1 and another has −3, both having made the same trading loss. Which has higher financial risk?

- a. The firm with −3
- b. The firm with −1, because it has higher financing charges
- c. They are equal
- d. It cannot be determined

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. The firm with −1, because it has higher financing charges

**Rationale:** The size of a negative coverage ratio is also determined by the absolute amount of interest charges. A firm with a ratio of −1 does not necessarily indicate lower financial risk than a firm with −3: both are incurring trading losses, but the lower ratio of −1 is due to higher financing charges, which is not a good thing.

</details>

### 186. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.11.4] Why do efficiency ratios use average balances in the denominator?

- a. Because year-end balances are unavailable
- b. Because averages are easier to compute
- c. Because the numerator covers a period, so the denominator should be an average of the opening and closing balances
- d. Because accounting standards require it

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Because the numerator covers a period, so the denominator should be an average of the opening and closing balances

**Rationale:** An average is calculated for the denominator because the numerator is measured over the period and, so, the denominator should be an average of the two year-end balances.

</details>

### 187. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §5.11.4] Sales are $3,816 and average total assets are $35,647. What is the total asset turnover?

- a. 0.11
- b. 0.50
- c. 0.58
- d. 9.34

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. 0.11

**Rationale:** Total asset turnover = Sales / Average total assets = 3,816 / 35,647 = 0.107, approximately 0.11. This is the SIA FY2020/2021 figure, depressed by the pandemic.

</details>

### 188. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.11.4] What range of total asset turnover is typical across industries?

- a. Always between 0.5 and 1.5
- b. Always above 5
- c. Always below 1
- d. From about 1 for capital-intensive industries to over 10 for some retailing operations

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. From about 1 for capital-intensive industries to over 10 for some retailing operations

**Rationale:** Total assets turnover ratios range from 1 for capital-intensive industries to over 10 for some retailing operations. This ratio should be compared with that of other firms in the industry because it varies substantially across industries.

</details>

### 189. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.11.5] What does the variability of the operating profit margin over time indicate?

- a. The firm's business risk
- b. The firm's liquidity
- c. The firm's dividend policy
- d. The firm's financial risk

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. The firm's business risk

**Rationale:** The variability of the operating profit margin over time is an indicator of the firm's business risk. Business risk is the uncertainty of income that is caused by the firm's industry.

</details>

### 190. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.11.5] Why is ROA normally expressed before interest?

- a. Because interest is not an expense
- b. To separate operating decisions from financing decisions and enable comparison of companies with different capital structures
- c. Because interest is paid after tax
- d. Because it simplifies the calculation

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. To separate operating decisions from financing decisions and enable comparison of companies with different capital structures

**Rationale:** Profit before interest is used to separate operating decisions from financing decisions and to enable comparisons of efficiency for companies with different capital structures.

</details>

### 191. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.11.5] Why did IAG and United Airlines show high ROE in 2019 before the pandemic?

- a. Because they paid no dividends
- b. Because their earnings were exceptionally strong
- c. Because their equity base had been substantially reduced by losses and charges in prior years
- d. Because they had no debt

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Because their equity base had been substantially reduced by losses and charges in prior years

**Rationale:** IAG charged losses of EUR0.9 billion to reserves in 2012, EUR2 billion in 2013 and EUR1.2 billion in 2014. United Airlines charged its losses to retained earnings in 2011 and 2012. Subsequent years of ROE look high due to the reduction of the value of shareholders' equity with losses in prior years.

</details>

### 192. **[Multiple response]** [CM-EIP §5.11.6] Which of the following are signs of strong and high-quality financial statements? (Select all that apply)

- a. Limited or lower use of debt relative to the industry
- b. Assets generally carried below market values
- c. Absence of off-balance sheet activities
- d. Aggressive revenue recognition that reflects higher sales

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A, B, C

**Rationale:** Signs of quality include limited or lower use of debt, higher liquidity, assets carried below market values, a preference not to reflect intangible assets, funds set aside for replacement, conservative revenue recognition, absence of off-balance-sheet activities, discipline in core business, realistic bad-debt recognition and steady sustainable growth. Aggressive revenue recognition is the opposite of quality.

</details>

### 193. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.11.7] Why must trend analysis cover at least one full business cycle?

- a. Because cycles are always five years long
- b. Because regulators require it
- c. Because accounting standards require five years of data
- d. Because otherwise the analysis could mislead, showing a company doing well when it is simply at the crest of a cycle about to turn down

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Because otherwise the analysis could mislead, showing a company doing well when it is simply at the crest of a cycle about to turn down

**Rationale:** Business cycles affect the performance of all companies. To have a better understanding, it is necessary to cover at least one cycle and track overall performance over a cycle. Otherwise the analysis could be misleading and indicate that a company is doing well when it is simply at the crest of a cycle that is about to turn down.

</details>

### 194. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §5.11.8] Which of the following is a limitation of ratio analysis?

- a. Companies in emerging sectors with little comparable or reliable financial information do not lend themselves easily to ratio analysis
- b. Ratios are always manipulated
- c. Ratios ignore the balance sheet
- d. Ratios cannot be calculated for listed companies

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Companies in emerging sectors with little comparable or reliable financial information do not lend themselves easily to ratio analysis

**Rationale:** Companies in emerging sectors, such as emerging technology or business models, do not lend themselves easily to ratio or financial analysis. The lack of comparable and reliable financial information greatly limits ratio analysis.

</details>

## Chapter 6 — Equity Securities

### 195. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.2.1] Why might the owners of a successful private company list it on an exchange?

- a. To avoid all regulation
- b. To share the risk of ownership, monetise part of the value and redeploy funds
- c. To reduce the number of shareholders
- d. To eliminate the need for management

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. To share the risk of ownership, monetise part of the value and redeploy funds

**Rationale:** Original owners may want to share their risk of ownership, realise and monetise the value of the company, and redeploy funds to other businesses for even greater gains. Through a well-planned IPO, ownership control can still be retained while the business value is partially monetised.

</details>

### 196. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.2.2] What is the maximum number of votes a multiple-vote share may carry under SGX dual class listing rules?

- a. 2
- b. 5
- c. 10
- d. Unlimited

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 10

**Rationale:** To prevent founder entrenchment, each multiple vote share can only carry a maximum of 10 votes. Extra votes also cannot be exercised on important matters where there may be conflicts of interest between founders and minority shareholders.

</details>

### 197. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.2.2] How many Class B shares of Berkshire Hathaway can one Class A share be converted into?

- a. 100
- b. 500
- c. 1,500
- d. 2,000

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 1,500

**Rationale:** Each share of Class A common stock is convertible, at the option of the holder, into 1,500 shares of Class B common stock. Class B shares are non-convertible and have 1/200th the voting power of Class A shares.

</details>

### 198. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.2.3] Which right do ordinary shareholders NOT have?

- a. A residual claim on remaining assets
- b. The right to vote on major policy decisions
- c. The right to be informed of major events
- d. A guaranteed right to dividends

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. A guaranteed right to dividends

**Rationale:** Ordinary share dividends are decided at the discretion of management and depend on the performance of the company. Common shareholders do not have a right to dividend payouts and such pay-outs are not guaranteed.

</details>

### 199. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.2.4] Why do non-callable preference shares carry a lower rate of return than callable ones?

- a. Because the issuer has no option to redeem them
- b. Because they rank above bonds
- c. Because they have a shorter life
- d. Because they have voting rights

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because the issuer has no option to redeem them

**Rationale:** Non-callable preference shares carry a lower rate of return than the callable ones because the issuer has no option to redeem the shares.

</details>

### 200. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.2.4] What happens if an issuer fails to pay a preference dividend?

- a. It constitutes default
- b. It does not constitute default, unlike a missed bond coupon
- c. The shares convert to ordinary shares
- d. The issuer must be liquidated

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. It does not constitute default, unlike a missed bond coupon

**Rationale:** Unlike a bond, the issuer can decide not to pay the preference dividend if earnings are insufficient and failure to pay the specified preference dividend does not result in a default.

</details>

### 201. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.2.5] A company has 300 million shares at $2.00 and announces a 1-for-3 rights issue at $1.90. What is the theoretical ex-rights price?

- a. $1.90
- b. $1.50
- c. $1.975
- d. $2.00

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. $1.975

**Rationale:** Before the issue, market capitalisation is 300m × $2.00 = $600m. After a 1-for-3 issue there are 400m shares and new capital of 100m × $1.90 = $190m, so the new market capitalisation is $790m. Theoretical ex-rights price = $790m / 400m = $1.975.

</details>

### 202. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.2.5] Using the same company, what is the theoretical ex-bonus price for a 1-for-3 bonus issue?

- a. $1.975
- b. $2.00
- c. $1.90
- d. $1.50

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. $1.50

**Rationale:** A bonus issue raises no capital, so the market capitalisation stays at $600m while the share count rises to 400m. Theoretical ex-bonus price = $600m / 400m = $1.50, sharply lower than the rights-issue price because no new capital is injected.

</details>

### 203. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.2.5] A company has 300 million shares for 9 months and 400 million for 3 months after a rights issue. What is the weighted-average number of shares?

- a. 325m
- b. 350m
- c. 400m
- d. 300m

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. 325m

**Rationale:** Weighted-average shares = (300m × 9 + 400m × 3) / 12 = (2,700m + 1,200m) / 12 = 3,900m / 12 = 325m.

</details>

### 204. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.2.5] What is the maximum pro-rata renounceable rights issue a company may now make, as a proportion of share capital?

- a. 25%
- b. 50%
- c. 75%
- d. 100%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. 100%

**Rationale:** Companies are now allowed to issue pro-rata renounceable rights shares of up to 100% of share capital, which is an increase from the previous limit of 50%.

</details>

### 205. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.2.5] Why did Citigroup carry out a 1-for-10 reverse stock split in 2011?

- a. To raise new capital
- b. Because institutional investors are discouraged from holding penny stocks priced below $5
- c. To reduce the number of shareholders
- d. To increase its market capitalisation

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Because institutional investors are discouraged from holding penny stocks priced below $5

**Rationale:** Certain institutional investors are governed by rules which discourage them from investing in penny stocks. The SEC defines penny stocks as stocks priced below $5 per share, which made it difficult for institutional investors to own Citigroup shares. The reverse split took the shares from $4.52 to $44.16.

</details>

### 206. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.3.1] Which of the following is a characteristic of blue-chip companies?

- a. No dividend payments
- b. High earnings growth in the current period
- c. A long and consistent record of earnings, allowing steady dividends through good and bad years
- d. High beta and high volatility

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. A long and consistent record of earnings, allowing steady dividends through good and bad years

**Rationale:** As these companies have a long and consistent record of earnings, they generally can pay steady dividends over a long period of time during both good and bad years. Growth would have slowed over time due to their sheer size.

</details>

### 207. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.3.2] What growth rate is typically meant by a high-growth company?

- a. Above 50% for one year
- b. Above 5% compounded annually
- c. Above 10% compounded annually
- d. Above 20% compounded annually over several years

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Above 20% compounded annually over several years

**Rationale:** When the investment community talks about growth companies, they are usually referring to companies that can generate high double-digit sales and earnings growth over a certain number of years, for example more than 20% growth compounded annually over several years.

</details>

### 208. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.3.3] If the fixed deposit rate is 3%, at what dividend yield are dependable high-yield stocks likely to find price support?

- a. 4–5%
- b. 8–10%
- c. Above 15%
- d. 1–2%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. 4–5%

**Rationale:** If the fixed deposit rate is 3% for annual deposits, stocks with dependable dividend payouts are unlikely to fall below a dividend yield of 4–5% before tax. The price is supported at the point where dividend/price = 4%.

</details>

### 209. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.3.4] Which sectors are described as cyclical in Singapore and Hong Kong?

- a. Technology and electronics
- b. Property and banking
- c. Utilities and food
- d. Healthcare and pharmaceuticals

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Property and banking

**Rationale:** For Singapore and Hong Kong, the cyclical stocks will also come from the property and banking sectors because they are usually amongst the first sectors to be affected in an economic downturn. For Taiwan and South Korea, technology and electronic manufacturing are cyclical.

</details>

### 210. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.3.5] Which of the following is a typical defensive stock?

- a. An automobile manufacturer
- b. A semiconductor company
- c. A food and beverage company such as Nestlé
- d. A property developer

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. A food and beverage company such as Nestlé

**Rationale:** Defensive companies tend to be those that provide consumers with necessities, such as pharmaceuticals, food, utilities and transportation. Shares of companies like Nestlé and Coca-Cola would be typical defensive stocks.

</details>

### 211. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.4.1] A value-weighted index has a base market value of 64,000,000 set at an index level of 100. The current market value is 67,000,000. What is the new index value?

- a. 100
- b. 104.69
- c. 105
- d. 107

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 105

**Rationale:** New index value = (Current market value / Base value) × Beginning index value = (67,000,000 / 64,000,000) × 100 = 1.05 × 100 = 105.

</details>

### 212. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.4.1] Why does a value-weighted index adjust automatically for bonus and rights issues?

- a. Because the index excludes such companies
- b. Because the index is rebalanced annually
- c. Because the divisor is recalculated daily
- d. Because these corporate actions typically do not affect market capitalisation, as a price fall is offset by more shares

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Because these corporate actions typically do not affect market capitalisation, as a price fall is offset by more shares

**Rationale:** An advantage of the value-weighted index is that it automatically adjusts for bonus and rights issues, as well as other capital changes, because these corporate actions typically do not affect market capitalisation: a decrease in share price is offset by an increase in the number of shares outstanding.

</details>

### 213. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.4.2] Why has the Dow Jones Industrial Average been criticised?

- a. Because it uses only 30 non-randomly selected blue-chip shares that are not representative of the NYSE
- b. Because it includes too many technology companies
- c. Because it is rebalanced monthly
- d. Because it is value-weighted

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because it uses only 30 non-randomly selected blue-chip shares that are not representative of the NYSE

**Rationale:** The DJIA has been criticized because it uses only 30 non-randomly selected blue-chip shares, which are not considered to be representative of the shares listed on the New York Stock Exchange.

</details>

### 214. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.4.3] Three shares change by factors of 1.04, 1.03 and 1.05. What is the equal-weighted geometric average change?

- a. 1.060
- b. 1.040
- c. 1.0400
- d. 1.120

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 1.040

**Rationale:** Geometric average = (1.04 × 1.03 × 1.05)^(1/3) = (1.12476)^(1/3) = 1.040. The index value on day 2 equals the day 1 value multiplied by 1.040.

</details>

### 215. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.5] Which of the following is an absolute valuation approach?

- a. Dividend yield
- b. Price-to-earnings ratio
- c. Discounted cash flow
- d. Price-to-book value

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Discounted cash flow

**Rationale:** There are essentially three ways of valuing a listed company: net asset valuation, discounting of future cash flows, which is also called the absolute valuation approach, and relative valuation, where comparisons are made against historical trends and other companies in the industry.

</details>

### 216. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.6.1] When does book value often represent the floor value of a company?

- a. When the share price is at a record high
- b. When the company has large intangible assets
- c. When the company is growing rapidly
- d. During bad times, if shareholders' equity has not been wiped out by losses

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. During bad times, if shareholders' equity has not been wiped out by losses

**Rationale:** The book value also represents the accumulation of retained earnings over the years. During bad times, if shareholder's equity has not been wiped out by losses, the book value often represents the floor value.

</details>

### 217. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.6.3] When revising asset values to market for a revised net asset value, what else must be checked?

- a. Whether debt and other liabilities also need revising to market value
- b. Whether the dividend has been declared
- c. Whether the company has changed auditors
- d. Whether the share count has changed

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Whether debt and other liabilities also need revising to market value

**Rationale:** When we revise the asset values to their respective market values, we should also check whether there is a need to revise debt and other liabilities to market value as well. For example, the market value of USD-denominated debt may have appreciated in the interim.

</details>

### 218. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.7] What is the examination expectation for the DCF approach in CM-EIP?

- a. Candidates must compute and derive fair value
- b. Candidates must explain the process and the variables used, but will not be asked to compute fair value
- c. Candidates must memorise the terminal value formula only
- d. DCF is not examinable

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Candidates must explain the process and the variables used, but will not be asked to compute fair value

**Rationale:** For the CM-EIP examination, investment representatives are only expected to know how to explain the process of valuing stocks and the variables used but will not be asked to compute and derive the fair value.

</details>

### 219. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.7.1] Which DCF variant does the CM-EIP module focus on?

- a. Replacement cost model
- b. Dividend discount model
- c. Free cash flow to firm discounted at the WACC
- d. Residual income model

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Free cash flow to firm discounted at the WACC

**Rationale:** For this module, we focus on the Free Cash Flow to Firm approach to DCF valuation, using the Weighted Average Cost of Capital as the discount rate.

</details>

### 220. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.7.2] The risk-free rate is 2.33%, the market return is 9.28% and beta is 0.68. What is the cost of equity by CAPM?

- a. 6.31%
- b. 8.65%
- c. 9.28%
- d. 7.056%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. 7.056%

**Rationale:** R = Rf + β(Rm − Rf) = 2.33 + 0.68(9.28 − 2.33) = 2.33 + 0.68(6.95) = 2.33 + 4.726 = 7.056%.

</details>

### 221. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.7.2] Debt is 30.414, equity is 224.17, the after-tax cost of debt is 1.08% and the cost of equity is 7%. What is the WACC?

- a. 6.29%
- b. 5.50%
- c. 7.00%
- d. 6.80%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. 6.29%

**Rationale:** Total capital = 30.414 + 224.17 = 254.584. WACC = 0.0108 × (30.414/254.584) + 0.07 × (224.17/254.584) = 0.0108 × 0.1195 + 0.07 × 0.8805 = 0.00129 + 0.06164 = 0.0629 = 6.29%.

</details>

### 222. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.7.4] How is the terminal value calculated in the FCFF approach?

- a. The year-10 FCFF multiplied by 10
- b. The year-11 FCFF divided by (WACC − terminal growth rate)
- c. The year-10 FCFF divided by the WACC
- d. The sum of all projected FCFFs

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. The year-11 FCFF divided by (WACC − terminal growth rate)

**Rationale:** The TV is calculated using the estimated FCFF in the 11th year, divided by (WACC − gc), where gc refers to the terminal growth rate, the rate at which the company's FCFF are expected to grow indefinitely. This lump sum is then discounted back over 10 years.

</details>

### 223. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.7.4] What constraint must hold for the terminal value calculation to be valid?

- a. The WACC must equal the risk-free rate
- b. The growth rate must exceed the WACC
- c. The discount rate must be higher than the growth rate
- d. The growth rate must be zero

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The discount rate must be higher than the growth rate

**Rationale:** The calculation of terminal value always requires the discount rate to be higher than the growth rate. If the growth rate exceeded the discount rate the terminal value would be negative or infinite.

</details>

### 224. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.7.5] Enterprise value is 260,391, debt is 30,514 and preference shares are 1,038. With 2,557.6 million shares, what is the intrinsic share value?

- a. USD 101.80
- b. USD 87.65
- c. USD 91.20
- d. USD 89.47

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. USD 89.47

**Rationale:** Estimated market value of share capital = 260,391 − 30,514 − 1,038 = 228,839. Intrinsic share value = 228,839 / 2,557.6 = USD 89.47. Against a price of USD 87.65 this implies about 2% upside.

</details>

### 225. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.7.6] What is the main criticism of the DCF model?

- a. It depends heavily on the terminal value, which can vary significantly with different assumptions
- b. It cannot be applied to listed companies
- c. It ignores the time value of money
- d. It ignores cash flows

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. It depends heavily on the terminal value, which can vary significantly with different assumptions

**Rationale:** The DCF model is still heavily dependent on the calculation of the terminal value, which can vary significantly if different assumptions are made. The process of determining fair value is not an exact science but only serves to provide an estimate based on certain assumptions.

</details>

### 226. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.8.1] What is the difference between a trailing and a leading P/E ratio?

- a. Trailing uses forecast earnings; leading uses historical earnings
- b. Trailing uses the most recent four quarters of EPS; leading uses consensus forecast earnings for the next year
- c. Trailing is used for bonds; leading for equities
- d. There is no difference

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Trailing uses the most recent four quarters of EPS; leading uses consensus forecast earnings for the next year

**Rationale:** A trailing P/E, also known as the current P/E, is the current market price divided by the most recent four quarters of EPS. A leading or prospective P/E divides the current market price by the consensus forecasted earnings for the next year.

</details>

### 227. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.8.1] A stock trades at USD 34.68 with FY2016 EPS of USD 2.72. What is the trailing P/E?

- a. 13.5x
- b. 12.1x
- c. 12.8x
- d. 11.7x

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 12.8x

**Rationale:** Trailing P/E = 34.68 / 2.72 = 12.8x. The prospective P/E for 2017 using forecast EPS of 2.86 would be 12.1x, and for 2018 using 2.97 would be 11.7x.

</details>

### 228. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.8.1] Which factor justifies a lower P/E multiple?

- a. A dominant market position
- b. Strong brand value
- c. High earnings growth
- d. Low transparency and predictability of earnings

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Low transparency and predictability of earnings

**Rationale:** When the risks are high, and transparency and predictability of earnings is low, even with high earnings growth, the lower predictability increases risks for investors; hence a lower P/E multiple is justified.

</details>

### 229. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.8.2] A stock has a prospective P/E of 44.5 and expected annual EPS growth of 28%. What is its PEG ratio?

- a. 1.6
- b. 0.63
- c. 2.8
- d. 1.59

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. 1.6

**Rationale:** PEG = P/E / % growth in EPS = 44.5 / 28 = 1.59, approximately 1.6. This is Alibaba's PEG, among the lowest despite having the highest prospective P/E, reflecting its high projected growth.

</details>

### 230. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.8.2] What does a PEG ratio below 1 indicate?

- a. The share is overvalued
- b. The share is undervalued, though such stocks are typically hard to find and may carry higher risk
- c. The company is loss-making
- d. The P/E is meaningless

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. The share is undervalued, though such stocks are typically hard to find and may carry higher risk

**Rationale:** A low PEG ratio indicates an undervalued share. As a rule of thumb, a PEG ratio below 1 would be attractive but typically very hard to find. Such stocks could be relatively new and therefore not actively covered and may also carry a higher level of risk.

</details>

### 231. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.8.3] For which type of company is the P/BV ratio best suited?

- a. A company with negative book value
- b. A software company with large intangible assets
- c. A bank, insurer or property company whose value is tied to tangible asset holdings
- d. A start-up with no assets

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. A bank, insurer or property company whose value is tied to tangible asset holdings

**Rationale:** The P/BV ratio is best suited for companies whose value is tied directly to their asset holdings. Book value is more important for investment companies, banks, insurance companies and property companies, as many of the assets held are reflected in the books and can be liquidated.

</details>

### 232. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.8.3] How do hidden reserves affect the calculated P/BV ratio?

- a. They have no effect
- b. They make the ratio meaningless
- c. They make book value overstated, so the true P/BV is higher
- d. They make book value understated, so the true P/BV is lower than calculated

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. They make book value understated, so the true P/BV is lower than calculated

**Rationale:** In such situations, the book value is understated, and the true P/BV ratio would be lower than what is calculated, making it more likely that the company is undervalued.

</details>

### 233. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.8.4] Why is the P/Sales ratio conceptually flawed?

- a. Because the numerator relates to shareholders only, while sales are generated from funds supplied by both shareholders and debtholders
- b. Because it ignores the share price
- c. Because it cannot be computed for listed companies
- d. Because sales are always negative

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because the numerator relates to shareholders only, while sales are generated from funds supplied by both shareholders and debtholders

**Rationale:** Conceptually, the P/Sales ratio is flawed. The numerator, price, relates to shareholders only, even though sales are generated from funds supplied by both shareholders and debtholders. Hence the numerator and denominator are inconsistent.

</details>

### 234. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.8.5] Market capitalisation is 18,000, debt is 400, preferred shares are 10, cash is 8,000 and operating cash flow is 2,210. What is the EV/CF ratio?

- a. 3.62
- b. 4.71
- c. 4.53
- d. 8.24

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 4.71

**Rationale:** EV/CF = (Market cap + Debt + Preferred − Cash) / Cash flow from operations = (18,000 + 400 + 10 − 8,000) / 2,210 = 10,410 / 2,210 = 4.71. A lower ratio is desirable.

</details>

### 235. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.8.6] Why is EBITDA preferred over operating cash flow in valuation?

- a. Because EBITDA is a shareholder concept
- b. Because it is easier to audit
- c. Because operating cash flow can swing widely with working capital changes that may not reflect business performance
- d. Because EBITDA includes interest

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Because operating cash flow can swing widely with working capital changes that may not reflect business performance

**Rationale:** EBITDA is preferred over operating cashflows because the latter can experience wide swings due to changes in working capital. Depending on the industry, working capital adjustments may not really reflect business performance.

</details>

### 236. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.8.6] Why is EV/EBITDA preferred over P/E?

- a. Because it ignores debt
- b. Because it uses forecast earnings
- c. Because it is easier to calculate
- d. Because it cannot be manipulated by changes in capital structure, allowing fair comparison of companies with different capital structures

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Because it cannot be manipulated by changes in capital structure, allowing fair comparison of companies with different capital structures

**Rationale:** Unlike the P/E, the EV/EBITDA ratio cannot be manipulated by changes in capital structure, which makes possible fair comparison of companies with different capital structures. EV considers the impact of debt and other capital on the total value of the firm while price relates only to equity value.

</details>

### 237. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.8.8] A share priced at $2.00 pays an annual gross dividend of $0.10. What is the dividend yield?

- a. 2.0%
- b. 5.0%
- c. 10.0%
- d. 20.0%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 5.0%

**Rationale:** Dividend yield = (Dividend per share / Market price per share) × 100 = (0.10 / 2.00) × 100 = 5%.

</details>

### 238. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.8.8] A company earns $0.40 per share and pays a dividend of $0.10 per share. What are the payout ratio and retention ratio?

- a. 25% payout, 75% retention
- b. 75% payout, 25% retention
- c. 40% payout, 60% retention
- d. 10% payout, 90% retention

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. 25% payout, 75% retention

**Rationale:** Dividend payout ratio = 0.10 / 0.40 = 25%. The retention ratio is 1 minus the dividend payout ratio = 75%, indicating the percentage of current earnings retained for reinvestment.

</details>

### 239. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.8.8] An analyst expects a 3-cent dividend and requires a 7.5% dividend yield. What is the implied share price?

- a. 31 cents
- b. 40 cents
- c. 22.5 cents
- d. 4 cents

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 40 cents

**Rationale:** Implied share price = Dividend / Required yield = 3 cents / 7.5% = 40 cents. This illustrates yield compression: as the price rises, the dividend yield falls.

</details>

### 240. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.8.8] Why can REITs be valued using a dividend discount model?

- a. Because they pay no dividends
- b. Because they have no debt
- c. Because they are required to pay out 90% of distributable income and their earnings are relatively predictable
- d. Because they are unlisted

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Because they are required to pay out 90% of distributable income and their earnings are relatively predictable

**Rationale:** Real estate investment trusts can be valued using a dividend discount model as they are required to pay out 90% of their distributable income and their earnings are relatively predictable.

</details>

### 241. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.8.9] Company A trades at 2.50 with EPS of 0.25 and net cash per share of 0.50. Company B trades at 3.00 with EPS of 0.30 and net debt. Both have a P/E of 10x. Which is undervalued?

- a. Company B, because it has higher EPS
- b. Neither, since both have the same P/E
- c. Company A, because it has a lower share price
- d. Company A, because after netting idle cash the effective P/E is 8x

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Company A, because after netting idle cash the effective P/E is 8x

**Rationale:** If Company A is acquired at 2.50 and the idle cash of $0.50 per share is netted out, the buyer is buying the company at a P/E of only 2.00/0.25 = 8x, which is lower than the 10x P/E of Company B. Company A is therefore undervalued.

</details>

### 242. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.8.10] What is break-up value in an acquisition?

- a. The expectation that the sum of the parts sold exceeds the target's value as an ongoing concern
- b. The liquidation value of the acquirer
- c. The book value of the target
- d. The value of the target as a going concern

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. The expectation that the sum of the parts sold exceeds the target's value as an ongoing concern

**Rationale:** There are also acquisitions in which the sum of the parts sold is expected to be higher than the target company's value as an ongoing concern, that is the company is currently undervalued because of how it is managed.

</details>

### 243. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.8.11] What is the yield gap?

- a. The difference between a bond's coupon and its yield
- b. The difference between the earnings yield and the bond yield
- c. The difference between the bid and offer price
- d. The difference between two companies' dividend yields

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. The difference between the earnings yield and the bond yield

**Rationale:** Earnings yields are usually compared against bond yields to assess whether share prices are over- or under-valued. The difference between these yields is referred to as the yield gap.

</details>

### 244. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.9] How does listing on SGX Catalist differ from the Mainboard?

- a. Catalist has no listing rules at all
- b. Catalist requires higher quantitative standards
- c. Catalist is supervised by a sponsor and no quantitative requirements are needed
- d. Catalist is only for foreign companies

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Catalist is supervised by a sponsor and no quantitative requirements are needed

**Rationale:** Listing on the SGX Mainboard requires the fulfilment of listing requirements spelt out by the exchange. However, listing on Catalist is supervised by a sponsor and no quantitative requirements are needed. Sponsors decide on the suitability of the candidate company.

</details>

### 245. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.9] What is the Greenshoe option?

- a. A claw-back of shares from retail investors
- b. A discount to the offer price
- c. A lock-up on pre-IPO shareholders
- d. An over-allotment option letting the issuer increase the offering, used by underwriters to stabilise the price

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. An over-allotment option letting the issuer increase the offering, used by underwriters to stabilise the price

**Rationale:** The over-allotment option, also known as the Greenshoe option, gives the issuer the right to increase its offering through an over-allotment of shares. IPO underwriters can use this option to stabilise the price of the IPO shares if they perform poorly on initial trading by buying back the additional shares sold from the market.

</details>

### 246. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.9] What is a claw-back provision in an IPO?

- a. The re-allocation of shares between institutional and retail investors
- b. A penalty for early selling
- c. A reduction in the offer price
- d. A right to buy back shares from the market

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. The re-allocation of shares between institutional and retail investors

**Rationale:** The issuer's right to claw back refers to the re-allocation of shares between institutional and retail investors. If institutional take-up is poorer than expected, the claw-back provision may be invoked and retail investors will get a higher allocation of a share that is probably avoided by institutional investors for good reason.

</details>

### 247. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.9] What document does a company lodge with MAS for a rights issue after listing, rather than a prospectus?

- a. A Product Highlights Sheet
- b. An Offer Information Statement
- c. A trust deed
- d. A circular to shareholders

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. An Offer Information Statement

**Rationale:** In a rights issue, the company will lodge an Offer Information Statement instead of a prospectus with the MAS.

</details>

### 248. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.10] Q-Star has sales of USD 7.65bn and the discounted EV/Sales multiple is 3.53. With debt of USD 11bn and 750m shares, what is the target price?

- a. USD 12.67
- b. USD 27.00
- c. USD 21.33
- d. USD 14.25

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. USD 21.33

**Rationale:** Firm value = 7.65 × 3.53 = USD 27.00bn. Market capitalisation = 27.00 − 11.00 = USD 16.00bn. Price = 16.00bn / 750m = USD 21.33.

</details>

### 249. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.11.1] What is the main difference between a non-voting depositary receipt and direct investment in the issuer's shares?

- a. NVDRs are not transferable
- b. NVDRs are only available to institutions
- c. NVDRs pay no dividends
- d. NVDRs carry no voting rights, so foreign investors cannot participate in corporate decisions

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. NVDRs carry no voting rights, so foreign investors cannot participate in corporate decisions

**Rationale:** The main difference between investing indirectly through an NVDR and directly in the issuer's shares is that NVDRs do not come with voting rights and thus do not allow foreign investors to participate in corporate decisions. This feature enables foreign investment in firms that would otherwise be prohibited.

</details>

### 250. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.11.2] Which level of ADR allows the issuer to raise new capital?

- a. Level 3
- b. All levels equally
- c. Level 1
- d. Level 2

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Level 3

**Rationale:** Level 1 ADRs trade in the traditional US OTC market and are not listed. Level 2 ADRs are listed on a US securities exchange but do not involve capital raising. Level 3 ADRs use a listed ADR programme to offer new shares to US investors in a capital-raising exercise.

</details>

### 251. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.11.3] Why might an ADR trade at a premium to its underlying shares?

- a. Because ADRs pay higher dividends
- b. Because of regulatory restrictions in the issuer's home market on foreign holdings of the shares
- c. Because ADRs are unregulated
- d. Because the depositary bank guarantees the price

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Because of regulatory restrictions in the issuer's home market on foreign holdings of the shares

**Rationale:** ADRs often trade at a premium to their underlying shares because of regulatory restrictions in the issuer's home market on foreign holdings of the shares.

</details>

### 252. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.13.1] What is the main practical difficulty with the general dividend discount model?

- a. It requires the share price
- b. It ignores the time value of money
- c. Investors must forecast and discount an infinite stream of dividends
- d. It cannot handle growth

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>
