**Answer:** C. Investors must forecast and discount an infinite stream of dividends

**Rationale:** The main difficulty with implementing the equation as a valuation model is that investors must forecast and discount an infinite stream of dividends, both insurmountable tasks. The model can be modified to account for a subsequent sale of the shares.

</details>

### 253. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.13.1] Dividends of $1.00, $1.05 and $1.10 are expected over three years, with a sale price of $10 at the end of year 3. At a 12% required return, what is the share value?

- a. $10.00
- b. $8.75
- c. $11.20
- d. $9.63

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. $9.63

**Rationale:** P0 = 1.00/1.12 + 1.05/1.12² + 1.10/1.12³ + 10.00/1.12³ = 0.8929 + 0.8371 + 0.7830 + 7.1178 = $9.63.

</details>

### 254. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.13.2] A company pays a dividend of $0.20, expected to grow at 5% a year. Investors require 12%. What is the estimated price?

- a. $3.00
- b. $2.86
- c. $4.00
- d. $1.67

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. $3.00

**Rationale:** P0 = D1 / (R − g) = (0.20 × 1.05) / (0.12 − 0.05) = 0.21 / 0.07 = $3.00. The current dividend must be compounded for one period because the formula uses the dividend expected one period from now.

</details>

### 255. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.13.2] Using the same company, if the required return rises from 12% to 13%, what is the new estimated price?

- a. $2.10
- b. $2.63
- c. $3.00
- d. $3.53

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. $2.63

**Rationale:** P0 = 0.21 / (0.13 − 0.05) = 0.21 / 0.08 = $2.63. A 1% change in R produces a large difference in estimated value, which is the model's central weakness.

</details>

### 256. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.13.3] A firm retains 60% of earnings and has an ROE of 15%. What is its sustainable growth rate?

- a. 6%
- b. 9%
- c. 15%
- d. 25%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 9%

**Rationale:** g = Retention Rate × ROE = 0.6 × 0.15 = 0.09 = 9%. A firm can increase its sustainable growth rate by increasing its retention rate or by increasing its ROE.

</details>

### 257. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.13.3] P&G has an ROE of 17.36% and a payout ratio of 0.70. What is its estimated growth rate?

- a. 7.00%
- b. 5.21%
- c. 12.15%
- d. 17.36%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 5.21%

**Rationale:** g = ROE × (1 − payout) = 0.1736 × (1 − 0.70) = 0.1736 × 0.30 = 0.0521 = 5.21%.

</details>

### 258. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.13.4] In the two-stage DDM, what does the second term represent?

- a. The current dividend
- b. The present value of dividends in the high-growth period
- c. The constant-growth value at the end of the abnormal growth period, discounted back to today
- d. The terminal dividend

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The constant-growth value at the end of the abnormal growth period, discounted back to today

**Rationale:** The second term is the constant-growth version, which divides the dividend expected at the end of period n+1 by the difference between R and g. Since the resulting figure is a present value at the end of period n, it must be discounted back to time period zero.

</details>

### 259. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.13.4] In the P&G two-stage example, the terminal value contributes 71.5797 of a total value of 84.15. What proportion of value comes from the terminal value?

- a. About 95%
- b. About 50%
- c. About 65%
- d. About 85%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. About 85%

**Rationale:** 71.5797 / 84.15 = 0.8506, about 85%. Because 85% of the value is determined by the terminal value, changes that affect the terminal value still affect the model significantly.

</details>

### 260. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §6.13.4] For which companies is the multi-stage DDM most useful?

- a. Mature companies with very high dividend payouts of 70–120% of EPS
- b. Companies with negative earnings
- c. Companies with no dividend history
- d. Early-stage technology companies with no dividends

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Mature companies with very high dividend payouts of 70–120% of EPS

**Rationale:** The accuracy and effectiveness of the dividend valuation model is better for stocks of companies that are in the mature phase of their life cycles and that have very high dividend payouts, for example 70–120% of EPS. Hence the DDM tends to be more useful for gaming companies, tobacco companies and other select mature companies.

</details>

### 261. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §6.13.5] A preference share has a $100 par value and pays $7 a year. At an 8% required return, what is its intrinsic value?

- a. $56.00
- b. $87.50
- c. $100.00
- d. $114.29

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. $87.50

**Rationale:** Because the dividend is a perpetuity, P0 = Dividend / Discount Rate = $7 / 0.08 = $87.50.

</details>

## Chapter 7 — Deposits and Fixed-Income Securities

### 262. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.1] Why was keeping excessive cash from 2008 to 2017 non-optimal?

- a. Because interest rates were high
- b. Because cash was illegal to hold
- c. Because of opportunity costs and loss of purchasing power
- d. Because deposit insurance was withdrawn

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Because of opportunity costs and loss of purchasing power

**Rationale:** Cash is often classified as a non-volatile, safe asset class. However, for that reason, cash pays the lowest return. Keeping excessive cash from 2008 to 2017 could be non-optimal because of the opportunity costs and loss of purchasing power.

</details>

### 263. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.2.1] When was the Singapore Deposit Insurance Scheme introduced, and what is the coverage limit from 1 April 2024?

- a. 1 April 2019; S$75,000
- b. 1 April 2024; S$75,000
- c. 1 April 2006; S$50,000
- d. 1 April 2006; S$100,000

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. 1 April 2006; S$100,000

**Rationale:** The DI Scheme was introduced in Singapore on 1 April 2006. The coverage limit was increased from S$50,000 to S$75,000 on 1 April 2019, and further revised to S$100,000 effective 1 April 2024.

</details>

### 264. **[Multiple response]** [CM-EIP §7.2.1] Which of the following are NOT insured by the Singapore Deposit Insurance Scheme? (Select all that apply)

- a. Foreign currency deposits
- b. Structured deposits
- c. Unit trusts and shares
- d. Singapore dollar savings accounts

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A, B, C

**Rationale:** The scheme is only valid for Singapore dollar-denominated deposits placed with a DI member. Foreign currency deposits, structured deposits and investment products such as unit trusts, shares and other securities are not insured.

</details>

### 265. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.2.1] An individual has S$300,000 of Singapore dollar deposits and S$100,000 of CPFIS deposits with one bank, plus USD 250,000 in a currency investment. How much is insured?

- a. S$200,000
- b. S$400,000
- c. S$650,000
- d. S$100,000

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. S$200,000

**Rationale:** For accounts with a single bank, the scheme covers only up to S$100,000 of the aggregate S$ deposit balance. CPF deposits invested with the bank receive separate cover of up to S$100,000. The USD currency investment is not insured. Total insured = S$200,000.

</details>

### 266. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.3] Which of the following is a money market instrument rather than a capital market instrument?

- a. A 10-year government bond
- b. A repurchase agreement
- c. A convertible bond
- d. An asset-backed security

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. A repurchase agreement

**Rationale:** Money market instruments have maturities of one year or less and include Treasury bills, commercial paper, negotiable certificates of deposit, banker's acceptances, repurchase agreements and trade bills.

</details>

### 267. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.4] What is the typical minimum denomination of a money market instrument?

- a. S$1,000 and above
- b. S$10,000 and above
- c. S$250,000 and above
- d. S$1 million and above

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. S$250,000 and above

**Rationale:** Owing to their relatively short maturity, money market instruments have relatively low risks. However, the minimum denomination of money market instruments is usually relatively large, at S$250,000 and above.

</details>

### 268. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.4] Why do money market instruments carry more reinvestment risk than longer-dated bonds?

- a. Because they are unsecured
- b. Because they are illiquid
- c. Because they pay higher coupons
- d. Because they are short-term, requiring the investor to reinvest proceeds each time the instrument matures

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Because they are short-term, requiring the investor to reinvest proceeds each time the instrument matures

**Rationale:** Money market instruments are subject to more reinvestment risk because they are short-term instruments, requiring the investor to reinvest his investment proceeds each time the investment matures.

</details>

### 269. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.4.1] Why is the T-bill yield used as a benchmark for the risk-free rate?

- a. Because they are obligations of the government and considered free of default risk
- b. Because they are issued by banks
- c. Because they have the longest maturity
- d. Because T-bills pay the highest yield

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because they are obligations of the government and considered free of default risk

**Rationale:** T-bills issued by a creditworthy government are the safest investments and are generally considered to be risk-free. Risk-free here means that there is no risk of defaulting on interest or principal payments when due. The yield on T-bills is usually used as a benchmark for risk-free rates.

</details>

### 270. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.4.3] Why can only corporations with very strong credit ratings issue commercial paper?

- a. Because commercial paper is secured
- b. Because commercial paper is an unsecured promissory note
- c. Because commercial paper is exchange-traded
- d. Because commercial paper has a long maturity

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Because commercial paper is an unsecured promissory note

**Rationale:** Commercial papers are short-dated unsecured promissory notes issued by a corporation. Due to their unsecured nature, only corporations with very strong credit ratings are able to issue commercial papers.

</details>

### 271. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.4.4] What is a repurchase agreement economically?

- a. A forward contract
- b. An unsecured loan
- c. A collateralised short-term loan, with the money market instrument as collateral
- d. An equity investment

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. A collateralised short-term loan, with the money market instrument as collateral

**Rationale:** A repo is the sale of a money market instrument, with a commitment by the seller to buy the security back at a specified price on a future date. Effectively it is a collateralised short-term loan, where the collateral is the money market instrument.

</details>

### 272. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.5] What maturity range defines medium-term notes?

- a. More than 10 years
- b. More than 30 years
- c. Up to one year
- d. More than one year and up to 10 years

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. More than one year and up to 10 years

**Rationale:** The debt securities market may be divided into three maturity segments: money market instruments maturing within one year, medium-term notes with maturities of more than one year and up to 10 years, and long-term bonds with maturities longer than 10 years.

</details>

### 273. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.5.1] What is the major disadvantage of fixed-coupon bonds?

- a. They do not adjust for inflation, which erodes purchasing power and can cause price fluctuations
- b. They are always illiquid
- c. They cannot be sold before maturity
- d. They pay no income

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. They do not adjust for inflation, which erodes purchasing power and can cause price fluctuations

**Rationale:** A major disadvantage of investing in fixed-income securities with fixed coupons and principal repayment is that these securities do not adjust for inflation. Inflation can erode the purchasing power of cash flows and lead to swings in interest rates, producing price fluctuations and capital losses if not held to maturity.

</details>

### 274. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.5.2] What is the difference between a term bond and a serial bond?

- a. A term bond is exchange-traded; a serial bond is OTC
- b. A term bond has a single maturity date; a serial bond has a series of maturity dates
- c. A term bond is unsecured; a serial bond is secured
- d. A term bond is floating rate; a serial bond is fixed rate

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. A term bond has a single maturity date; a serial bond has a series of maturity dates

**Rationale:** Most corporate bonds are typically term bonds, which have a single maturity date. In contrast, serial bonds have a series of maturity dates and are essentially bundles of bonds issued at the same time, but with different maturities.

</details>

### 275. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.6.1] A T-bill with 90 days to maturity, face value $100,000, sells for $98,000. What is the bank discount yield?

- a. 7.35%
- b. 2.04%
- c. 8.00%
- d. 8.16%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 8.00%

**Rationale:** D = 100,000 − 98,000 = 2,000. Yd = (D/F) × (360/t) = (2,000/100,000) × (360/90) = 0.02 × 4 = 0.08 = 8.0%.

</details>

### 276. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.6.1] Why is the bank discount yield not a meaningful measure of return?

- a. Because it uses a 365-day year
- b. Because it ignores the coupon
- c. Because it ignores the maturity date
- d. Because it is calculated based on face value rather than the actual dollar amount invested

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Because it is calculated based on face value rather than the actual dollar amount invested

**Rationale:** Though it is widely used, the quoted yield on a bank discount basis is not a meaningful measure of return from holding a T-bill because it is calculated based on the face value rather than on the actual dollar amount invested.

</details>

### 277. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.6.2] A corporate bond is quoted at 95¾ with a par value of $1,000. What is the price?

- a. $957.50
- b. $955.75
- c. $975.00
- d. $950.00

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. $957.50

**Rationale:** Prices are quoted as a percentage of par value. A quote of 95¾ represents 95.75% of $1,000 = $957.50. Corporate bonds are quoted in eighths of a percentage point.

</details>

### 278. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.6.2] An 8% bond pays semi-annual interest on 1 June and 1 December and is sold on 1 September. What accrued interest is the seller entitled to?

- a. $20
- b. $40
- c. $60
- d. $80

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. $20

**Rationale:** The annual coupon is $80, paid semi-annually as $40. The seller has held the bond for three months since the last coupon, so accrued interest = $80 × 3/12 = $20.

</details>

### 279. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.7.1] Why does the Singapore Government issue Singapore Government Securities if it runs a balanced budget?

- a. To fund infrastructure spending
- b. To build a liquid market and establish a benchmark yield curve for pricing Singapore dollar private debt
- c. To finance the CPF
- d. To control the money supply

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. To build a liquid market and establish a benchmark yield curve for pricing Singapore dollar private debt

**Rationale:** The Singapore Government does not need to finance its expenditures through the issuance of government bonds as it operates on a balanced budget policy. SGS issuance serves primarily to build a liquid SGS market to establish a benchmark yield curve for the pricing of Singapore dollar private debt securities, encourage an active secondary market, and attract issuers and investors.

</details>

### 280. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.7.2] What are the three defining features of a Eurobond?

- a. Issued by a European company, sold in Europe, settled in euros
- b. Rated AAA, unsecured, long-dated
- c. Underwritten by an international syndicate, offered simultaneously in several countries, issued outside any single jurisdiction
- d. Denominated in euros, sold in Europe, listed in London

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Underwritten by an international syndicate, offered simultaneously in several countries, issued outside any single jurisdiction

**Rationale:** A Eurobond is a bond that is underwritten by an international syndicate, offered at issuance simultaneously to investors in a number of countries, and issued outside of the jurisdiction of any single country.

</details>

### 281. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.7.3] What important function does the secondary bond market perform beyond trading?

- a. It guarantees bond payments
- b. It regulates issuers
- c. It sets the coupon on new issues
- d. It benchmarks the market values and yields of traded bonds, which is needed for valuation and for establishing benchmark yield curves

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. It benchmarks the market values and yields of traded bonds, which is needed for valuation and for establishing benchmark yield curves

**Rationale:** Other than being a place where bonds can be bought and sold, the secondary market also serves the important market function of benchmarking the market values and yields of the traded bonds. Such information is necessary for the valuation of bond portfolios and for establishing benchmark yield curves.

</details>

### 282. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.8.1] Why are floating rate bonds attractive to some institutional investors?

- a. Because the income stream more closely matches the floating nature of some of their liabilities
- b. Because they have no credit risk
- c. Because they are tax-exempt
- d. Because they pay a fixed coupon

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because the income stream more closely matches the floating nature of some of their liabilities

**Rationale:** Floating rate securities are attractive to some institutional investors because the securities allow investors to buy an asset with an income stream that more closely matches the floating nature of some of their liabilities.

</details>

### 283. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.8.2] What is the most attractive feature of a zero-coupon bond?

- a. High current income
- b. No reinvestment risk within the life of the bond, so the return is locked in if held to maturity
- c. Low price volatility
- d. Call protection

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. No reinvestment risk within the life of the bond, so the return is locked in if held to maturity

**Rationale:** Perhaps the most attractive feature of zero-coupon bonds is that there is no reinvestment risk within the life of the bond because there is no coupon to reinvest, so the rate of return is locked in when they are held to maturity.

</details>

### 284. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.8.2] Which is a disadvantage of zero-coupon bonds?

- a. They have no maturity value
- b. They pay coupons that must be reinvested
- c. They experience high price volatility, and a rise in interest rates affects them more than a coupon bond
- d. They cannot be sold before maturity

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. They experience high price volatility, and a rise in interest rates affects them more than a coupon bond

**Rationale:** Zero-coupon bonds experience high price volatility: a rise in interest rates will negatively impact the price of a zero-coupon bond much more than that of a coupon bond. Liquidity may also be limited, and call features can limit appreciation.

</details>

### 285. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.8.3] Why are convertible bond yields lower than comparable straight bond yields?

- a. Because convertibles have shorter maturities
- b. Because convertibles are tax-exempt
- c. Because convertibles are unsecured
- d. Because the bondholder effectively pays for the conversion privilege through the lower yield

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Because the bondholder effectively pays for the conversion privilege through the lower yield

**Rationale:** Yields on convertible bonds are generally lower than those on comparable straight bonds because of the conversion privilege, which the bondholder effectively pays for through the lower yield.

</details>

### 286. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.8.4] What is a covered bond?

- a. A highly rated paper backed by bank assets, typically costing less to issue than unsecured bonds
- b. A bond with an insurance wrapper
- c. A bond convertible into shares
- d. A bond secured by a government guarantee

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. A highly rated paper backed by bank assets, typically costing less to issue than unsecured bonds

**Rationale:** Generally, a covered bond is a highly rated paper, backed by bank assets, and typically costs less to issue than unsecured bonds. Covered bonds have a long history in Europe dating to 1769 and have never experienced a default.

</details>

### 287. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.8.5] What rating threshold separates high-yield bonds from investment grade?

- a. Below A
- b. Below BBB
- c. Below BB
- d. Below CCC

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Below BBB

**Rationale:** Bonds with a credit rating below triple B, BBB, are called high-yield bonds or junk bonds. High-yield bonds are securities that trade primarily on their creditworthiness as opposed to the level of interest rates.

</details>

### 288. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.8.6] Which deferred-coupon structure gives the issuer the option to pay cash or issue a similar bond?

- a. Deferred-interest bond
- b. Step-up bond
- c. Payment-in-kind bond
- d. Perpetual bond

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Payment-in-kind bond

**Rationale:** Payment-in-kind bonds give the issuer an option to pay cash at a coupon payment date or give the bondholder a similar bond, with the same coupon and a par value equal to the amount of the coupon payment that would have been paid. The choice period varies from five to ten years.

</details>

### 289. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.8.6] How do perpetual bonds rank in the order of discharge on default?

- a. Equal to equities
- b. After preference shares but before ordinary shares
- c. After equities
- d. Before equities

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Before equities

**Rationale:** Perpetual bonds rank before equities in the order of discharge in the event of default. They pay a higher coupon rate to compensate the investor for an indefinite holding period and the correspondingly higher interest rate risk.

</details>

### 290. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.9.1] Why do callable bonds carry higher yields than equivalent non-callable bonds?

- a. Because the investor risks losing a high-coupon bond when rates decline and the upside price potential is limited
- b. Because they are unsecured
- c. Because they are less liquid
- d. Because they have longer maturities

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because the investor risks losing a high-coupon bond when rates decline and the upside price potential is limited

**Rationale:** The call provision is unattractive to investors who run the risk of losing a high-coupon bond when interest rates decline. The prospect of a call also limits the upside price potential of the bond in a falling interest rate environment. Due to these disadvantages, bonds with call features carry higher yields.

</details>

### 291. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.9.1] How does the call premium behave over the life of a bond?

- a. It rises towards maturity
- b. It tends to be higher in the early life of the bond and declines gradually towards par as maturity approaches
- c. It is constant
- d. It is zero throughout

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. It tends to be higher in the early life of the bond and declines gradually towards par as maturity approaches

**Rationale:** The call price is normally higher than the par value of the bond. The difference between the call price and par value is the call premium, which tends to be higher in the early life of the bond and declines gradually towards par as the bond approaches maturity.

</details>

### 292. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.9.2] What is the purpose of a sinking-fund provision?

- a. To convert the bond into shares
- b. To increase the coupon rate
- c. To reduce credit risk by requiring the issuer to retire a certain amount of debt each year
- d. To allow the holder to sell back at par

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. To reduce credit risk by requiring the issuer to retire a certain amount of debt each year

**Rationale:** Corporate bond indentures may require the issuer to retire a certain amount of the outstanding debt each year. The purpose of a sinking-fund provision is to reduce credit risk.

</details>

### 293. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.9.3] Why does a put provision lower a bond's yield?

- a. Because it extends the maturity
- b. Because it removes the coupon
- c. Because it increases credit risk
- d. Because the investor has effectively bought a put option from the issuer and pays for it with a lower yield

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Because the investor has effectively bought a put option from the issuer and pays for it with a lower yield

**Rationale:** Puttable bonds give the bondholder the right to sell the bonds back to the issuer at par value on designated dates. As with a convertible bond, the investor has effectively bought a put option from the issuer and will pay for it with a lower yield.

</details>

### 294. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.9.4] Which bondholders have a claim subordinated to secured debt and after the main creditors?

- a. Subordinated (junior) bondholders
- b. Income bondholders
- c. Debenture holders
- d. Mortgage bondholders

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Subordinated (junior) bondholders

**Rationale:** Subordinated (junior) bondholders have a claim on the income and assets of the issuer that is subordinated to secured debt and after the main creditors have made their claims. Income bonds are junior debt as the issuing firm can omit or delay interest if earnings are too low.

</details>

### 295. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.10] What is the primary difference between asset-backed securities and mortgage-backed securities?

- a. ABS are exchange-traded; MBS are OTC
- b. MBS are created from pooling mortgages, while ABS are created from pooling non-mortgage assets
- c. ABS are government-guaranteed
- d. MBS have no credit risk

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. MBS are created from pooling mortgages, while ABS are created from pooling non-mortgage assets

**Rationale:** The primary difference between asset-backed securities and mortgage-backed securities is the underlying asset that is being securitised. MBS are created from the pooling of mortgages whereas ABS evolved from MBS and are created from the pooling of non-mortgage assets.

</details>

### 296. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.10.1] What is a collateralised loan obligation?

- a. A government-guaranteed loan
- b. A CDO backed by a pool of bonds
- c. A CDO backed by a pool of loans
- d. A loan secured by collateral

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. A CDO backed by a pool of loans

**Rationale:** CDOs that are backed by a pool of bonds are also known as collateralised bond obligations, while those that are backed by a pool of loans are known as collateralised loan obligations.

</details>

### 297. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.11] What is credit risk?

- a. The risk that the bond is called
- b. The risk that the bond is illiquid
- c. The risk that interest rates rise
- d. The risk that the issuer will be unable to make timely principal and interest payments as agreed

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. The risk that the issuer will be unable to make timely principal and interest payments as agreed

**Rationale:** Credit risk or default risk refers to the risk that the issuer of the bond will be unable to make timely principal and interest payments as agreed in the terms of issue.

</details>

### 298. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.11.1] Which rating indicates selective default on a specific issue while other obligations continue to be met?

- a. SD
- b. R
- c. WR
- d. D

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. SD

**Rationale:** An SD rating is assigned when S&P believes that the obligor has selectively defaulted on a specific issue or class of obligations, but it will continue to meet its payment obligations on other issues or classes of obligations in a timely manner. D indicates a general default.

</details>

### 299. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.11.1] What is the highest short-term issuer credit rating category in the S&P scale?

- a. AAA
- b. A-1
- c. A+
- d. B

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. A-1

**Rationale:** An obligor rated A-1 has strong capacity to meet its financial commitments and is rated in the highest category by S&P. Within this category, certain obligors are designated with a plus sign, indicating extremely strong capacity.

</details>

### 300. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.11.2] A 10-year Treasury yields 2% and a 10-year corporate bond yields 4%. What is the credit spread?

- a. 2 basis points
- b. 20 basis points
- c. 200 basis points
- d. 2,000 basis points

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 200 basis points

**Rationale:** The spread is 4% − 2% = 2% = 200 basis points. A credit spread is the rate difference, usually quoted in basis points, between two bonds of the same maturity but of differing credit quality.

</details>

### 301. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.11.2] What happens to credit spreads during a crisis?

- a. They are unchanged
- b. They become negative
- c. They narrow
- d. They widen against the benchmark

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. They widen against the benchmark

**Rationale:** Credit spreads will widen against the benchmark during crises and narrow when market conditions, and investors' risk appetites, improve. As the issuer's default risk increases, its bond price falls and its bond spread widens.

</details>

### 302. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.11.3] Why might an Asian company choose not to be rated?

- a. Because it believes the main investors who would buy its bonds know its business and history
- b. Because unrated bonds pay lower yields
- c. Because ratings are illegal in Asia
- d. Because rating agencies refuse to rate Asian companies

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because it believes the main investors who would buy its bonds know its business and history

**Rationale:** Some companies in Asia have chosen not to be rated by rating agencies. They do not see a need for it because they believe the main investors who would buy their bonds know their business and history. An unrated company may not necessarily be of poor credit.

</details>

### 303. **[Multiple response]** [CM-EIP §7.11.4] Which of the following can mitigate poor credit quality of a bond issue? (Select all that apply)

- a. Creating a dedicated sinking fund
- b. Offering specified assets as collateral
- c. Offering parental guarantees
- d. Removing the trustee

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A, B, C

**Rationale:** Companies can improve credit quality by creating a dedicated sinking fund, offering specified assets as collateral or security, offering parental guarantees, working with a financial institution to repackage the bonds with higher-quality bonds, or obtaining credit insurance or credit default swaps.

</details>

### 304. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.11.5] In a credit default swap, who pays a lump sum on default?

- a. The buyer of the CDS
- b. The seller of the CDS
- c. The reference entity
- d. The clearing house

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. The seller of the CDS

**Rationale:** The buyer of a CDS pays a premium to be insured against a debt default. The seller of a CDS receives regular payments from the buyer to indemnify the buyer with a lump-sum payment in the event of a debt default.

</details>

### 305. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.11.5] Which institution was bailed out because it had sold multiple CDS on the same mortgage-backed securities?

- a. Fannie Mae
- b. Lehman Brothers
- c. AIG
- d. Bear Stearns

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. AIG

**Rationale:** Insurers that sold multiple CDS on the MBS found themselves unable to provide the protection that they sold, exemplified by the bailout of AIG. Lehman Brothers was a purchaser of the securities that went bankrupt.

</details>

### 306. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.11.6] Which of the following is a red flag for credit analysis?

- a. An increase in cash flow from operations
- b. A decline in the debt level
- c. Rising sales with stable costs
- d. A non-diversified revenue stream coupled with the loss of major clients

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. A non-diversified revenue stream coupled with the loss of major clients

**Rationale:** Projected revenue falls include a non-diversified revenue stream coupled with the loss of major clients. Other red flags include cost rises, regulatory restrictions, falling operating cash flow and the departure of key officers.

</details>

### 307. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.11.7] At what price range might a distressed bond offer a worthwhile recovery opportunity?

- a. $0.30–$0.40 on the dollar
- b. $0.01–$0.05 on the dollar
- c. At par
- d. $0.90–$0.99 on the dollar

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. $0.30–$0.40 on the dollar

**Rationale:** A distressed bond priced at $0.30–$0.40 on the dollar may provide for potential to recover a significant portion of its value when key variables normalise, especially if the challenges relate to a short-term credit squeeze or cyclical downturn.

</details>

### 308. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.12.1] What is the relationship between interest rates and bond prices?

- a. Direct
- b. Inverse
- c. Unrelated
- d. Exponential

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Inverse

**Rationale:** There is an inverse relationship between interest rates and bond prices. As interest rates rise, the prices of bonds fall, and as rates fall, prices rise. This is also referred to as delta risk.

</details>

### 309. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.12.2] For which bonds is reinvestment risk greatest?

- a. Convertible bonds
- b. Zero-coupon bonds with short maturities
- c. Higher coupon bonds with longer holding periods
- d. Floating rate bonds

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Higher coupon bonds with longer holding periods

**Rationale:** Reinvestment risk is greater for higher coupon bonds and longer holding periods. A zero-coupon bond has no reinvestment risk if held to maturity because there is nothing to reinvest.

</details>

### 310. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.12.4] Which type of inflation does inflation risk concern?

- a. Deflation only
- b. Hyperinflation only
- c. Anticipated inflation
- d. Unanticipated inflation

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Unanticipated inflation

**Rationale:** Inflation risk is actually concerned with unanticipated inflation. If inflation is anticipated and has been factored into the price of the bond, then investors are adequately compensated for anticipated inflation.

</details>

### 311. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.12.5] What indicates the marketability of a bond?

- a. The size of the spread between the bid and the offer quoted by the dealer
- b. The credit rating
- c. The maturity date
- d. The size of the coupon

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. The size of the spread between the bid and the offer quoted by the dealer

**Rationale:** An indication of the marketability of a security is the size of the spread between the bid and the offer quoted by the dealer. The greater the spread, the greater the marketability risk.

</details>

### 312. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.12.7] What is vega risk?

- a. The risk that interest rates rise
- b. The risk that a change in expected interest rate volatility adversely affects the price of a security with an embedded option
- c. The risk of default
- d. The risk of early redemption

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. The risk that a change in expected interest rate volatility adversely affects the price of a security with an embedded option

**Rationale:** The price of a bond that has an embedded option depends on the level of interest rates and other factors that influence the value of that embedded option, one of which is the expected volatility of interest rates, also referred to as vega risk.

</details>

### 313. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.13] A 10-year bond with par $1,000 and an 8% coupon, with a required yield of 6% and annual coupons, is priced at approximately:

- a. $920.00
- b. $1,000.00
- c. $1,147.20
- d. $1,148.77

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. $1,147.20

**Rationale:** Discounting eight $80 coupons and the $1,000 principal at 6% gives $1,147.20. With semi-annual coupons the price would be $1,148.77, because the number of periods is doubled and the discount rate halved.

</details>

### 314. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.13.1] A zero-coupon bond matures in 15 years with par $1,000, at a required return of 9%. What is its price?

- a. $274.54
- b. $1,000.00
- c. $641.86
- d. $267.00

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. $267.00

**Rationale:** P = M / (1 + k/2)^(2n) = 1,000 / (1.045)^30 = 1,000 / 3.7453 = $267.00. Even without coupons, the semi-annual rate of 4.5% and the half-year period are used to stay consistent with semi-annual coupon bonds.

</details>

### 315. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.14] A 10-year 12% semi-annual coupon bond is priced at 112.46% of par. What is the required yield?

- a. 8%
- b. 10%
- c. 12%
- d. 14%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 10%

**Rationale:** The price-yield table shows that at a required yield of 10% the bond value is 112.46%, at 12% it is 100.00% and at 14% it is 89.41%. A price above par means the coupon exceeds the yield.

</details>

### 316. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.15] When does a bond trade at a premium?

- a. When the coupon rate is higher than the market yield
- b. When the coupon rate equals the market yield
- c. When the bond is close to maturity
- d. When the coupon rate is lower than the market yield

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. When the coupon rate is higher than the market yield

**Rationale:** When the coupon rate is higher than the market yield, the bond will trade at a premium. When the coupon rate is lower than the market yield, the bond will trade at a discount. When they are equal, the bond trades at par.

</details>

### 317. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.16.1] Why does a longer maturity increase a bond's price sensitivity to interest rates?

- a. Because longer bonds pay higher coupons
- b. Because longer-term cash flows are more sensitive to changes in interest rates
- c. Because longer bonds are less liquid
- d. Because longer bonds have more credit risk

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Because longer-term cash flows are more sensitive to changes in interest rates

**Rationale:** The longer the maturity of a bond, the greater the impact of a change in interest rate on the price of the bond because longer term cash flows are more sensitive to changes in interest rates.

</details>

### 318. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.16.2] For two bonds of equal maturity, which is more affected by a rise in market yields?

- a. It depends on the credit rating
- b. The bond with the larger coupon
- c. The bond with the smaller coupon
- d. Both equally

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The bond with the smaller coupon

**Rationale:** A bond with smaller coupons generally has a larger percentage of its value attributable to its long-term principal repayment component. Since longer term cash flows are more susceptible to changes in interest rates, bonds with smaller coupons will be more severely affected by a rise in yield.

</details>

### 319. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.17.1] For which bonds is duration highest, all else equal?

- a. Higher coupons, lower yields and shorter maturities
- b. Zero coupons with short maturities
- c. Higher coupons, higher yields and shorter maturities
- d. Lower coupons, lower yields and longer maturities

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Lower coupons, lower yields and longer maturities

**Rationale:** All else equal, duration is higher for bonds with lower coupons, lower yields and longer maturities.

</details>

### 320. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.17.1] Over what size of interest rate change is duration a reliable linear approximation?

- a. Not more than 1% or 100 basis points
- b. Up to 5%
- c. Up to 10%
- d. Any size

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Not more than 1% or 100 basis points

**Rationale:** Duration is a linear measure of the approximate price sensitivity of a bond to small interest rate changes, that is not more than 1% or 100 basis points.

</details>

### 321. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.17.2] Which bond has the highest convexity?

- a. A 10-year 4% coupon bond
- b. A 10-year zero-coupon bond
- c. A 5-year 8% coupon bond
- d. A 10-year 8% coupon bond

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. A 10-year zero-coupon bond

**Rationale:** Convexity is directly related to the coupon rate and the bond's remaining term to maturity. The lower the coupon, the higher the convexity. Zero coupon bonds have the highest convexity.

</details>

### 322. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.17.3] What should an investor do with portfolio duration when interest rates are expected to fall?

- a. Set duration to zero
- b. Decrease duration to minimise sensitivity
- c. Increase duration to maximise sensitivity
- d. Leave duration unchanged

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Increase duration to maximise sensitivity

**Rationale:** With the anticipation of falling interest rates, bond prices are anticipated to rise, and a bond portfolio's duration should be increased to maximise the sensitivity of the portfolio. Conversely, when rising rates are expected, duration should be decreased.

</details>

### 323. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.17.3] How does greater convexity affect a bond's price when interest rates rise?

- a. The price is unaffected
- b. The price rises
- c. The price falls faster
- d. The price falls slower

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. The price falls slower

**Rationale:** When interest rates rise, the price of a more convex bond will not fall as much. When interest rates fall, the price of a more convex bond will rise much faster. Regardless of whether rates are rising or falling, investors should seek bonds with greater convexity.

</details>

### 324. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.17.4] What does PVBP or PV01 measure?

- a. The change in present value of the entire bond portfolio for a one basis point movement in interest rates
- b. The bond's yield to maturity
- c. The bond's convexity
- d. The bond's price

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. The change in present value of the entire bond portfolio for a one basis point movement in interest rates

**Rationale:** The PVBP or PV01 is used as a risk management tool for a bond portfolio. It is the change in the present value of the entire bond portfolio for a one basis point movement in interest rates.

</details>

### 325. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.18] What happens to the price of a discount bond as it approaches maturity, all else constant?

- a. It falls
- b. It rises towards par
- c. It stays constant
- d. It becomes zero

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. It rises towards par

**Rationale:** Over time, holding all other factors constant, a bond's price must converge to its par value as the maturity date approaches because the bond will be redeemed at par. The price of a discount bond will increase and the price of a premium bond will decline.

</details>

### 326. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.19.1] A 10-year 6% coupon bond with par $1,000 is priced at 115%. What is the current yield?

- a. 5.00%
- b. 6.90%
- c. 5.22%
- d. 6.00%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 5.22%

**Rationale:** Current yield = Annual interest payment / Bond price = $60 / $1,150 = 5.22%. Current yield considers only the annual interest payment and does not account for any capital gain or loss.

</details>

### 327. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.19.2] A bond priced at $1,148.80 with an 8% semi-annual coupon and 10 years to maturity has a semi-annual YTM of 3.00%. What is the bond-equivalent yield?

- a. 3.00%
- b. 6.00%
- c. 6.09%
- d. 6.12%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 6.00%

**Rationale:** The market convention is to annualise the semi-annual yield by doubling its value: 3.00% × 2 = 6.00%, which is the bond-equivalent yield. The effective annual yield, which is the proper way to annualise, is (1.03)² − 1 = 6.09%.

</details>

### 328. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.19.2] A bond priced at $1,148.80 with par $1,000 and an 8% annual coupon has 10 years to maturity. Using the approximation formula, what is the YTM?

- a. 6.00%
- b. 6.09%
- c. 6.96%
- d. 6.06%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. 6.06%

**Rationale:** Amortised loss per year = (1,148.80 − 1,000)/10 = $14.88. Approximate YTM = (80 − 14.88) / ((1,148.80 + 1,000)/2) = 65.12 / 1,074.40 = 6.06%.

</details>

### 329. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.19.2] A 15-year zero-coupon bond with maturity value $1,000 sells for $300. What is the bond-equivalent yield?

- a. 8.2%
- b. 4.1%
- c. 6.0%
- d. 12.0%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. 8.2%

**Rationale:** k/2 = (1,000/300)^(1/30) − 1 = 0.0409 = 4.1% semi-annually. The bond-equivalent yield is 4.1% × 2 = 8.2%. The number of periods is 30 semi-annual periods, double the years.

</details>

### 330. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.19.2] Under what two conditions will an investor actually realise the stated YTM?

- a. If the bond is sold before maturity and rates fall
- b. If coupon payments can be consistently reinvested at the YTM and the bond is held to maturity
- c. If the issuer does not call the bond
- d. If the credit rating is upgraded

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. If coupon payments can be consistently reinvested at the YTM and the bond is held to maturity

**Rationale:** The investor will only realise the YTM stated at the time of purchase if the coupon payments can be consistently reinvested at the YTM and the bond is held to maturity.

</details>

### 331. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.19.4] An investor in the 28% marginal tax bracket holds a tax-exempt bond with a YTM of 7%. What is the taxable equivalent yield?

- a. 5.04%
- b. 7.0%
- c. 9.7%
- d. 8.96%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 9.7%

**Rationale:** Taxable equivalent yield = Tax-exempt yield / (1 − marginal tax rate) = 0.07 / (1 − 0.28) = 0.07 / 0.72 = 9.7%.

</details>

### 332. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.20.1] What does a declining yield curve indicate?

- a. Inflation is expected to rise
- b. The market is ambivalent
- c. Short-term rates are expected to rise
- d. Generally lower short-term interest rates in the future

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Generally lower short-term interest rates in the future

**Rationale:** The declining yield curve is seen when the yields on short-term issues are higher than the yields on longer-maturity issues. This generally indicates lower short-term interest rates in the future.

</details>

### 333. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.20.2] Which theory argues that the yield curve should always be upward sloping?

- a. Liquidity preference theory
- b. Market segmentation theory
- c. Purchasing power parity
- d. Expectations theory

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Liquidity preference theory

**Rationale:** The theory of liquidity preference argues that the shape of the yield curve should always be upward sloping and that any other shape would be a temporary aberration, because investors require a higher return to hold long-term securities.

</details>

### 334. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.20.2] A 1-year T-bill yields 6% and a new 1-year T-bill is expected to yield 8% next year. Under the expectations theory, what should a 2-year security yield?

- a. 6.0%
- b. 7.0%
- c. 8.0%
- d. 14.0%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 7.0%

**Rationale:** The average annual rate of return from rolling 1-year bills is [(1.06)(1.08)]^(1/2) − 1 = 0.07. An investor in a 2-year security will require a yield of 7%, the average of the 6% realised in year one and the 8% expected in year two.

</details>

### 335. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.20.2] Which theory holds that each maturity's yield is determined solely by supply and demand in that segment?

- a. Liquidity preference theory
- b. Market segmentation theory
- c. Quantity theory
- d. Expectations theory

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Market segmentation theory

**Rationale:** The market segmentation theory presumes that investors have their own maturity preferences, which are dictated by the nature of their liabilities. The yield for each maturity is determined solely by the supply and demand in each segment.

</details>

### 336. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.21.1] A convertible bond has a conversion ratio of 40 and the share price is $24. What is the conversion value?

- a. $1,100
- b. $728
- c. $960
- d. $1,000

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. $960

**Rationale:** Conversion value = Conversion ratio × Market share price = 40 × $24 = $960. The minimum value of a convertible bond is the greater of its conversion value and its investment value.

</details>

### 337. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.21.2] A convertible bond has a par value of $1,000 and a conversion ratio of 40. What is the conversion price?

- a. $27.50
- b. $24
- c. $40
- d. $25

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. $25

**Rationale:** Conversion price = Par value of CB / Conversion ratio = $1,000 / 40 = $25.

</details>

### 338. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.21.3] A convertible bond trades at $1,100 with a conversion ratio of 40. What is the conversion parity price?

- a. $27.50
- b. $25.00
- c. $24.00
- d. $26.00

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. $27.50

**Rationale:** Conversion parity price = Market price of CB / Conversion ratio = $1,100 / 40 = $27.50. This can be considered a share price breakeven point.

</details>

### 339. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.21.4] A convertible trades at $1,100 with a conversion value of $960. What is the conversion premium in percentage terms?

- a. 51.0%
- b. 14.6%
- c. 12.7%
- d. 4.6%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 14.6%

**Rationale:** Conversion premium (%) = (Market value of CB − Conversion value) / Conversion value = (1,100 − 960) / 960 = 140 / 960 = 14.6%.

</details>

### 340. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.21.5] A convertible has a conversion premium of $140, bond income of $40 and dividend income of $14. What is the breakeven time?

- a. 10.00 years
- b. 2.60 years
- c. 5.38 years
- d. 3.50 years

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 5.38 years

**Rationale:** Breakeven time = Conversion premium / (Bond income − Dividend income) = 140 / (40 − 14) = 140 / 26 = 5.38 years. The measure does not consider the time value of money.

</details>

### 341. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §7.21.6] A convertible trades at $1,100 with an investment value of $728. What is the premium over straight value?

- a. 34%
- b. 66%
- c. 151%
- d. 51%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. 51%

**Rationale:** Premium over straight value = (Market price of CB / Investment value) − 1 = 1,100/728 − 1 = 1.51 − 1 = 51%. Despite its use in practice, this measure is flawed because the straight value changes as the discount rate changes.

</details>

### 342. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §7.21.7] How does a convertible bond's yield compare with the underlying share's dividend yield and a plain vanilla bond?

- a. Higher than the dividend yield but lower than a comparable plain vanilla bond
- b. Lower than the dividend yield but higher than a plain vanilla bond
- c. Lower than both
- d. Higher than both

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Higher than the dividend yield but lower than a comparable plain vanilla bond

**Rationale:** The yield on a convertible bond is usually higher than the dividend yield of the underlying ordinary share but lower than the yield of a comparable plain vanilla bond.

</details>

## Chapter 8 — Portfolio Management

### 343. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.2] What does the Efficient Market Hypothesis state?

- a. Prices are always above fair value
- b. Security prices always fully reflect available information
- c. Security prices never reflect available information
- d. Only fundamental analysis can beat the market

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Security prices always fully reflect available information

**Rationale:** The EMH states that an efficient market is one where security prices always fully reflect available information. The direct implication is that stocks always trade at their fair values and it is impossible to outperform the overall market consistently on a risk-adjusted basis.

</details>

### 344. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.2.1] Under the weak form of the EMH, which technique cannot consistently produce excess returns?

- a. Portfolio diversification
- b. Fundamental analysis
- c. Technical analysis
- d. Insider trading

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Technical analysis

**Rationale:** Under the weak form, all past price information is fully reflected in the current stock price, so future prices follow a random walk. This implies that investors will not be able to consistently achieve excess returns using technical analysis.

</details>

### 345. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.2.2] Under the semi-strong form of the EMH, which techniques cannot consistently beat the market?

- a. Both fundamental and technical analysis
- b. Technical analysis only
- c. Fundamental analysis only
- d. Neither fundamental nor technical analysis

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Neither fundamental nor technical analysis

**Rationale:** Under the semi-strong form, all publicly available information is fully reflected in the current stock price and prices react rapidly to new public information. This implies that neither fundamental analysis nor technical analysis will allow investors to consistently beat the market.

</details>

### 346. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.2.3] Why is the strong form of the EMH not supported by the evidence?

- a. Because the history of insider trading suggests inside information does provide an advantage
- b. Because technical analysis always works
- c. Because prices never adjust
- d. Because markets are perfectly efficient

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because the history of insider trading suggests inside information does provide an advantage

**Rationale:** The evidence suggests real-world financial markets are not strong form efficient. The history of insider trading in the US and numerous high-profile cases continue to suggest that having inside information does provide an information advantage to generate abnormal gains.

</details>

### 347. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.2.4] What do supporters of market efficiency highlight about fund managers?

- a. That most outperform their benchmarks
- b. That the majority underperform their benchmarks even before fees, and outperformance does not persist
- c. That all managers generate alpha
- d. That fees are irrelevant

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. That the majority underperform their benchmarks even before fees, and outperformance does not persist

**Rationale:** Supporters of market efficiency highlight that the average fund manager does not outperform the market. Despite all the expertise most fund managers claim to have, the majority underperform their respective benchmarks even if fees are excluded. Even for managers that outperform, the outperformance does not generally persist.

</details>

### 348. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.3.1] Which investor should probably adopt a passive investment approach?

- a. One who wants to trade frequently
- b. One with large amounts, time and expertise
- c. One who can only invest small amounts, lacks time and expertise, and does not wish to pay fees
- d. One who believes markets are inefficient

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. One who can only invest small amounts, lacks time and expertise, and does not wish to pay fees

**Rationale:** Investors who are only able to invest in small amounts, lack the time or expertise to monitor and manage their investments, and do not wish to pay fees should probably adopt a passive investment approach and rely on index funds.

</details>

### 349. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.3.2] Which of the following is an active strategy?

- a. Buying a market-cap weighted ETF
- b. Dollar cost averaging into an index
- c. Buying and holding an index fund
- d. Sector rotation

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Sector rotation

**Rationale:** Security selection, sector rotation and market timing are examples of active strategies. The objective of active management is to outperform the market on a risk-adjusted basis.

</details>

### 350. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.3.2] How many industries does the Industry Classification Benchmark classify the market into?

- a. 5
- b. 8
- c. 10
- d. 12

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 10

**Rationale:** Under the Industry Classification Benchmarks, the market is classified into 10 industries, which are further sub-divided into super sectors and sectors. A sector is the smallest subset of an industry.

</details>

### 351. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.1] What does the efficient frontier represent?

- a. All combinations of assets that generate the highest return at a given level of risk
- b. The portfolio with the lowest risk
- c. The portfolio with the highest return
- d. All possible portfolios, efficient or not

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. All combinations of assets that generate the highest return at a given level of risk

**Rationale:** The Efficient Frontier represents all possible combinations of the two asset classes that would generate the highest return at a given level of risk. All the different combinations that lie on the Efficient Frontier represent optimal portfolios, but for clients with different risk profiles.

</details>

### 352. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.1] Why is the efficient frontier a curve rather than a straight line?

- a. Because of transaction costs
- b. Because returns are not perfectly correlated
- c. Because risk is measured in percentages
- d. Because the number of assets is finite

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Because returns are not perfectly correlated

**Rationale:** The frontier is a curve instead of a straight line because the returns of the two assets are not perfectly correlated.

</details>

### 353. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.2] Approximately what proportion of portfolio standard deviation is eliminated by the time there are 10 securities in the portfolio?

- a. One quarter
- b. One third
- c. One half
- d. All of it

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. One half

**Rationale:** Studies have shown that around half of portfolio standard deviation is eliminated by the time that there are 10 securities in the portfolio. Unfortunately, the benefits of diversification diminish as more securities are added.

</details>

### 354. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.2] What is the only condition required for adding a second stock to reduce portfolio risk?

- a. The second stock must have a higher return
- b. The second stock must have lower volatility
- c. The two stocks must be in different industries
- d. Correlation between the two stocks must be less than perfectly positive

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Correlation between the two stocks must be less than perfectly positive

**Rationale:** It does not matter whether factors affecting the second stock are diametrically opposed to those affecting the first. All that matters is that there is less than perfect positive correlation between the factors driving the two price movements. As long as correlation is not 1, the addition has the potential to diversify risk.

</details>

### 355. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.3] Which statement about systematic risk is correct?

- a. It cannot be reduced through diversification but can be partly or almost fully offset through hedging
- b. It is the same as firm-specific risk
- c. It is captured by the error term
- d. It can be eliminated by diversification

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. It cannot be reduced through diversification but can be partly or almost fully offset through hedging

**Rationale:** Systematic risk cannot be reduced through diversification. However, depending on the level of exposure and asset class invested, investors can partly or almost fully offset systematic risk through hedging, for example the use of options and futures.

</details>

### 356. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.3] What does beta reflect?

- a. Total risk including firm-specific risk
- b. Systematic risk, the sensitivity of the portfolio to market movement
- c. The error term in the risk decomposition
- d. The correlation coefficient

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Systematic risk, the sensitivity of the portfolio to market movement

**Rationale:** Systematic risk is reflected in the beta of an investment portfolio. This is the sensitivity of the portfolio to market movement.

</details>

### 357. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.4] What happens to portfolio risk when two securities with a correlation of +1 are combined?

- a. Risk is eliminated
- b. Risk is reduced but not eliminated
- c. There is no reduction in portfolio risk; it is the weighted average of the individual risks
- d. Risk becomes zero

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. There is no reduction in portfolio risk; it is the weighted average of the individual risks

**Rationale:** Combining securities that are perfectly positively correlated with each other provides no reduction in portfolio risk. The risk of the resulting portfolio is simply a weighted average of the individual risks of the securities.

</details>

### 358. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.4] What is the principle behind hedging strategies in terms of correlation?

- a. Combining securities with zero correlation
- b. Combining securities with correlation of 0.5
- c. Combining securities with correlation of +1
- d. Combining two securities with perfect negative correlation, which can eliminate portfolio risk altogether

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Combining two securities with perfect negative correlation, which can eliminate portfolio risk altogether

**Rationale:** Combining two securities with perfect negative correlation with each other can eliminate portfolio risk altogether. This is the principle behind hedging strategies.

</details>

### 359. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.6] What happens to correlations between asset classes during a major financial crisis?

- a. They move towards 1
- b. They remain stable
- c. They become zero
- d. They fall towards −1

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. They move towards 1

**Rationale:** One concern in relation to diversification is the potential for asset correlations to move towards 1 during major financial crises. Under such conditions, correlation between assets gravitates towards 1, and there is nowhere for funds to hide except in cash and government bonds.

</details>

### 360. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §8.4.7] Security A has an expected return of 18% and a weight of 40%; Security B has 9% and a weight of 60%. What is the portfolio expected return?

- a. 27.0%
- b. 12.6%
- c. 13.5%
- d. 13.3%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 12.6%

**Rationale:** Rp = 0.4(0.18) + 0.6(0.09) = 0.072 + 0.054 = 0.126 = 12.6%. The expected portfolio return is a weighted average of the constituent returns.

</details>

### 361. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §8.4.7] Security A has a standard deviation of 20% and weight 40%; Security B has 10% and weight 60%; correlation is 0.8. What is portfolio risk?

- a. 2.00%
- b. 14.00%
- c. 13.30%
- d. 10.00%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 13.30%

**Rationale:** σp = √(0.4²×0.2² + 0.6²×0.1² + 2×0.4×0.6×0.8×0.2×0.1) = √(0.0064 + 0.0036 + 0.00768) = √0.01768 = 0.1330 = 13.30%.

</details>

### 362. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §8.4.7] Using the same two securities, what would portfolio risk be if the correlation were −1?

- a. 0.00%
- b. 14.00%
- c. 10.00%
- d. 2.00%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. 2.00%

**Rationale:** With ρ = −1, σp = √(0.0064 + 0.0036 − 0.00768) = √0.00232 = 0.0482, but the weighted difference is |0.4(0.2) − 0.6(0.1)| = |0.08 − 0.06| = 0.02 = 2%. Portfolio risk falls from 14% at ρ = 1 to 2% at ρ = −1.

</details>

### 363. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.8] How can foreign exchange risk be removed from an international portfolio?

- a. By hedging the risk using forward exchange contracts
- b. By holding only emerging market securities
- c. By increasing the equity allocation
- d. By avoiding foreign investment entirely

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. By hedging the risk using forward exchange contracts

**Rationale:** Inherent in international diversification is the risk of fluctuating exchange rates of foreign currency investments. However, one way to remove foreign exchange risk from international portfolio investments is to hedge the risk using forward exchange contracts.

</details>

### 364. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.8] Does international diversification always improve risk-adjusted returns?

- a. Yes, always
- b. No, adding the wrong securities and markets can lower the risk-adjusted return
- c. Only for institutional investors
- d. Only in developed markets

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. No, adding the wrong securities and markets can lower the risk-adjusted return

**Rationale:** International diversification does not always lead to higher risk-adjusted returns. It makes sense to add securities from new markets only if the additions have the potential to raise the risk-adjusted return of the portfolio. Sometimes adding the wrong securities and markets can actually lower the risk-adjusted return.

</details>

### 365. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.9] Which of the following is NOT an assumption of the CAPM?

- a. Investors can borrow or lend at the risk-free rate
- b. There are no taxes or transaction costs
- c. Investors have heterogeneous expectations of risk and return
- d. All investors have the same one-period time horizon

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Investors have heterogeneous expectations of risk and return

**Rationale:** CAPM assumes investors have homogeneous expectations of risk, return and covariances of securities. Heterogeneous expectations would mean investors arrive at different optimal portfolios, which breaks the market portfolio argument.

</details>

### 366. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.9] What is the beta of the market portfolio?

- a. 0
- b. 0.5
- c. 1
- d. 2

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 1

**Rationale:** The beta of the market portfolio is 1. A security with beta greater than 1 contains proportionately higher systematic risk than the market and should be compensated with a higher expected return.

</details>

### 367. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.9] What is the risk-free asset's covariance with any risky asset?

- a. Equal to the market variance
- b. Negative
- c. 1
- d. Zero

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Zero

**Rationale:** The risk-free asset has no risk and provides a risk-free rate of return. The risk-free asset's return variance is zero and its return covariance with any risky asset is zero as well.

</details>

### 368. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.9] What does the capital market line specify?

- a. The equilibrium relation between expected return and total risk for efficiently diversified portfolios
- b. The relation between coupon and yield
- c. The relation between inflation and exchange rates
- d. The equilibrium relation between expected return and systematic risk for all assets

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. The equilibrium relation between expected return and total risk for efficiently diversified portfolios

**Rationale:** The capital market line specifies the equilibrium relation between expected return and total risk for efficiently diversified portfolios. The security market line specifies the relation between expected return and systematic risk and applies to individual securities as well as portfolios.

</details>

### 369. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.9] What does the slope of the capital market line represent?

- a. The risk-free rate
- b. The market price of risk for efficient portfolios
- c. The beta of the market
- d. The correlation coefficient

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. The market price of risk for efficient portfolios

**Rationale:** The slope of the CML, [E(Rm) − Rf]/σm, is the market price of risk for efficient portfolios. It indicates the additional return that the market requires for a 1% increase in the portfolio's standard deviation of returns.

</details>

### 370. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §8.4.9] A portfolio has a beta of 1.20, the risk-free rate is 2.5% and the expected market return is 14%. What is the expected return by CAPM?

- a. 19.3%
- b. 11.5%
- c. 16.3%
- d. 14.0%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 16.3%

**Rationale:** E(r) = rf + β(rm − rf) = 2.5 + 1.2(14 − 2.5) = 2.5 + 1.2(11.5) = 2.5 + 13.8 = 16.3%.

</details>

### 371. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §8.4.9] Using the same portfolio, if the actual return is 17%, what is the alpha?

- a. −0.7%
- b. +1.0%
- c. +3.0%
- d. +0.7%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. +0.7%

**Rationale:** Alpha = actual return − CAPM predicted return = 17% − 16.3% = +0.7%. At an actual return of 15% the alpha would be −1.3%.

</details>

### 372. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.9] What does a security lying above the security market line indicate?

- a. It is under-priced, providing above-average expected returns for its beta
- b. It has zero systematic risk
- c. It is fairly priced
- d. It is overpriced

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. It is under-priced, providing above-average expected returns for its beta

**Rationale:** Securities that lie above the SML provide above average expected returns given their respective betas. Such securities are considered to be under-priced. Securities below the SML are overpriced.

</details>

### 373. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.9] What does a steep security market line suggest about the market?

- a. The market is overvalued
- b. The market may be excessively risk-averse and therefore cheap
- c. The market is in equilibrium
- d. The risk-free rate is zero

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. The market may be excessively risk-averse and therefore cheap

**Rationale:** A steep SML indicates that a large incremental return can be earned in the market with a small increase in systematic risk. As such, the market may be excessively risk-averse and therefore cheap. A flat SML suggests the market is overvalued.

</details>

### 374. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.10] Who developed the Arbitrage Pricing Theory?

- a. Eugene Fama
- b. William Sharpe
- c. Stephen Ross
- d. Harry Markowitz

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Stephen Ross

**Rationale:** In response to criticisms of the CAPM, Stephen Ross developed an alternative asset pricing model in the early 1970s, the Arbitrage Pricing Theory. The APT is a multifactor equilibrium pricing model.

</details>

### 375. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.10] Which of the following does the APT NOT assume, unlike the CAPM?

- a. Markets are perfect
- b. Investors have homogeneous beliefs
- c. Investors are risk-averse utility maximisers
- d. A single-period investment horizon

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. A single-period investment horizon

**Rationale:** In contrast to the CAPM, the APT does not assume a single-period investment horizon, absence of taxes, borrowing and lending at the risk-free rate, or investors selecting portfolios on the basis of expected return and variance.

</details>

### 376. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.10] Why is the rate of inflation not an APT risk factor, while unexpected inflation is?

- a. Because the rate of inflation is partially predictable, whereas unexpected inflation is unpredictable by the market as a whole
- b. Because inflation only affects bonds
- c. Because the APT ignores inflation
- d. Because inflation is not an economic factor

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because the rate of inflation is partially predictable, whereas unexpected inflation is unpredictable by the market as a whole

**Rationale:** At the beginning of each period, the risk factor must be unpredictable by the market as a whole. The rate of inflation is not an APT risk factor because it is partially predictable. In contrast, unexpected inflation, the difference between actual and expected inflation, is an APT risk factor.

</details>

### 377. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.4.10] How many systematic factors did Chen, Roll and Ross identify?

- a. Three
- b. Four
- c. Five
- d. Two

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Four

**Rationale:** Chen, Roll and Ross identified four systematic factors: unanticipated changes in inflation, in industrial production, in the default-risk premium, and in the term structure of interest rates.

</details>

### 378. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.5.1] What is the typical range of investment management fees quoted in the guide?

- a. 3.0%–5.0%
- b. 0.1%–0.5%
- c. 1.0%–1.5%
- d. 2.0%–3.0%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 1.0%–1.5%

**Rationale:** There are also investors who do not wish to incur investment management fees, which can range from 1.0% to 1.5%, on average.

</details>

### 379. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.5.1] What is the difference between advisory and discretionary portfolio management?

- a. Advisory charges no fees
- b. Discretionary requires client approval for each trade
- c. Advisory is for institutions; discretionary is for retail
- d. In advisory the client remains engaged in decisions; in discretionary the manager decides without permission for each transaction

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. In advisory the client remains engaged in decisions; in discretionary the manager decides without permission for each transaction

**Rationale:** For investors who wish to remain actively engaged in investment decisions, an advisory portfolio management approach is needed. Under discretionary portfolio management, the fund manager is able to decide on asset allocation and securities selection without having to obtain permission from the client for each transaction.

</details>

### 380. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.5.2] What is the first step in the portfolio management process for retail investors?

- a. Setting investment objectives
- b. Monitoring market conditions
- c. Reviewing and adjusting the portfolio
- d. Measuring portfolio performance

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Setting investment objectives

**Rationale:** The process is dynamic, continuous and systematic: setting investment objectives, developing and implementing strategies, monitoring market conditions, reviewing and adjusting the portfolio, and measuring investment portfolio performance.

</details>

### 381. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.5.2] Why should objectives, constraints and strategy be stated in a written document?

- a. Because regulators require it
- b. Because a well-drafted investment policy provides discipline, minimising whipsaw reactions to temporary price swings
- c. Because it reduces fees
- d. Because it guarantees returns

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Because a well-drafted investment policy provides discipline, minimising whipsaw reactions to temporary price swings

**Rationale:** A well-drafted investment policy provides the discipline required for efficient management of the portfolio, minimising whipsaw reactions to temporary price swings or even protracted volatility.

</details>

### 382. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.5.3] Who does the investment policy statement belong to?

- a. The custodian
- b. The portfolio manager
- c. The client
- d. The regulator

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The client

**Rationale:** The IPS or investment mandate belongs to the client and not to the portfolio manager. It is therefore essential that the investment manager spends sufficient time with the client to define the client's investment goals and acceptable levels of risk.

</details>

### 383. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §8.5.4] In the life cycle approach, what is the appropriate risk posture in the early career stage?

- a. Only cash and money market funds
- b. Balanced income and growth
- c. Capital preservation with low-risk assets
- d. High return, high risk, growth-oriented investments

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>
