### 512. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §10.2.3] In the DF Int'l example, the share price rose 85% while the warrant price rose 167%. What does this illustrate?

- a. That the warrant was overpriced
- b. That the conversion ratio changed
- c. That warrants are less risky than shares
- d. The leverage effect, offering greater percentage gains and losses than the underlying share

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. The leverage effect, offering greater percentage gains and losses than the underlying share

**Rationale:** Investors are interested in warrants mainly because of the leverage effect. Leverage offers the potential for greater percentage gains and losses than the underlying share for a given change in the price of the underlying share.

</details>

### 513. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §10.2.4] A share price is $0.265 and the warrant price is $0.030 with a conversion ratio of 1. What is the gearing ratio?

- a. 8.8x
- b. 0.11x
- c. 88x
- d. 1.9x

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. 8.8x

**Rationale:** Gearing ratio = SP / (n × WP) = 0.265 / 0.030 = 8.8x approximately. Gearing gives investors the ability to participate in the performance of the underlying share at a lower price.

</details>

### 514. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §10.2.5] For a warrant with a conversion ratio of 1, what is the maximum value?

- a. The intrinsic value
- b. The underlying share price
- c. The exercise price
- d. The warrant premium

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. The underlying share price

**Rationale:** For a warrant with a conversion ratio of 1, the maximum value of a warrant is the underlying share price. Most warrant prices do not trade at their maximum value and prices fall as the warrants approach expiration.

</details>

### 515. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §10.2.5] For a warrant with a conversion ratio of 1, what is the minimum value?

- a. The exercise price
- b. Zero always
- c. The intrinsic value, which is SP − EP if SP > EP and zero otherwise
- d. The share price

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The intrinsic value, which is SP − EP if SP > EP and zero otherwise

**Rationale:** For a warrant with a conversion ratio of 1, the minimum value of a warrant is the intrinsic value of the warrant: SP − EP if SP > EP, and zero if SP is less than or equal to EP.

</details>

### 516. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §10.2.5] Why does the premium of the warrant price over intrinsic value decrease as the share price rises well above the exercise price?

- a. Because the warrant expires
- b. Because the conversion ratio changes
- c. Because the exercise price falls
- d. Because the leverage potential declines, with a given share price increase constituting an increasingly smaller percentage of the share price

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Because the leverage potential declines, with a given share price increase constituting an increasingly smaller percentage of the share price

**Rationale:** As the price of the underlying share increases beyond a point, the premium of the warrant price over the intrinsic value decreases. This is because the leverage potential of the warrant declines at some point, with a given share price increase constituting an increasingly smaller percentage of the share price.

</details>

### 517. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §10.3] How does an increase in the remaining life of a warrant affect its price?

- a. It increases the price
- b. It has no effect
- c. It makes the price zero
- d. It reduces the price

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. It increases the price

**Rationale:** Other things being equal, the longer the time before the warrants expire, the greater the warrant price. A warrant that is out-of-the-money may become attractive later as the share price appreciates. On the day of expiration, warrants are worth their intrinsic value, after which they are worthless.

</details>

### 518. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §10.3] How does an increase in the exercise price affect a warrant's price?

- a. It increases the price
- b. It reduces the price
- c. It has no effect
- d. It doubles the price

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. It reduces the price

**Rationale:** The lower the exercise price, the greater the warrant price. The ability to convert warrants into a share by paying a lower exercise price increases the intrinsic value of the warrant and hence the warrant's price.

</details>

### 519. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §10.3] Why does higher volatility of the underlying share increase a warrant's price?

- a. Because volatility extends the maturity
- b. Because volatility increases the exercise price
- c. Because warrants benefit from price upside but limit price downside
- d. Because volatility reduces the conversion ratio

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Because warrants benefit from price upside but limit price downside

**Rationale:** The more volatile the price of the underlying share, the higher the warrant price, because warrants benefit from price upside but limit price downside. Volatility can change over time.

</details>

### 520. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §10.3] Why do dividend declarations reduce a call warrant's price?

- a. Because dividends reduce volatility
- b. Because dividends shorten the warrant's life
- c. Because dividends increase the exercise price
- d. Because warrant holders are not entitled to dividends, so call warrants lose out

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Because warrant holders are not entitled to dividends, so call warrants lose out

**Rationale:** As warrant holders are not entitled to dividends, the declaration of dividends by the company will reduce the call warrant's price slightly, since call warrants lose out.

</details>

### 521. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §10.3] Why do higher interest rates increase a call warrant's price?

- a. Because of interest savings from the lower capital needed to buy warrants compared with an equivalent long stock position
- b. Because volatility rises
- c. Because dividends fall
- d. Because the exercise price falls

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because of interest savings from the lower capital needed to buy warrants compared with an equivalent long stock position

**Rationale:** The higher the interest rate, the higher the call warrant price due to interest savings from the lower capital needed to buy warrants compared to an equivalent long stock position. For completeness, the higher the interest rate, the lower the put warrant price.

</details>

### 522. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §10.3] Why is there a negative relation between the size of a warrant issue and the warrant price?

- a. Because larger issues are less liquid
- b. Because more warrants outstanding means larger potential earnings dilution if the warrants are deep in-the-money
- c. Because larger issues have shorter maturities
- d. Because larger issues have lower exercise prices

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Because more warrants outstanding means larger potential earnings dilution if the warrants are deep in-the-money

**Rationale:** The more warrants a company has outstanding, the larger the potential earnings dilution of the existing shares. If the warrants were deep in-the-money, the conversion of the warrants would increase the number of outstanding shares and reduce the value of each share. Thus there is a negative relation between the size of the warrant issue and the warrant price.

</details>

### 523. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §10.4] How are company warrants settled compared with structured warrants?

- a. Both deliver shares
- b. Both are cash-settled
- c. Company warrants are settled with delivery of shares; structured warrants are cash-settled
- d. Company warrants are cash-settled; structured warrants deliver shares

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Company warrants are settled with delivery of shares; structured warrants are cash-settled

**Rationale:** Company warrants are settled with a delivery of shares. Structured warrants are cash-settled, that is no delivery of shares.

</details>

## Chapter 11 — Technical Analysis and Quantitative Analysis

### 524. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.1.2] What does technical analysis study?

- a. The economy and industry structure
- b. Financial statements
- c. A company's business profitability
- d. Historical market data such as price changes and trading volume, to forecast price fluctuations

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Historical market data such as price changes and trading volume, to forecast price fluctuations

**Rationale:** Technical analysis refers to the methodology of forecasting fluctuations in securities prices through an examination of historical market data. It is sometimes called market or internal analysis because it studies demand and supply conditions based on market data such as price changes and trading volume.

</details>

### 525. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.1.2] What is the key difference between technical and fundamental analysts regarding irrational factors?

- a. Technical analysts expect irrational factors to persist; fundamental analysts expect only a temporary effect
- b. Fundamental analysts expect irrational factors to persist; technical analysts expect only a temporary effect
- c. Both expect them to persist
- d. Neither considers them

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Technical analysts expect irrational factors to persist; fundamental analysts expect only a temporary effect

**Rationale:** A key difference in opinion relates to the influence of irrational factors: technical analysts expect irrational factors to persist while fundamental analysts expect only a temporary effect, with rational beliefs prevailing in the long run.

</details>

### 526. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.1.2] Why do technical analysts believe prices exhibit trends that persist over long periods?

- a. Because markets are perfectly efficient
- b. Because new information is priced by the market over a period of time rather than at one point, so prices adjust gradually
- c. Because all investors have the same information
- d. Because prices are random

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Because new information is priced by the market over a period of time rather than at one point, so prices adjust gradually

**Rationale:** Technical analysts believe that new information affecting supply and demand is priced by the market over a period of time rather than at one point in time, given that there are different sources of information or because some investors have earlier access. As the speed of adjustment is gradual, prices are expected to exhibit trends that persist over long periods.

</details>

### 527. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.1.3] What presents the greatest challenge to technical analysis?

- a. Behavioural finance
- b. Fundamental analysis
- c. The Efficient Market Hypothesis
- d. The random walk theory alone

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The Efficient Market Hypothesis

**Rationale:** The Efficient Market Hypothesis presents the greatest challenge to technical analysis. Studies that tested weak-form market efficiency generally support the notion that prices adjust rapidly to new information, leaving little opportunity for technical analysts.

</details>

### 528. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.1.3] Why might a popular technical trading rule eventually stop working?

- a. Because regulators ban it
- b. Because charts are unavailable
- c. Because the rule is mathematically wrong
- d. Because many participants act on it at the same time, so some act first and the resulting competition negates the rule's value

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Because many participants act on it at the same time, so some act first and the resulting competition negates the rule's value

**Rationale:** If a popular technical analysis trading rule is followed by many market participants, some participants will act first, causing prices to change quicker than expected, so that only those who act earliest will benefit. The popularity and the resulting competition will eventually negate the value of the technical trading rule.

</details>

### 529. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.2.1] Why is a moving average more useful than the daily price for detecting trends?

- a. Because it includes past prices, making it more stable and smoothing out erratic changes
- b. Because it predicts the future exactly
- c. Because it ignores volume
- d. Because it is more volatile

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Because it includes past prices, making it more stable and smoothing out erratic changes

**Rationale:** The moving average is more stable than the daily price because of the inclusion of past prices, making it a more useful measure for detecting trends. If the current market price starts to diverge directionally from the moving average, this may indicate a trend reversal.

</details>

### 530. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.2.2] What does the advance-decline line measure?

- a. The average price of all shares
- b. On a cumulative daily basis, the net difference between the number of shares with price advances and those with price declines
- c. The trading volume of the index
- d. The volatility of the index

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. On a cumulative daily basis, the net difference between the number of shares with price advances and those with price declines

**Rationale:** The A/D line measures, on a cumulative daily basis, the net difference between the number of shares that have price advances and those that have price declines. The net advance or decline is calculated by subtracting the number of declines from the number of advances, and the line plots a running total over time.

</details>

### 531. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.2.2] What does it mean if the index is rising while the advance-decline line is falling?

- a. Volume is increasing
- b. The market is technically strong
- c. Most shares are not participating in the rising market, indicating a market peak
- d. The market is oversold

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Most shares are not participating in the rising market, indicating a market peak

**Rationale:** In a rising market, any divergence between the index and A/D line is of concern because it means that most shares are not participating in the rising market, indicating a market peak. Conversely, if the A/D line is rising and the index is declining, it signals a reversal of the falling trend.

</details>

### 532. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.2.3] What are the most commonly used MACD parameters?

- a. 20, 50, 200
- b. 14, 30, 7
- c. 5, 10, 20
- d. 12, 26, 9

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. 12, 26, 9

**Rationale:** The most commonly used values are 12, 26 and 9 days, that is MACD (12,26,9). The MACD series is the difference of EMAs with characteristic times a and b, and the average series is an EMA of the MACD series with characteristic time c.

</details>

### 533. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.2.3] What signal is given when the MACD crosses and falls below the signal line?

- a. A bearish signal indicating it may be time to sell
- b. A neutral signal
- c. No signal
- d. A bullish signal

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. A bearish signal indicating it may be time to sell

**Rationale:** When the MACD crosses and falls below the signal line, it is a bearish signal that indicates that it may be time to sell. Conversely, when the MACD crosses and rises above the signal line, the indicator gives a bullish signal.

</details>

### 534. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.2.5] What moving average and multiplier are typically used to construct Bollinger Bands?

- a. A 50-day SMA with a multiplier of 1
- b. A 20-day SMA with a multiplier of 2
- c. A 200-day SMA with a multiplier of 3
- d. A 10-day EMA with a multiplier of 2

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. A 20-day SMA with a multiplier of 2

**Rationale:** The first step is to compute the simple moving average, typically using a 20-day SMA. Then multiply the standard deviation by two, a commonly used multiplier, and both add and subtract that amount from each point along the SMA.

</details>

### 535. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.2.5] What does it mean when prices move close to the upper Bollinger Band?

- a. Volatility has fallen to zero
- b. The market is oversold
- c. The market is more overbought and may be primed for a trend reversal or price correction
- d. The market is fairly valued

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The market is more overbought and may be primed for a trend reversal or price correction

**Rationale:** Many traders believe the closer the prices move to the upper band, the more overbought the market, and the closer the prices move to the lower band, the more oversold the market. An overbought or oversold market may be primed for a trend reversal or price correction.

</details>

### 536. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.3] What defines an uptrend?

- a. Current highs below previous highs and current lows below previous lows
- b. Current highs above previous highs but current lows below previous lows
- c. A flat price line
- d. Current highs above previous highs and current lows above previous lows

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Current highs above previous highs and current lows above previous lows

**Rationale:** An uptrend is in place when current highs are above previous highs and current lows are also above previous lows. A downtrend occurs when current lows are lower than previous lows and current peaks are also lower than previous peaks.

</details>

### 537. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.3] What is a support level?

- a. A price range where a substantial increase in demand is expected
- b. The highest price ever reached
- c. The average price over a year
- d. A price range where buyers refuse to pay higher prices

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. A price range where a substantial increase in demand is expected

**Rationale:** A support level is a price range where a substantial increase in demand is expected. At this level, prices are relatively attractive for buyers who had missed the opportunity to buy earlier.

</details>

### 538. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.3] What is the market overhang?

- a. Excess demand at a support level
- b. The supply created by sellers attracted by higher prices to take profits at a resistance level
- c. The volume traded in a day
- d. The gap between bid and offer

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. The supply created by sellers attracted by higher prices to take profits at a resistance level

**Rationale:** As prices increase, a resistance level develops where buyers refuse to pay higher prices. At the same time, sellers may be attracted by higher prices to take profits on their holdings. This supply is called the market overhang.

</details>

### 539. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.3] What is a breakout below a support level?

- a. A continuation of the uptrend
- b. A bullish signal
- c. A bearish signal indicating a reversal of the uptrend
- d. A neutral signal

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. A bearish signal indicating a reversal of the uptrend

**Rationale:** A breakout above the resistance level is a bullish signal, whereas a breakout below the support level is a bearish signal. A penetration below a support line would be a sell signal as it indicates a reversal of the uptrend.

</details>

### 540. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.4] What does a price decline accompanied by heavy volume indicate?

- a. A neutral signal
- b. A buying opportunity
- c. A bullish signal
- d. A very bearish signal reflecting a strong and widespread desire to sell

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. A very bearish signal reflecting a strong and widespread desire to sell

**Rationale:** A price decline with heavy volume is very bearish because it reflects a strong and widespread desire to sell the security.

</details>

### 541. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.4] What might a rally developing on contracting volume signal?

- a. A possible trend reversal
- b. Increased buying interest
- c. Higher volatility
- d. A strong continuation of the rally

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. A possible trend reversal

**Rationale:** In a sustained bull market, if prices correct downward on declining volume, this may indicate a lull followed by a continuation of the rally. On the other hand, a rally that develops on contracting volume could signal a possible trend reversal.

</details>

### 542. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.5.1] What are the three movements in the Dow Theory?

- a. Opening, intraday and closing
- b. Primary, secondary and daily fluctuations
- c. Bull, bear and flat
- d. Short, medium and long term

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Primary, secondary and daily fluctuations

**Rationale:** The Dow Theory postulates three movements in the market: the primary movement, the secondary movement, and the daily fluctuations. The primary movement is the market trend, lasting from several months to several years.

</details>

### 543. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.5.1] What proportion of the previous advance does the secondary movement typically retrace?

- a. The full amount
- b. One-tenth to one-quarter
- c. One-third to two-thirds
- d. Three-quarters to nine-tenths

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. One-third to two-thirds

**Rationale:** The secondary movement of the market is shorter in duration than the primary movement and opposite in direction. It usually lasts from several weeks to months, and usually retraces one-third to two-thirds of the previous advance or decline.

</details>

### 544. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.5.1] When is a new primary trend confirmed under the Dow Theory?

- a. When volume increases
- b. When the RSI crosses 70
- c. When the DJIA alone signals it
- d. When both the DJIA and DJTA give the same signals

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. When both the DJIA and DJTA give the same signals

**Rationale:** A new primary trend will only be confirmed when both the DJIA and DJTA give the same signals. When the two averages diverge from each other, the Dow Theory assumes that the prior trend is maintained.

</details>

### 545. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.5.1] Which of the following is a weakness of the Dow Theory?

- a. It is slow in identifying a major trend and may be whiplashed
- b. It is too fast to signal
- c. It identifies which shares to buy
- d. It uses too many shares

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. It is slow in identifying a major trend and may be whiplashed

**Rationale:** The Dow Theory is slow in identifying a major trend and may miss an important price move or be whiplashed, that is be caught by a short-term price reversal even though the long-term trend is intact but not yet visible. It also does not help identify which shares to buy or sell.

</details>

### 546. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.5.2] How are trend changes identified using a moving average line?

- a. By a change in the direction of the moving average
- b. By a crossover between the moving average and the index itself
- c. By the slope of the moving average
- d. By the volume traded

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. By a crossover between the moving average and the index itself

**Rationale:** Trend changes are identified not by a change in the direction of the moving average, but by a crossover between the moving average and the index itself.

</details>

### 547. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.5.2] Which moving average is normally used to identify long-term trends?

- a. The 30-day
- b. The 50-day
- c. The 200-day
- d. The 20-day

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The 200-day

**Rationale:** Popular moving averages used in technical analysis are the 200-day and 50-day moving averages. The 200-day moving average is normally used to identify long-term trends while a 20- to 30-day moving average is used to identify short- and intermediate-term trends.

</details>

### 548. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.5.3] What does an RSI reading above 70 indicate?

- a. A neutral market
- b. A trend reversal has occurred
- c. An oversold condition
- d. An overbought condition

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. An overbought condition

**Rationale:** The index ranges from zero to 100, and an RSI above 70 is considered overbought. Conversely, an RSI below 30 would signal an oversold condition.

</details>

### 549. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.5.3] When is the RSI less useful?

- a. When trends are steady and consistent, such as constant demand for a growth stock keeping its RSI persistently high
- b. In a falling market
- c. When volume is high
- d. During surprises and shocks

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. When trends are steady and consistent, such as constant demand for a growth stock keeping its RSI persistently high

**Rationale:** The RSI is less useful when trends are steady and consistent. Where constant demand for a growth stock causes its RSI to be persistently high, the RSI level should not be viewed as an indication to sell; rather one should look for an opportunity to accumulate the stock when the RSI is not as high.

</details>

### 550. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.6] What is the difference between reversal and continuation patterns?

- a. Reversal patterns indicate a pause; continuation patterns indicate a reversal
- b. Reversal patterns indicate an important reversal in trend; continuation patterns suggest the market is only taking a pause before the existing trend resumes
- c. Both indicate reversals
- d. Both indicate continuations

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Reversal patterns indicate an important reversal in trend; continuation patterns suggest the market is only taking a pause before the existing trend resumes

**Rationale:** Reversal patterns indicate that an important reversal in trend is occurring. The continuation patterns suggest that the market is only taking a pause, possibly to correct a short term overbought or oversold condition, after which the existing trend is expected to resume.

</details>

### 551. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.6.1] By how much must the price close below the neckline for a head and shoulders breakout to be confirmed?

- a. 10% of the share price
- b. 1% of the share price
- c. 3% of the share price
- d. 5% of the share price

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 3% of the share price

**Rationale:** A breakout is confirmed if the price closes below the neckline by 3% of the share price. Once penetrated, the potential magnitude of decline is measured by the distance between the head and the neckline, projected downward from the neckline.

</details>

### 552. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.6.1] What does an inverse head and shoulders pattern indicate?

- a. A continuation of a downtrend
- b. No change in trend
- c. A market peak
- d. A market bottom

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. A market bottom

**Rationale:** While the regular upright head and shoulders formation indicates a market peak, the inverse head and shoulders formation indicates a market bottom.

</details>

### 553. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.6.2] What is the main feature of a double top?

- a. The second top forms with distinctly lower volume than the first
- b. Both tops form on equal volume
- c. The pattern has no volume characteristic
- d. The second top forms with distinctly higher volume

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. The second top forms with distinctly lower volume than the first

**Rationale:** A double top is a reversal pattern indicating a market top. It consists of two tops separated by a valley in prices. The main feature of a double top is that the second top is formed with distinctly lower volume than the first.

</details>

### 554. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.6.2] How does volume behave in a double bottom formation?

- a. The first bottom is dull and the second is heavy
- b. The first bottom is accompanied by heavy volume, the second is dull, and volume increases significantly on the breakout
- c. Volume is constant throughout
- d. Volume falls on the breakout

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. The first bottom is accompanied by heavy volume, the second is dull, and volume increases significantly on the breakout

**Rationale:** The formation of the first bottom is normally accompanied by heavy volume while the second bottom is usually dull with little trading volume. Volume increases significantly on the breakout.

</details>

### 555. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.6.3] What is assumed during the formation of a consolidation rectangle?

- a. That the price will break upwards
- b. That the trend will reverse
- c. That the prevailing trend will continue until proven otherwise
- d. That volume will fall to zero

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. That the prevailing trend will continue until proven otherwise

**Rationale:** During the formation of the rectangle, it is difficult to predict which way the price will ultimately break. Therefore, it is assumed that the prevailing trend will continue until proven otherwise.

</details>

### 556. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.6.4] Which triangle is a bullish indicator?

- a. Descending triangle
- b. All triangles
- c. Symmetrical triangle
- d. Ascending triangle

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Ascending triangle

**Rationale:** Ascending triangles are bullish indicators, formed by price consolidations bounded by an upward sloping support line and an almost horizontal resistance line. The ascending triangle indicates that buyers are more aggressive than sellers.

</details>

### 557. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.6.4] How many points of contact form a triangle?

- a. Four
- b. Five
- c. Two
- d. Three

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Four

**Rationale:** Triangles are formed with four points of contact, two support and two resistance points.

</details>

### 558. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.6.5] What is the difference between a flag and a pennant?

- a. A flag is formed by converging lines; a pennant by parallel lines
- b. A flag is formed by parallel lines; a pennant by converging lines
- c. A flag is a reversal pattern; a pennant is a continuation pattern
- d. There is no difference

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. A flag is formed by parallel lines; a pennant by converging lines

**Rationale:** A pennant is formed after a sharp rise or fall in price with lower volume during its formation. The difference between a pennant and a flag is that the pennant is formed by a set of converging, instead of parallel, lines.

</details>

### 559. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.6.5] How long can a flag take to form?

- a. One to two years
- b. One day
- c. As few as 5 days to as many as 3–5 weeks
- d. Three to six months

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. As few as 5 days to as many as 3–5 weeks

**Rationale:** Flags can take as few as 5 days to as many as 3–5 weeks to form. Essentially, they represent a period of controlled profit taking in a rising market.

</details>

### 560. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.6.6] What does a rising wedge usually indicate?

- a. A market bottom
- b. A neutral market
- c. A continuation of an uptrend
- d. A bear market rally, with prices usually falling very sharply after completion

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. A bear market rally, with prices usually falling very sharply after completion

**Rationale:** In a rising wedge, both boundary lines slant upwards. A rising wedge usually develops on a falling price trend. Following its completion, prices usually fall very sharply, especially if volume is high. Rising wedges are characteristic of bear market rallies.

</details>

### 561. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.6.6] What does a falling wedge represent?

- a. A temporary interruption of a rising trend
- b. A market peak
- c. A reversal to a downtrend
- d. A bear market rally

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. A temporary interruption of a rising trend

**Rationale:** A falling wedge is bounded by converging lines that slant downwards. It represents a temporary interruption of a rising trend. When prices move out of a falling wedge, they are likely to drift sideways before they begin to rise.

</details>

### 562. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.6.7] Where does a breakaway gap appear?

- a. In the middle of a move
- b. At the beginning of a move, when price breaks out of a price pattern
- c. At the end of a move
- d. Only in weekly charts

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. At the beginning of a move, when price breaks out of a price pattern

**Rationale:** A breakaway gap appears when a price breaks out of a price pattern. Generally, breakaway gaps develop at the beginning of a move, runaway gaps in the middle of a move, and exhaustion gaps at the end of a move.

</details>

### 563. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.6.7] What is an exhaustion gap?

- a. A gap caused by a dividend
- b. The first gap in a series
- c. The last in a series of runaway gaps, representing the end of a rapid rise or fall
- d. A gap that is never filled

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The last in a series of runaway gaps, representing the end of a rapid rise or fall

**Rationale:** Exhaustion gaps represent the end of a rapid rise or fall and are the last in a series of runaway gaps. It is not easy to ascertain whether a gap is another runaway gap or the exhaustion gap.

</details>

### 564. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.6.7] Should a trading strategy rely on a gap being filled?

- a. Yes, within one week
- b. Only for daily gaps
- c. Yes, gaps are always filled
- d. No, although the general principle is that gaps will eventually be covered, this is not always the case

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. No, although the general principle is that gaps will eventually be covered, this is not always the case

**Rationale:** Although the general principle is that gaps will eventually be covered, this is not always the case. Accordingly, trading strategies should not be implemented solely on the assumption that a gap will be filled in the immediate future.

</details>

### 565. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.7] What pattern does the Elliott Wave Theory describe?

- a. A 5-wave advance and a 3-wave retreat
- b. A single continuous wave
- c. Random price movements
- d. A 3-wave advance and a 5-wave retreat

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. A 5-wave advance and a 3-wave retreat

**Rationale:** In his study of equity prices in 1938, R.N. Elliott came up with the Elliott Wave Theory, which states that prices move in cycles of waves and sub-sets of waves and exhibit the Golden Ratio, 1.618. Each cycle comprises a 5-wave advance and a 3-wave retreat.

</details>

### 566. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.7] What is the Golden Ratio exhibited by Elliott Wave patterns?

- a. 1.000
- b. 1.618
- c. 2.000
- d. 3.142

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 1.618

**Rationale:** Elliott Wave charts exhibit patterns related to Fibonacci's series, where each number is obtained by adding the two preceding consecutive numbers. As the series progresses, the ratio of consecutive numbers approaches the Golden Ratio of 1.618.

</details>

### 567. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.8] What does quantitative analysis involve?

- a. Reading financial statements
- b. The abstraction of market reality into relations incorporated into a financial model
- c. Chart reading
- d. Interviewing management

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. The abstraction of market reality into relations incorporated into a financial model

**Rationale:** Quantitative analysis essentially involves the abstraction of market reality, which comprises the economy, financial markets and its environment, into relations that are incorporated into a financial model.

</details>

### 568. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.8] What do quants examine beyond the first order of price change?

- a. Only the bid-offer spread
- b. Only the delta
- c. Second and third order derivatives of price changes
- d. Only volume

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Second and third order derivatives of price changes

**Rationale:** While less sophisticated traders might just look at price changes, quants will decompose these changes so that specific risk profiles can be created to benefit from these changes. Instead of just looking at the first order of price change, that is delta, quants will look at price changes in their second and third order derivatives.

</details>

### 569. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §11.8] Which hedge fund is cited as a cautionary example of quantitative analysis failing?

- a. Bridgewater Associates
- b. Renaissance Technologies
- c. Two Sigma
- d. Long Term Capital Management

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Long Term Capital Management

**Rationale:** Long Term Capital Management was a hedge fund that relied extensively on quantitative analysis. Despite the elegance and seeming mathematical robustness of their quantitative strategies, it still incurred huge losses.

</details>

## Chapter 12 — Case Studies

### 570. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §12.1] An entity has S$5 million worth of net assets. Is it an accredited investor?

- a. No, because an entity needs net assets exceeding S$10 million
- b. Yes, because any entity with over S$1 million qualifies
- c. No, because only individuals can be accredited investors
- d. Yes, because net assets exceed S$2 million

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. No, because an entity needs net assets exceeding S$10 million

**Rationale:** A corporation or other entity qualifies as an accredited investor with net assets exceeding S$10 million, as determined by its most recent audited balance sheet. S$5 million is below that threshold.

</details>

### 571. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §12.1] Which return objective is likely to be unique to an individual investor rather than an institutional investor?

- a. Potential for higher yields
- b. Regular savings to fund children's education
- c. Current income to meet recurring liquidity needs
- d. Long-term capital gain to beat inflation

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Regular savings to fund children's education

**Rationale:** Current income, long-term capital gain and higher yields can be return objectives of both individual and institutional investors. Regular savings to fund children's education is likely to be unique to an individual investor.

</details>

### 572. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §12.1] Which statement about the difference between accredited and retail investors is INCORRECT?

- a. A retail investor is likely to be more risk averse than an accredited investor
- b. An accredited investor can withstand a higher level of monetary risk
- c. A retail investor has a longer investment time horizon than an accredited investor
- d. An accredited investor can access a wider range of investment products

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. A retail investor has a longer investment time horizon than an accredited investor

**Rationale:** Investment time horizon is dependent on the needs of the investor, not the type of investor. The other three statements are correct.

</details>

### 573. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §12.2] Returns are 12% with probability 0.3, 8% with probability 0.5 and −6% with probability 0.2. What is the expected return?

- a. 5.30%
- b. 6.40%
- c. 8.45%
- d. 9.28%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 6.40%

**Rationale:** E(R) = 0.3(0.12) + 0.5(0.08) + 0.2(−0.06) = 0.036 + 0.040 − 0.012 = 0.0640 = 6.40%.

</details>

### 574. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §12.2] Using the same returns with an expected return of 6.40%, what is the coefficient of variation?

- a. 0.47
- b. 0.68
- c. 1.01
- d. 1.33

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 1.01

**Rationale:** Variance = 0.3(0.12 − 0.064)² + 0.5(0.08 − 0.064)² + 0.2(−0.06 − 0.064)² = 0.004144. Standard deviation = √0.004144 = 6.4374%. CV = 6.4374% / 6.40% = 1.01.

</details>

### 575. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §12.2] An investor sets aside $200,000 for 30 years at an expected return of 6.4% with 2% inflation. What is the future value in today's dollars?

- a. $709,264.72
- b. $1,610,176.77
- c. $2,279,904.66
- d. $2,865,831.06

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. $709,264.72

**Rationale:** Real rate = (1.064/1.02) − 1 = 4.31%. Future value in real terms = 200,000 × (1.0431)^30 = $709,264.72.

</details>

### 576. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §12.3] Which of the following suggests a potential economic downturn?

- a. Average duration of unemployment is increasing
- b. The 10-year T-bond spread over the Fed Funds rate is increasing
- c. Disposable personal incomes start to rise
- d. New orders by suppliers start to decline

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. New orders by suppliers start to decline

**Rationale:** Suppliers normally cut orders when they expect demand to drop. Average duration of unemployment is a lagging indicator, an increasing bond spread bodes well for economic health, and rising disposable personal income is a coincident indicator moving in the wrong direction for a downturn.

</details>

### 577. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §12.3] A slowdown in the Chinese economy occurs with rising interest rates. Which asset classes are likely to do well?

- a. Defensive stocks and investment grade bonds
- b. Cyclical stocks and high-yield bonds
- c. Growth stocks and precious metals
- d. Cash and real estate

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Defensive stocks and investment grade bonds

**Rationale:** A slowdown coupled with rising interest rates is characteristic of a late bull to early bear phase. Defensive stocks and investment grade bonds are likely to do well. Real estate is typically leveraged and risky when rates rise, cyclical stocks perform worse in a downturn, and high-yield issuers may default.

</details>

### 578. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §12.3] Uninformed fund managers reduced positions out of fear because news spread that many were doing likewise, and the selling triggered computer-driven strategies. What is the root cause of the price fall?

- a. Algorithmic trading
- b. Herd instinct
- c. Loss aversion
- d. Cognitive bias

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Herd instinct

**Rationale:** The managers simply followed the crowd without exercising independent decision-making ability and doing the due diligence, which is herd instinct. Loss aversion and algorithmic trading may have exacerbated the fall but did not provide the original impetus.

</details>

### 579. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §12.4] An investor buys IDR 100 billion of bonds at SGD/IDR 10,076.68, earning 6% while paying 1.5% on an SGD loan. If the exchange rate stays flat, what is the annual gain?

- a. SGD 9,923,903.51
- b. SGD 148,858.55
- c. SGD 446,575.66
- d. SGD 595,434.21

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. SGD 446,575.66

**Rationale:** The SGD-equivalent loan is 100bn / 10,076.68 = SGD 9,923,903.51. The annual gain is (0.06 − 0.015) × 9,923,903.51 = SGD 446,575.66.

</details>

### 580. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §12.4] Using the same carry trade, if the IDR weakens 10% against the SGD over the year, what is the total gain?

- a. −SGD 942,771.98
- b. SGD 605,357.18
- c. SGD 942,771.98
- d. −SGD 605,357.18

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. −SGD 605,357.18

**Rationale:** The new rate is 10,076.68/0.9 = 11,196.31. Bond proceeds = 100bn/11,196.31 = SGD 8,931,514.04. Interest income = 6bn/11,196.31 = SGD 535,890.84. Interest expense = 0.015 × 9,923,903.51 = SGD 148,858.55. Total = (8,931,514.04 − 9,923,903.51) + (535,890.84 − 148,858.55) = −SGD 605,357.18.

</details>

### 581. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §12.4] If SGD/USD is 0.7 and USD/IDR is 13,086.60, how does the carry trade gain compare with the flat-rate scenario?

- a. Higher gain
- b. Insufficient information
- c. Lower gain
- d. Same gain

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Higher gain

**Rationale:** The SGD/IDR cross rate is 0.7 × 13,086.60 = 9,160.62, which is lower than 10,076.68, so the IDR has appreciated against the SGD. The IDR-denominated investment and income appreciate in value, so the gain is higher than SGD 446,575.66.

</details>

### 582. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §12.5] A stock trades at USD 40 with next-year EPS of USD 1.08. What is the prospective P/E, and why is it high?

- a. 37.04, because the market has overvalued the stock
- b. 37.04, because the market is factoring in growth potential
- c. 35.71, because the market has overvalued the stock
- d. 35.71, because the market is factoring in growth potential

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 37.04, because the market is factoring in growth potential

**Rationale:** Prospective P/E = 40 / 1.08 = 37.04x. Since P/E ratios can be high because of strong expected future growth, the high prospective P/E is likely because investors are factoring in the high double-digit long-term growth potential.

</details>

### 583. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §12.5] EPS is 1.12 in FY18A and 5.81 in FY23F. What is the 5-year compounded annualised EPS growth rate?

- a. 31.57%
- b. 38.99%
- c. 40.01%
- d. 52.30%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 38.99%

**Rationale:** 5-year EPS growth = [(5.81/1.12)^(1/5) − 1] × 100% = [(5.1875)^0.2 − 1] × 100% = 38.99%.

</details>

### 584. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §12.5] A stock has a prospective P/E of 37.04 and a 5-year EPS growth rate of 38.99%. What is the PEG ratio and the recommendation?

- a. 0.92, buy
- b. 0.92, sell
- c. 0.95, buy
- d. 0.95, sell

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 0.95, buy

**Rationale:** PEG = 37.04 / 38.99 = 0.95. As the PEG ratio is less than 1, the stock is slightly undervalued even after accounting for its strong expected future growth, so the investor can consider buying.

</details>

### 585. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §12.6] Free cash flows to a levered firm should be discounted using:

- a. The cost of ordinary equity only
- b. The cost of preferred equity only
- c. The cost of debt only
- d. The weighted average cost of capital

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. The weighted average cost of capital

**Rationale:** The appropriate discount rate should take into account capital from all sources for which a return is required. The cost of ordinary equity alone would be correct only if the firm were all-equity funded, which it is not because it is levered.

</details>

### 586. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §12.6] Which of the following is NOT part of estimating free cash flows to the firm?

- a. Analysing historical ratios to make interest expense projections
- b. Interviewing company management on revenue growth potential
- c. Consulting industry specialists on expected profitability margins
- d. Estimating future fixed asset investments

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Analysing historical ratios to make interest expense projections

**Rationale:** Revenue growth and profitability margins are needed to derive EBIT, and capital expenditure is another key input. Interest expense is excluded from the FCFF computation.

</details>

### 587. **[Multiple response]** [CM-EIP §12.6] Enterprise value can be calculated using which of the following? (Select all that apply)

- a. The sum of the present values of the FCFFs for the projection period plus the terminal value
- b. The sum of the present values of the FCFFs for the projection period plus the present value of the terminal value
- c. The sum of the FCFFs for the projection period plus the present value of the terminal value
- d. The sum of the estimated market values of debt, common stock and preferred stock

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B, D

**Rationale:** Enterprise value can be calculated either as the worth today of the cash flows the firm generates, or as the value today of the claims on those cash flows. Options (a) and (c) fail to discount the terminal value and the FCFFs respectively.

</details>

### 588. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §12.7] A 10-year 4% bond has 6 years left and a YTM of 6.0%. Why is it priced below face value?

- a. Because it is callable
- b. Because its YTM is higher than its coupon rate
- c. Because its YTM is lower than its coupon rate
- d. Because it is unrated

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Because its YTM is higher than its coupon rate

**Rationale:** A bond has to be priced at a discount in order for the YTM to be higher than the coupon rate. Here 6.0% > 4%, so the bond trades at a discount.

</details>

### 589. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §12.7] An investor insists on holding a bond to maturity. What should he be most concerned with?

- a. That interest rates are decreasing
- b. The coupon rate of the bond
- c. That the bond is callable
- d. That interest rates are increasing

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. That the bond is callable

**Rationale:** A callable bond can be redeemed before maturity, and if that happens the investor is exposed to reinvestment risk from having to reinvest the proceeds at lower yields. Holding to maturity means interest rate changes are of little concern because the price converges to par.

</details>

### 590. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §12.7] Interest rates are expected to rise. Which bond price is likely to decrease the most in percentage terms?

- a. The 10-year 6% callable bond with 5 years left
- b. The 8% bond with 4 years left
- c. All will decrease equally
- d. The 10-year 4% bond with 6 years left

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. The 10-year 4% bond with 6 years left

**Rationale:** The bond with the longest maturity and lowest coupon is most sensitive to interest rate changes. The 4% bond with 6 years left has both the longest remaining maturity and the lowest coupon, so its price is most sensitive.

</details>

### 591. **[Multiple response]** [CM-EIP §12.8] An investor does not believe superior returns can be achieved through fundamental analysis. Which forms of market efficiency must he necessarily believe in? (Select all that apply)

- a. Weak form
- b. Semi-weak form
- c. Semi-strong form
- d. Strong form

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A, C

**Rationale:** Conducting fundamental analysis to identify a mispriced stock assumes all publicly available information is not already reflected in the price, so he must believe the market is semi-strong form efficient. Because publicly available information includes historical price and volume information, he must also believe it is weak form efficient. Semi-weak form is not a standard classification.

</details>

### 592. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §12.8] A share has a beta of 1.2 and a past return of 9.5%. The risk-free rate is 3% and the market risk premium is 6%. What is Jensen's alpha?

- a. −2.9%
- b. −0.7%
- c. 0.7%
- d. 2.9%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. −0.7%

**Rationale:** CAPM required return = 0.03 + 1.2(0.06) = 10.2%. Jensen's alpha = 9.5% − 10.2% = −0.7%.

</details>

### 593. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §12.8] A retiree needs $100,000 from a $500,000 actively managed portfolio in three months, is willing to risk only $100,000 in full, and needs income for 20 years. Which allocation best fits?

- a. 20% money market, 10% T-bills, 50% long-term government bonds, 20% developed equities
- b. 20% money market, 10% T-bills, 10% long-term government bonds, 10% high yield, 50% developed equities
- c. 10% money market, 80% long-term government bonds, 10% developed equities
- d. 20% money market, 10% T-bills, 10% long-term government bonds, 40% high yield, 20% developed equities

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. 20% money market, 10% T-bills, 50% long-term government bonds, 20% developed equities

**Rationale:** He needs 20% of the portfolio for the gift shortly, so 20% in money market funds is appropriate. His risk budget is $100,000 of $500,000, so a 60% allocation to high-yield bonds and equities is too risky. He needs long-term government bond coupons to fund annual expenses, so a 50% allocation to long-term government bonds is appropriate.

</details>

### 594. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §12.9] A warrant has a share price of $2.50, an exercise price of $1.50 and a conversion ratio of 1. What is its intrinsic value?

- a. $0
- b. $1
- c. $1.50
- d. $2.50

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. $1

**Rationale:** Since the warrant is in-the-money, intrinsic value = (2.50 − 1.50)/1 = $1.

</details>

### 595. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP §12.9] A warrant trades at $1.30 with an exercise price of $1.50 and a conversion ratio of 1. If the share price rises to $4.00, what is the minimum percentage gain on the warrant?

- a. Impossible to determine
- b. 92.31%
- c. 207.69%
- d. 307.69%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. 92.31%

**Rationale:** At a share price of $4.00 the intrinsic value is (4.00 − 1.50)/1 = $2.50, which is the minimum value of the warrant. Minimum percentage gain = 2.50/1.30 − 1 = 92.31%. Only the future minimum price is needed, not the future warrant price.

</details>

### 596. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §12.9] An investor wants to sell USD 50,000 for SGD. Quote A is USD/SGD 1.2500–1.2550 and Quote B is USD/SGD 1.2475–1.2515. Which rate is best?

- a. 1.2475
- b. 1.2515
- c. 1.2500
- d. 1.2550

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 1.2500

**Rationale:** For each quote the first rate is the price at which the market maker will buy USD and the second is the price at which it will sell USD. Since the investor wants to sell USD, the first rate of each quote applies, and the highest of those is best: 1.2500.

</details>

### 597. **[Multiple response]** [CM-EIP §12.10] Which features are common to unit trusts, business trusts and REITs? (Select all that apply)

- a. They are established under a trust deed
- b. They are exchange-traded
- c. They allow for diversification
- d. They are professionally managed

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A, C, D

**Rationale:** All three are established under a trust deed, allow diversification by pooling and investing funds, and have professionally managed assets. Option (b) is incorrect because while all REITs are listed, unit trusts are typically not listed and some business trusts are unlisted.

</details>

### 598. **[Multiple response]** [CM-EIP §12.10] What are the main differences between business trusts and REITs? (Select all that apply)

- a. The trustee and manager are separate in a REIT but not in a business trust
- b. Business trusts do not face investment restrictions, unlike REITs
- c. Business trusts are unlisted whereas REITs are listed
- d. There is a 75% gearing limit on REITs but only a 45% limit on business trusts

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A, B

**Rationale:** A business trust is a single responsible entity organisation with a trustee-manager, unlike a REIT where trustee and manager are separate. Business trusts can actively engage in any business area whereas REITs must invest only in real estate for rental income. Most business trusts are listed, and there is no gearing limit for business trusts but a 45% limit for REITs.

</details>

### 599. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP §12.10] An investor wants a steady stream of income rather than trading gains. Which scheme best suits him?

- a. A closed-end fund investing for capital appreciation
- b. A unit trust described as an aggressive fund
- c. A business trust comprising golf course assets
- d. A REIT focused on heartland retail malls

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. A REIT focused on heartland retail malls

**Rationale:** An aggressive unit trust generates returns from capital gains and is unlikely to provide steady income. Golf course cash flows are highly sensitive to economic fluctuations and business trusts have no mandatory minimum distribution. A REIT typically distributes at least 90% of annual income, which is likely to be more stable for heartland retail malls.

</details>

## Appendix A — Review Questions

### 600. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix A] An investment manager discovers the discount rate used in an NPV calculation was underestimated. What happens when the error is corrected?

- a. NPV falls and IRR is unchanged
- b. Both NPV and IRR fall
- c. NPV rises and IRR falls
- d. Both NPV and IRR rise

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. NPV falls and IRR is unchanged

**Rationale:** NPV discounts the cash flows at the given rate, so raising the rate lowers NPV. IRR is the rate at which NPV equals zero and is a property of the cash flows alone, so it does not change.

</details>

### 601. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix A] A portfolio has an expected return of 6% and a standard deviation of 12%, with normally distributed returns. The probability of a loss exceeding 6% is 16%. How often should such a loss be expected?

- a. Once every 5–6 years
- b. Once every 6–7 years
- c. Once every 7–8 years
- d. Once every 8–9 years

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Once every 6–7 years

**Rationale:** A 16% probability is about 1 in 6.25, so the loss should be expected roughly once every 6–7 years.

</details>

### 602. **[Multiple response]** [CM-EIP Appendix A] Which statements about value at risk are TRUE? (Select all that apply)

- a. It is not applicable to bonds, shares and currencies
- b. It measures and quantifies the minimum possible loss over a specific time within a confidence level
- c. It can be calculated by Monte Carlo simulation or the historical method
- d. It can be used when determinants of future returns differ from those of past returns

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B, C

**Rationale:** VaR quantifies the loss at a stated confidence level and can be computed historically or by Monte Carlo simulation. It applies to bonds, shares and currencies, and its historical form assumes past determinants persist.

</details>

### 603. **[Multiple response]** [CM-EIP Appendix A] Which of the following are forms of operational risk? (Select all that apply)

- a. A flash crash caused by a massive erroneous sell order
- b. Poor system infrastructure leading to technical disruptions
- c. Human errors resulting in erroneous trades
- d. Inability to access funding due to a credit downgrade

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A, B, C

**Rationale:** Execution errors, system failures and human error are operational risks. Inability to access funding due to a credit downgrade is funding liquidity risk, not operational risk.

</details>

### 604. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix A] Which of the following is a leading indicator?

- a. The average prime rate charged by banks
- b. Average duration of unemployment
- c. The 10-year T-bond spread over the Fed Funds rate
- d. Nonfarm payroll employment

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The 10-year T-bond spread over the Fed Funds rate

**Rationale:** The 10-year spread is one of the ten Composite Leading Economic Index components. Average duration of unemployment and the average prime rate are lagging indicators; nonfarm payrolls are coincident.

</details>

### 605. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix A] Spot USD/SGD is 1.2520 and the 30-day forward points are −10. What is the 30-day forward rate?

- a. 1.2530
- b. 1.2520
- c. 1.2500
- d. 1.2510

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. 1.2510

**Rationale:** Negative forward points are subtracted: 1.2520 − 0.0010 = 1.2510, a forward discount.

</details>

### 606. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix A] A company has current assets of $500,000 and current liabilities of $250,000. What is its current ratio?

- a. 2.0
- b. 1.5
- c. 2.5
- d. 0.5

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. 2.0

**Rationale:** Current ratio = Current assets / Current liabilities = 500,000 / 250,000 = 2.0.

</details>

### 607. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix A] Which index weighting scheme automatically adjusts for bonus and rights issues?

- a. Geometric average
- b. Value-weighted
- c. Price-weighted
- d. Equal-weighted

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Value-weighted

**Rationale:** Capital changes typically do not affect market capitalisation, so a value-weighted index adjusts automatically. A price-weighted index requires a divisor adjustment.

</details>

### 608. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix A] Which of the following is NOT covered by the Singapore Deposit Insurance Scheme?

- a. A Singapore dollar savings account
- b. A Singapore dollar fixed deposit
- c. A foreign currency deposit
- d. Monies under the CPF Investment Scheme

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. A foreign currency deposit

**Rationale:** The scheme covers Singapore dollar-denominated deposits only. Foreign currency deposits, structured deposits and investment products are excluded.

</details>

### 609. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix A] Which factor reduces a company warrant's price?

- a. An increase in the remaining life of the warrant
- b. An increase in the underlying share price
- c. An increase in volatility
- d. An increase in dividend expectations

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. An increase in dividend expectations

**Rationale:** Warrant holders do not receive dividends, so higher dividend expectations reduce the call warrant's price. Rising share price, rising volatility and longer remaining life all increase the price.

</details>

## Appendix B — Formulae Sheet

### 610. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix B] Which formula gives the coefficient of variation?

- a. Standard deviation of returns / Expected rate of return
- b. Expected rate of return / Standard deviation of returns
- c. Variance / Expected rate of return
- d. Standard deviation × Expected rate of return

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Standard deviation of returns / Expected rate of return

**Rationale:** The coefficient of variation measures risk per unit of expected return: CV = Standard Deviation of Returns / Expected Rate of Return.

</details>

### 611. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix B] Which formula gives the taxable equivalent yield?

- a. Tax-exempt yield × (1 − marginal tax rate)
- b. Tax-exempt yield / (1 − marginal tax rate)
- c. Tax-exempt yield / marginal tax rate
- d. Tax-exempt yield + marginal tax rate

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Tax-exempt yield / (1 − marginal tax rate)

**Rationale:** Taxable Equivalent Yield = Tax Exempt Yield / (1 − Marginal Tax Rate). An investor in the 28% bracket with a 7% tax-exempt yield has a TEY of 0.07/0.72 = 9.7%.

</details>

### 612. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix B] Which formula gives the approximate yield-to-maturity?

- a. Annual interest payment / current market price
- b. Annual interest payment / par value
- c. (Annual interest payment ± amortised gain or loss) / ((Current market price + Par value)/2)
- d. (Annual interest payment ± amortised gain or loss) / Current market price

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. (Annual interest payment ± amortised gain or loss) / ((Current market price + Par value)/2)

**Rationale:** The approximation relates the net annual effective cash flow to the average amount invested in the bond during the holding period, so the denominator is the average of current market price and par value.

</details>

### 613. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix B] Which formula gives the Sortino ratio?

- a. αp / σp
- b. (Rp − Rf) / σp
- c. (Rp − Rf) / βp
- d. (Rp − Rf) / σnegative

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. (Rp − Rf) / σnegative

**Rationale:** The Sortino ratio is (Rp − Rf) / σnegative, where σnegative is the average downside volatility. The Sharpe ratio uses total standard deviation and the Treynor ratio uses beta.

</details>

### 614. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix B] Which formula gives the gearing ratio of a warrant?

- a. SP / (nWP)
- b. (SP − EP) / n
- c. nWP + EP
- d. nWP / SP

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. SP / (nWP)

**Rationale:** Gearing ratio = SP / (nWP), where SP is the share price, WP the warrant price and n the conversion ratio. It compares the price of warrants needed to convert to one share relative to the share price.

</details>

## Appendix C — Essential Readings

### 615. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix C] How many items are on the essential readings list?

- a. Five
- b. Seven
- c. Ten
- d. Twelve

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Seven

**Rationale:** The source list runs to seven items across five publishers, covering general investments texts, technical analysis and portfolio management.

</details>

### 616. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix C] Which listed text is the only one covering technical analysis?

- a. Essentials of Investments
- b. Analysis of Investments and Management of Portfolios
- c. Technical Analysis Plain and Simple
- d. Managing Investment Portfolios: A Dynamic Process

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Technical Analysis Plain and Simple

**Rationale:** Technical Analysis Plain and Simple by Michael N. Kahn is the only technical analysis text on the list and covers Chapter 11.

</details>

## Appendix D — Glossary

### 617. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix D] What is the definition of a basis point?

- a. 1% or 0.01
- b. 0.001% or 0.00001
- c. 0.1% or 0.001
- d. 0.01% or 0.0001

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. 0.01% or 0.0001

**Rationale:** A basis point is a measure equal to 0.01% or 0.0001. It is the standard unit for quoting credit spreads and interest rate changes.

</details>

### 618. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix D] What is the definition of a blue chip company?

- a. A company known for quality, reliability and the ability to weather downturns and operate profitably in adverse conditions
- b. A company listed on a secondary board
- c. A company with a share price below $5
- d. A company with high growth and no dividends

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. A company known for quality, reliability and the ability to weather downturns and operate profitably in adverse conditions

**Rationale:** Blue chip companies are known for quality, reliability and the ability to weather downturns and operate profitably in the face of adverse economic conditions, which helps to contribute to their long record of stable and reliable growth.

</details>

### 619. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Appendix D] What is the definition of a call market?

- a. A market where trading is continuous
- b. A market where trades can be arranged only when the market is called, usually once a day
- c. A market where prices are negotiated by telephone
- d. A market for call options only

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. A market where trades can be arranged only when the market is called, usually once a day

**Rationale:** A call market is a market in which trades can be arranged only when the market is called, which is usually once a day. Price, but not timing, prioritisation is practised.

</details>

## Exam method and final revision checklist

### 620. **[Calculation]** [CM-EIP Exam method] The CM-EIP paper has 90 questions in 150 minutes. How many seconds per question does that allow, and how many correct answers are needed to pass at 70%?

- a. 100 seconds; 68 correct
- b. 120 seconds; 70 correct
- c. 100 seconds; 63 correct
- d. 90 seconds; 63 correct

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. 100 seconds; 63 correct

**Rationale:** 150 minutes is 9,000 seconds, and 9,000 / 90 = 100 seconds per question. The pass mark is 70% of 90 = 63 correct answers, leaving room for 27 errors and no more.

</details>

### 621. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Exam method] Why should a candidate never leave a blank answer in the CM-EIP paper?

- a. Because the system rejects incomplete papers
- b. Because blanks are marked as wrong and also reduce the pass mark
- c. Because blanks are penalised twice
- d. Because there is no penalty for a wrong answer, so a guess can only help

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. Because there is no penalty for a wrong answer, so a guess can only help

**Rationale:** There is no penalty for a wrong answer, so never leave a blank. If time is short, fill the remaining answers with the best judgement and return only if time allows.

</details>

### 622. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Exam method] Which day-count convention applies to Singapore Government Securities in the CM-EIP examination?

- a. 365 days
- b. Actual/actual
- c. 30/360
- d. 360 days

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. 365 days

**Rationale:** Use 365 days for Singapore Government Securities and 360 days for everything else. Carrying the 360-day convention into an SGS question is one of the most common calculation errors.

</details>

### 623. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Exam method] A candidate is told the formulae sheet is provided in the examination. What skill should the final revision therefore target?

- a. Memorising every formula verbatim
- b. Retrieval — being able to locate any formula within seconds
- c. Deriving each formula from first principles
- d. Ignoring the formulae sheet entirely

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. Retrieval — being able to locate any formula within seconds

**Rationale:** Because the formulae sheet is provided, the skill is retrieval rather than memorisation. Rehearse Appendix B until any formula can be located within seconds.

</details>

### 624. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Exam method] A candidate carries over the 75% pass mark from FMRP or RES 12B. What is the error?

- a. The CM-EIP pass mark is 80%
- b. None — all three papers share a 75% pass mark
- c. The CM-EIP pass mark is 70%, so the target is 63 of 90, not 68
- d. The CM-EIP pass mark is 65%

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. The CM-EIP pass mark is 70%, so the target is 63 of 90, not 68

**Rationale:** The CM-EIP pass mark is 70%, not 75%. At 90 questions that is 63 correct answers, not 68. Carrying over the FMRP or RES 12B figure is a listed trap.

</details>

### 625. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Exam method] How are the case studies weighted in the CM-EIP paper?

- a. There are 6 case studies carrying 18 questions
- b. There are no case studies
- c. They form a separate section worth a fifth of the marks
- d. There are 3 case studies carrying 9 questions between them, within the 90-question paper

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. There are 3 case studies carrying 9 questions between them, within the 90-question paper

**Rationale:** The paper includes 3 case studies of 9 questions in total, not a separate case-study section worth a fifth of the marks. The 6 case studies carrying 18 questions is the CM-SIP structure.

</details>

### 626. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Exam method] A calculation question is consuming time and the candidate has two minutes invested. What is the recommended action?

- a. Mark it, move on, and return on a second pass
- b. Guess immediately and move on
- c. Skip the rest of the section
- d. Continue until it is solved, since the marks are equal

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** A. Mark it, move on, and return on a second pass

**Rationale:** Leave the hardest calculation for a second pass. Mark it, move on, and return. Two minutes lost on one question costs two easier questions, and every question carries the same mark.

</details>

### 627. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Exam method] What should a candidate note first when reading a case study?

- a. The names of the parties
- b. The client's objective, horizon, risk tolerance and any constraint
- c. The currency of the transaction
- d. The date of the scenario

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** B. The client's objective, horizon, risk tolerance and any constraint

**Rationale:** Read the scenario once and note the client's objective, horizon, risk tolerance and any constraint — the questions usually turn on one of those.

</details>

### 628. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Exam method] How should a multiple-response question be approached?

- a. Select all options if any one is clearly correct
- b. Look for a pattern in the letter combinations
- c. Evaluate each option independently on its own merits
- d. Select only the option that appears most technical

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** C. Evaluate each option independently on its own merits

**Rationale:** Evaluate each option independently. Do not look for a pattern in the letters; a statement is true or false on its own merits.

</details>

### 629. **[Single choice]** [CM-EIP Exam method] Which pair of distinctions is described as deciding the most marks?

- a. Primary versus secondary market, and dealer versus broker
- b. Duration versus convexity, and CML versus SML
- c. REIT versus business trust, and company versus structured warrant
- d. All of the ten listed distinctions are high value

<details>
<summary>Show answer</summary>

**Answer:** D. All of the ten listed distinctions are high value

**Rationale:** The guide lists ten distinctions that decide the most marks, including primary versus secondary market, dealer versus broker, duration versus convexity, CML versus SML, DWR versus TWR, REIT versus business trust and company versus structured warrant. All are worth learning as pairs.

</details>
